# Quantum Steenrod powers and Hamiltonian maps

**arXiv ID**: 2607.25960v1
**Authors**: Shaoyun Bai, Egor Shelukhin, Nicholas Wilkins, Guangbo Xu
**Published**: 2026-07-28
**Categories**: math.SG, math.DS
**Comments**: 91 pages, 4 figures. Comments are welcome!
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2607.25960v1

## Abstract

We prove a series of new results in Hamiltonian dynamics on a general closed symplectic manifold $(M, ω)$, including:   1. If $M$ admits a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism which is either a pseudo-rotation or has finite order, then $M$ is geometrically uniruled. This resolves a variant of Problem 24 in McDuff--Salamon's list, which predicts an obstruction to the existence of such special Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms in terms of genus zero numerical invariants.   2. If a Hamiltonian possesses a periodic orbit in a non-torsion homology class, then it has infinitely many simple periodic points. The same conclusion holds if the manifold is not geometrically uniruled and the diffeomorphism is minimal for rational Floer homology. These two general results complement the known cases of the Hofer--Zehnder conjecture.   3. If a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism possesses a symplectically degenerate maximum, then it has infinitely many simple periodic points. This resolves an open question which stems from the work of Ginzburg and Gürel.   We also establish new cases of the generic Conley conjecture: infinitely many periodic points for generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.   The proofs rely on a systematic application of the integral Hamiltonian Floer theory package developed by the first and fourth author, a Künneth isomorphism in equivariant Floer homology, and new quantitative analysis of quantum power maps, which is of independent interest.

## Quantum Steenrod powers and Hamiltonian mapsShaoyun BaiMIT, 77 Massachusetts Avenue Cambridge, MA 02139, USAshaoyunb@mit.edu,Egor ShelukhinDepartment of Mathematics, University of Montreal, Canadaegorshel@gmail.com,Nicholas Wilkinsnick.mick.wilk@gmail.comandGuangbo XuDepartment of Mathematics, Rutgers University, Hill Center–Busch Campus, 110 Frelinghuysen Road, Piscataway, NJ 08854-8019, USAguangbo.xu@rutgers.edu

## Abstract.

We prove a series of new results in Hamiltonian dynamics on a general closed symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega), including:

1. IfMMadmits a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism which is either a pseudo-rotation or has
finite order, thenMMis geometrically uniruled. This resolves a variant of Problem 24 in
McDuff–Salamon’s list which predicts an obstruction to the existence of such special Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms in terms of genus zero numerical invariants.

2. If a Hamiltonian possesses a periodic orbit in a non-torsion homology class, then it has infinitely many simple periodic points. The same conclusion holds if the manifold is not geometrically uniruled and the diffeomorphism is minimal for rational Floer homology. These two general results complement the known cases of the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture.

3. If a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism possesses a symplectically degenerate maximum, then it has infinitely many simple periodic points. This resolves an open question which stems from the work of Ginzburg and Gürel.

We also establish new cases of the generic Conley conjecture: infinitely many periodic points for generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.

The proofs rely on a systematic application of the integral Hamiltonian Floer theory package developed by the first and fourth author, a Künneth isomorphism in equivariant Floer homology, and new quantitative analysis of quantum power maps, which is of independent interest.

## 1.Introduction

## 1.1.Overview

In recent years there has been active research into equivariant Floer theory for symplectomorphisms, which takes into account the action of a cyclic group on the loop space of a symplectic manifold via discrete rotation. This theory has its origins in the work of Fukaya[18], who first suggested a quantum version of Steenrod square operations. This theory has applications to Gromov–Witten invariants of symplectic manifolds[53,54,43,41,30,31,11,12,44,6,5]as well as to the topological, dynamical, and algebraic properties of groups of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms and, more generally, of symplectomorphisms[42,45,48,13,46,41,47,4,51,1,2,9,3,49,14]. In this paper we continue the Floer-theoretic study of the rotationalℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-symmetry on loops and deduce applications to Hamiltonian dynamics onallclosed symplectic manifolds using the foundational package[10]developed by the first and fourth author.

Extending the results of[53,13,46,47,4]to arbitrary primesppand to arbitrary closed symplectic manifolds, in this paper we provide new obstructions to the existence of Hamiltonian pseudo-rotations and Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms of finite order. Along the way, we establish a new Kunneth formula for equivariant power operations in Floer theory and quantum cohomology, and prove a new divisibility bound in local equivariant Floer cohomology. We also prove the existence of infinitely many periodic points for generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms for certain symplectic manifolds beyond already known cases
(see[20,50]). Furthermore, we prove a new result on the existence of infinitely many periodic orbits of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism. It is of complementary nature to that in[48]and provides further evidence for the Chance–McDuff conjecture according to which the existence of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism with finitely many periodic points on a closed symplectic manifold must imply the non-vanishing of a Gromov–Witten invariant, or more generally, the existence of a non-constant pseudo-holomorphic sphere. Finally, extending the results of[51,19]to full generality, we prove the existence of infinitely many periodic points of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism which either possesses a periodic orbit in a non-torsion homology class with non-vanishing local Floer cohomology or a symplectically degenerate maximum, foranyclosed symplectic manifold. This completes two directions of research initiated in the work of Ginzburg and Gürel.

## 1.2.Main results

Let us now discuss our main results in more detail and provide contexts in the literature. Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold. For any primepp, let𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}be the finite field ofppelements.

## 1.2.1.Hamiltonian pseudo-rotations and Hamiltonian torsions

The first main result deals with Hamiltonian pseudo-rotations: those Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms that have the homologically minimal number of periodic points of all periods from the perspective of the Arnol’d conjecture (i.e. the total Betti number). Letϕ\phibe a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism with a finite setFixc​(ϕ)\mathrm{Fix}_{c}(\phi)of contractible fixed pointsxx–those for which the loop{ϕHt​x}\{\phi^{t}_{H}x\}is contractible for every HamiltonianHHwith time-one mapϕH1=ϕ\phi^{1}_{H}=\phi. Given a field𝕂{\mathbb{K}}, following[48], we define the homological count of contractible fixed points ofϕ\phiover𝕂{\mathbb{K}}as follows:N​(ϕ;𝕂)=∑x∈Fixc​(ϕ)dim𝕂H​Floc​(ϕ,x;𝕂),N(\phi;{\mathbb{K}})=\sum_{x\in\mathrm{Fix}_{c}(\phi)}\dim_{\mathbb{K}}HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi,x;{\mathbb{K}}),

whereH​Floc​(ϕ,x;𝕂)HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi,x;{\mathbb{K}})denotes the local Floer cohomology with𝕂{\mathbb{K}}-coefficients.

## Definition 1.1.

A Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega)of a closed symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega)is called an𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}Hamiltonian pseudo-rotationif it has finitely many periodic points of periods being powers ofppand for allk≥1k\geq 1there holdsN​(ϕpk;𝔽p)=dim𝔽pH∗​(M;𝔽p).N(\phi^{p^{k}};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})=\dim_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p}).

We callϕ\phiapseudo-rotationif it has finitely many periodic points, and for every periodm≥1m\geq 1, over every field𝕂{\mathbb{K}}we haveN​(ϕm;𝕂)=dim𝕂H∗​(M;𝕂)N(\phi^{m};{\mathbb{K}})=\dim_{{\mathbb{K}}}H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{K}})

for all such iterations.

It is believed (such as in the well-known Chance–McDuff conjecture) that the existence of Hamiltonian pseudo-rotations implies the abundance of holomorphic spheres. Such a connection was established in terms of the quantum Steenrod square by[46,13,47]and
was recently applied to a new example of the monotone66-point blowup ofℂ​ℙ2\mathbb{CP}^{2}in[41]. Indeed, it was proven in[46,47,13]that if(M,ω)(M,\omega)is a spherically monotone symplectic manifold and admits an𝔽2{\mathbb{F}}_{2}Hamiltonian pseudo-rotation, then it is uniruled in the sense of quantum Steenrod operations. Namely, the quantum Steenrod square𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(μ){\it QSt}_{2}(\mu)of the counitμ∈H2​n​(M;𝔽2)\mu\in H^{2n}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})is deformed by holomorphic spheres in the sense that𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(μ)≠S​t2​(μ),{\it QSt}_{2}(\mu)\neq St_{2}(\mu),

whereS​t2St_{2}denotes the total Steenrod powerS​tpSt_{p}forp=2p=2. It is easy to see that this implies that(M,ω)(M,\omega)is geometrically uniruled: for everyω\omega-compatible almost complex structureJ,J,and every pointx∈M,x\in M,there exists a non-constantJJ-holomorphic sphere passing throughx.x.

Our result below, which was proved in Section4.3, generalizes this criterion to all primesppin terms of quantum Steenrodpp-th powers for arbitrary closed symplectic manifolds. In the following discussion, we denote byQ​S​tpQSt_{p}thepp-th quantum Steenrod power as defined in[53,43,41,10].

## Theorem 1.2(Pseudo-rotation⇒\Rightarrowuniruledness).

Letppbe a prime. Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold which admits an𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}Hamiltonian pseudo-rotation. Then it is𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}Steenrod uniruled. Namely, the quantum Steenrodpp-th power𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)of the counitμ∈H2​n​(M;𝔽p)\mu\in H^{2n}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})is deformed by holomorphic spheres in the sense that𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)≠S​tp​(μ).{\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)\neq St_{p}(\mu).

Extending the main result in[4], similar ideas in the proof of Theorem1.2allow us to prove the following result regarding Hamiltonian torsion, which provides an answer to[35, Chapter 14, Problem 24]in full generality (see proof in Section4.4).

## Theorem 1.3(Hamiltonian torsion⇒\Rightarrowuniruledness).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a compact symplectic manifold, which admits a nontrivial Hamiltonian action by a finite groupGG. Then(M,ω)(M,\omega)is𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}Steenrod uniruled for any primeppwhich does not divide|G||G|.

## Remark 1.4.

In other words, we prove that the Gromov–Witten type invariant coming from the “quantum part” of𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)is nontrivial. This is arguably not the only form of answer to[35, Chapter 14, Problem 24], but we believe that the condition𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)≠S​tp​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)\neq St_{p}(\mu)should imply the non-triviality of certain ordinary Gromov–Witten invariants, possibly with gravitational descendants. However, establishing such a statement is more in the realm of enumerative geometry, and Theorem1.3should reflect the essence of the interaction between Hamiltonian torsion and abundance of rational curves. See[39]for related work in this direction.

We can also deduce the following result concerning finite group actions via Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, extending[4, Theorem H]to full generality.

## Theorem 1.5(Contractibility of fixed points of finite-order Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a compact symplectic manifold. Ifϕ\phiis a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism of(M,ω)(M,\omega)of finite order, then all the fixed points ofϕ\phiare contractible.

## 1.2.2.Results towards the generic Conley conjecture

Hamiltonian pseudo-rotation and Hamiltonian torsion both represent the rare case when a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism has only finitely many periodic points. In fact, the so-called “generic Conley conjecture” asserts that on any symplectic manifold, generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms should have infinitely many periodic points. The method used to prove Theorem1.2and Theorem1.3can be modified to prove the following theorem and two corollaries in the scope of this conjecture. We remind the reader that by the Birkhoff–Moser theorem[36], the potential failure of the generic Conley conjecture could come from Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms with only hyperbolic fixed points. Hence the main argument (see Subsection4.6) is to use quantum Steenrod operations to rule out such transformations.

## Theorem 1.6.

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold andϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega). Supposeϕ\phihas finitely many periodic points, all of which are hyperbolic. Then for any primepp, and a classu∈Hr​(M;𝔽p)u\in H^{r}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})withr>nr>nsatisfyingS​tp​(u)=cp​tr​(p−1)/2​u,St_{p}(u)=c_{p}t^{r(p-1)/2}u,(1.1)

for somecp∈𝔽p∖{0}c_{p}\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}\setminus\{0\}, we have𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(u)≠S​tp​(u).{\it QSt}_{p}(u)\neq St_{p}(u).

The conceptual reason for having Theorem1.6is that the hyperbolicity of periodic points puts a strong restriction (via grading) on the local version of the equivariant pair-of-pants product, which further constrains𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}.

## Corollary 1.7(Generic Conley conjecture from𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold such that there exists a primeppand a classu∈Hr​(M;𝔽p)u\in H^{r}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})withr>nr>nsuch that𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(u)=S​tp​(u)=cp​tr​(p−1)/2​u{\it QSt}_{p}(u)=St_{p}(u)=c_{p}t^{r(p-1)/2}u

for0≠cp∈𝔽p0\neq c_{p}\in{\mathbb{F}}_{p}. Then anyC2C^{2}-generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega)has infinitely many periodic points.

## Remark 1.8.

Note that (1.1) holds whenever2​(p−1)+r>2​n2(p-1)+r>2nandβp​(u)=0\beta_{p}(u)=0whereβp:Hr​(M;𝔽p)→Hr+1​(M;𝔽p)\beta_{p}:H^{r}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\to H^{r+1}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})is the Bockstein operator. However, it also holds in certain other cases, such as the setting of the proof of Corollary1.9below.

The rationale of Corollary1.7is to provide new examples of manifolds for which generic Conley conjecture holds, which are also not covered by existing results. Indeed, by[20,50], generic Conley conjecture holds for any symplectic manifoldMMfor which one of the following three conditions fails to hold.

MMadmits no hyperbolic pseudo-rotations, and anyC2C^{2}-generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega)has infinitely many periodic points.

## Remark 1.12.

Note that we prove Theorem1.11only in the monotone setting, even though our discussions about quantum Steenrod operations are concerned with general symplectic manifolds elsewhere in the paper. We expect that proving Theorem1.11in general is a subtle problem. A related result, the Kunneth theorem for quantum cohomology in the symplectic setting[27], demonstrates part of the difficulties: the moduli spaces of stable maps of a product can be different from the product of the respective moduli spaces, which makes the comparison between the virtual fundamental cycles a nontrivial problem. The interaction between such differences and the perturbation scheme in[7]needs to be sorted out in order to find the generalization in our setting. Nevertheless, Theorem1.11suffices for the applications in this paper.

## 1.2.3.Generalizations of the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture

Next, we provide a new criterion for the existence of infinitely many periodic points of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphism, which is quite different from other criteria in the literature (see Section4.7for the proof).

## Theorem 1.13(“Reversed” Hofer–Zehnder conjecture).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold such that𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)=S​tp​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)=St_{p}(\mu)for infinitely many primespp. Supposeϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega)has finitely many contractible fixed points andN​(ϕ;ℚ)=dimℚH∗​(M;ℚ)N(\phi;{\mathbb{Q}})=\dim_{{\mathbb{Q}}}H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}}). Thenϕ\phihas infinitely many periodic points.

## Remark 1.14.

The proof shows that under an additional non-degeneracy assumption on the fixed points ofϕ\phi(for instance if none of them is a symplectically degenerate maximum), there exist infinitely many primesppsuch thatϕ\phipossesses a simplepp-periodic point.

Using a standard Gromov compactness argument, Theorem1.13shows that a Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ\phiof a closed symplectic manifold which is not geometrically uniruled, having the minimal possible number of fixed points (corresponding to contractible orbits as constrained by the Arnol’d conjecture) must in fact have infinitely many periodic points. This result is interesting in comparison with prior results in this direction. For instance, the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture, proved in[48]for closed spherically monotone manifolds with semi-simple quantum cohomology (see also[3]for the generalization to weakly monotone symplectic manifolds, as well as[2,9]for weighted projective spaces and all toric varieties respectively), asserts the existence of infinitely many periodic points under the contrary assumption thatϕ\phihas strictly more fixed points than necessary.

## Remark 1.15.

Using the integral Gromov–Witten-type invariants defined in[7], one can define an analog of quantum cohomology ring, whose Floer-theoretic counterpart is discussed in detail in[10]. It is straightforward to adapt the methods from[48,3]to prove that under the assumption that this quantum cohomology ring is semi-simple, the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture as discussed above holds.

Our final applications are concerned with two sufficient conditions for the existence of infinitely many periodic points.

The first sufficient condition has its roots in the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture, in the way that noncontractible11-periodic orbits of a Hamiltonian should be considered as more than necessary in view of the Arnol’d conjecture. For a HamiltonianHHonMM, ifx:S1→Mx:S^{1}\to Mis a noncontractible 1-periodic orbit ofHH, then one can also define the local Floer cohomologyH​Floc​(H,x)HF^{\rm loc}(H,x)(overℤ{\mathbb{Z}}or any field). The details for the definition can be found in[51, Section 2.3].

## Theorem 1.16(Non-torsion orbit inH1H_{1}⇒\RightarrowConley conjecture).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a compact symplectic manifold andH:S1×M→ℝH:S^{1}\times M\to\mathbb{R}be a smooth Hamiltonian. If there exists a11-periodic orbit with underlying homology class0≠γ∈H1​(M;ℤ)∖Torsion0\neq\gamma\in H_{1}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})\setminus{\rm Torsion}which has non-vanishing local Floer cohomology, then for any sufficiently large prime numberpp, the time-one mapϕ=ϕH1\phi=\phi_{H}^{1}must admit a simple periodic point in the classp​γp\gammaof periodpporp′p^{\prime}, wherep′p^{\prime}is the next prime number afterpp. In particular,ϕ\phihas infinitely many periodic points.

Theorem1.16extends the main result in[51]to full generality, establishing a form of the Hofer–Zehnder conjecture for general symplectic manifolds. For deducing infinitely many periodic orbits from noncontractible Hamiltonian orbits, see also[24,22].

The second sufficient condition closes up the search of infinitely many Hamiltonian periodic orbits from the point of view of symplectically degenerate maxima, which was a phenomenon introduced to symplectic dynamics by Hingston[26]and explored by Ginzburg in his proof of the Conley conjecture for symplectically aspherical manifolds[23]as well as in numerous further works. Recall that an isolated capped 1-periodic orbitx¯\overline{x}of a HamiltonianHHis called a symplectically degenerate maximum if the mean indexΔ​(x¯,H)=0\Delta(\overline{x},H)=0and the local Floer cohomology satisfiesH​Floc,2​n​(H,x¯;ℚ)≠0HF^{{\rm loc},2n}(H,\overline{x};{\mathbb{Q}})\neq 0(here we use the cohomological gradingn+CZn+\operatorname{\mathrm{CZ}}to grade the generators so that the index of a nondegenerate maximum of aC2C^{2}-small Morse function is2​n2n).

Finally, the following theorem extends[19, Theorem 1.18],[25, Theorem 1.2]to full generality, solving a question which stems from[19, Remark 1.19]. It is proved in Section4.9. The possibility of working with different primes allows us to prove the stronger result on infinitely many periodic points: the number of periodic points of period≤k\leq kgrows at least ask2/log⁡(k)k^{2}/\log(k), which is the bound from the prime number theorem.

## Theorem 1.17(SDM⇒\RightarrowConley conjecture).

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be any compact symplectic manifold. If a HamiltonianHHhas a capped 1-periodic orbit which is a symplectically degenerate maximum, then its time-1 mapϕ\phihas infinitely many periodic points.

## 1.3.Methods

On the foundational side, fitting into the theme of recent extensive investigations, our results use operations in equivariant Floer theory. Historically, the earliest example of a power operation in Floer theory goes back to Fukaya,[18], where he describes the construction of a version of the Steenrod square on quantum cohomology. This construction was generalized and explored in the third author’s work[53]in relation with the Cartan and Adem relations. In the context of fixed-point Floer cohomology of exact symplectomorphisms of Liouville manifolds, a power operation was introduced by Seidel in[42]. The third author has shown an isomorphism between the last two operations in[54]. The construction of[42]was extended to odd primes in[45,48], and that of[53]was extended to odd primes in[43,41]. The constructions mentioned so far in this paragraph are in the monotone or weakly monotone setting.

The crucial technical input of this paper is the foundational package of integral Hamiltonian Floer theory from[10]built by the first and fourth author. In[10], the first and fourth author constructed quantum Steenrod operations and power operations on Hamiltonian Floer cohomology for arbitrary closed symplectic manifolds. It is also shown in[10]that these two operations are compatible (via a PSS type map) with each other. The construction is based on modppreduction of the integral virtual counts of pseudo-holomorphic curves initiated in[7], which is genuinely different from theℚ{\mathbb{Q}}-valued counts under the classical framework[17,32,28,37]in general. Such definition forms the base point of all the applications in this paper. Furthermore, quantitatively,
the virtual framework in[10]has a certain advantage over the traditional approach. Indeed, the traditional transversality argument requires inhomogeneous perturbations of the Floer equation, which alter the quantitative feature of solutions (by an arbitrarily small but nonzero amount). In the equivariant setting where one typically has an infinite-dimensional parameter space, the accumulations of these small defects become cumbersome to control. Such an issue does not arise when defining chain-level operations using the techniques in[10]because transversality of moduli spaces is achieved by abstract perturbations, which do not interfere with the energy estimates of Floer-type equations. This is why our results have maximum level of generality, especially for those applications that remove technical assumptions like monotonicity, and why the arguments presented in this paper are largely algebraic based on the black box built in[10].

Nevertheless, many of our arguments go well beyond directly plugging known arguments into the package[10]. There are new conceptual inputs except for the technical advancements. Most notably, to deal with degenerate isolated periodic points of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, we establish a divisibility result for the local equivariant pants product which is essential for many applications. Moreover, our arguments showcase how to fruitfully combine equivariant cohomology, filtered Floer theory, and the compatibility between quantum and Floer-theoretic operations to deduce strong constraints on Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms. For instance, our proof of Theorem1.17consists in a novel application of the equivariant pants product combined with considerations of filtered Floer theory. We expect the methods developed in this paper to be useful in other contexts.

## 1.4.Outline of the paper

In Section2, we recall the necessary preliminary knowledge about Floer theory and quantum Steenrod operations that will be used in the paper, with an emphasis on highlighting some formal aspects of the theory built in[10]and their connection with the more classical approach. In Section3, we apply the homological perturbation method to extend many useful constructions from the nondegenerate case to the isolated but possibly degenerate case. In Section4, we prove our main results modulo some technical proofs deferred to later sections. In Section5, we establish Kunneth type results for both the quantum Steenrod operations and local equivariant pair-of-pants product, which are used for proving Corollary1.9and the divisibility result of local equivariant pants product respectively. This paper has three appendices. In AppendixA, we discuss the agreement of quantum Steenrod operations constructed using different frameworks. In AppendixB, we provide the proofs of various homological perturbation results that are used in Section3. In AppendixC, we provide technical details of homological perturbation in the Morse–Bott setting that are used for discussing finite-order Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.

## 2.Preliminaries

We start by recalling some basic terminology and setting up our conventions. Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold with first Chern classc1c_{1}. First, setΓ=π2​(M)/ker⁡([ω])∩ker⁡(c1).\Gamma=\pi_{2}(M)/\ker([\omega])\cap\ker(c_{1}).

Let𝕂{\mathbb{K}}be a commutative unital ring. SetΛ𝕂Γ={∑i=1∞ai​qAi|ai∈𝕂,Ai∈Γ,limi→∞ω​(Ai)=+∞}.\Lambda_{{\mathbb{K}}}^{\Gamma}=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}q^{A_{i}}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}},\ A_{i}\in\Gamma,\ \lim_{i\to\infty}\omega(A_{i})=+\infty\Big\}.(2.1)

It will also be useful to consider the universal Novikov ringΛ𝕂univ={∑i=1∞ai​qλi|ai∈𝕂,λi∈ℝ,limi→∞λi=+∞}\Lambda^{{\mathrm{univ}}}_{{\mathbb{K}}}=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}q^{\lambda_{i}}\ |\ a_{i}\in{\mathbb{K}},\ \lambda_{i}\in{\mathbb{R}},\ \lim_{i\to\infty}\lambda_{i}=+\infty\Big\}

These rings are fields when𝕂{\mathbb{K}}is a field. Moreover,Λ𝕂Γ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}resp.Λ𝕂univ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}contains the subringΛ0,𝕂Γ\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\Gamma}resp.Λ0,𝕂univ\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}of sums as above withω​(Ai)≥0\omega(A_{i})\geq 0resp.λi≥0\lambda_{i}\geq 0for allii. There exists a ring homomorphismΛ𝕂Γ→Λ𝕂univ\displaystyle\ \Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}\to\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}\∑Ai∈Γai​qAi↦∑ai​qω​(Ai),\displaystyle\ \sum_{A_{i}\in\Gamma}a_{i}q^{A_{i}}\mapsto\sum a_{i}q^{\omega(A_{i})},(2.2)

by which we viewΛ𝕂univ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}as aΛ𝕂Γ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}-module.

As we primarily use a ground field𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}forppbeing a prime number, when we drop the ground ring from notations, we always mean the Novikov ring/field over𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}. NamelyΛΓ:=Λ𝔽pΓ,\displaystyle\ \Lambda^{\Gamma}:=\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}^{\Gamma},\Λuniv:=Λ𝔽puniv.\displaystyle\ \Lambda^{\rm univ}:=\Lambda^{\rm univ}_{{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}.

As different coefficient rings/fields will be used, one needs to be sensitive about taking tensor products. As a convention, when we writeA⊗BA\otimes Bwithout referring to the coefficient ring/field, one always means the tensor product as abelian groups. Notice that ifAAandBBare vector spaces over𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},A⊗BA\otimes Bcoincides with the vector space tensor productA⊗𝔽pBA\otimes_{{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}B.

## 2.1.Floer theory

For our applications, we use cohomological operations in Floer theory. Therefore, we use Floer cohomology throughout our discussion.

## 2.1.1.Conventions

together with−𝒜H-{\mathcal{A}}_{H}is aℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-graded non-archimedean normed space overΛ𝕂Γ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}, where forA∈ΓA\in\Gamma,qA∈Λ𝕂Γq^{A}\in\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}has grading2​c1​(A)2c_{1}(A)and valuationω​(A)\omega(A).

whereH(k)​(t,x):=k​H​(k​t,x)H^{(k)}(t,x):=kH(kt,x)is called thekk-th iteration ofHH.

## 2.1.2.Floer complex

If(M,ω)(M,\omega)is a general compact symplectic manifold, the Gromov–Floer compactification of moduli spaces of Floer trajectories fail to be manifolds even for generic choices of(H,J)(H,J)due to the presence of nontrivial automorphisms and thus the failure of transversality. Also, the traditional approach to moduli problems ofJJ-holomorphic maps necessarily introduces rational numbers in the enumeration (see[17][32][37]). Nevertheless, in[10], based on[7,8]and[16], the following construction is realized.

## Theorem 2.1.

([10, Theorem A, Theorem B])
Given a nondegenerateHHand a compatible almost complex structureJJ, there exists a cochain complexC​F​(H,J,Ξ;ΛℤΓ)CF(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})

of free gradedΛℤΓ\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma}-modules, generated by capped 1-periodic orbits ofHH, whose differential depends on a system of choicesΞ\Xi.

Moreover, for any two choices of almost complex structuresJ1J_{1}andJ2J_{2}, and two choices of the auxiliary dataΞ1\Xi_{1}andΞ2\Xi_{2}, there exists a chain homotopy equivalenceΦ12:C​F​(H,J1,Ξ1;ΛℤΓ)→C​F​(H,J2,Ξ2;ΛℤΓ)\Phi^{12}:CF(H,J_{1},\Xi_{1};\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})\to CF(H,J_{2},\Xi_{2};\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})(2.3)

whose homotopy class is independent of choices.

In particular, for any choice(J,Ξ)(J,\Xi), the cohomology ofC​F​(H,J,Ξ;ΛℤΓ)CF(H,J,\Xi;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})is independent of(J,Ξ)(J,\Xi), and we denote the cohomology byH​F​(H;ΛℤΓ)HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma}). When the choice(J,Ξ)(J,\Xi)is irrelevant to the discussion, we often abbreviate the complex byC​F​(H;ΛℤΓ)CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma}).

We often need to switch to coefficients in the (universal) Novikov ring/field with coefficients in a base field𝕂{\mathbb{K}}(often𝕂=𝔽p{\mathbb{K}}={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}). DenoteC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ):=C​F​(H;ΛℤΓ)​⊗ΛℤΓ​Λ𝕂Γ,\displaystyle\ CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}):=CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})\underset{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma},\C​F​(H;Λ𝕂univ):=C​F​(H;ΛℤΓ)​⊗ΛℤΓ​Λ𝕂univ\displaystyle\ CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}):=CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma})\underset{\Lambda_{\mathbb{Z}}^{\Gamma}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}

and denote the corresponding Floer cohomology byH​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})orH​F​(H;Λ𝕂univ)HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}).

From now on, we will no longer use integer coefficients unless explicitly saying so.

## Remark 2.2.

We refer the reader to the introduction of[10]for a complete exposition as we will mostly use formal properties of Hamiltonian Floer theory in this paper. Strictly speaking, the above statement is not exactly the same as the original statement inloc. cit., and we spell out the difference and discuss how to adapt it to the above setting. First, Theorem2.1is for Floercohomologyrather than Floer homology, the latter of which is used in[10, Theorem A]. Second,[10, Theorem A]is stated for continuation maps which also allow the HamiltonianHHto vary, and we state Theorem2.1in the above form as the theme of this paper is concerned with Hamiltonian dynamics. For all our discussions later on, similar comparisons need to be carried out in order to adapt the results from[10]to our setting. We will take this as an implicit assumption as the modifications are cosmetic.

## 2.1.3.Barcodes and bar-length spectrum

By[10, Theorem K], the differential onC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})increases the action of critical points of𝒜H\mathcal{A}_{H}and the continuation mapΦ12\Phi^{12}(see (2.3)) respects the action filtration onC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}). Therefore, we can look at Floer cohomology with action filtrations as in the classical setting.

A few more notations need to be specified.
Let𝒜H:C​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)→ℝ∪{+∞}{\mathcal{A}}_{H}:CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})\to{\mathbb{R}}\cup\{+\infty\}be the energy filtration induced from the symplectic action. For anya∈ℝa\in{\mathbb{R}}, writeC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>a:=𝒜H−1​((a,+∞]).CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}:={\mathcal{A}}_{H}^{-1}((a,+\infty]).

Then the filtered version of Floer cohomology, denotedH​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>aHF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}, is the cohomology ofC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>aCF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}, which only depends onHH. More generally, given some intervalI=(a,b)⊂ℝI=(a,b)\subset\mathbb{R}such thata,b∉Spec​(H)a,b\notin\mathrm{Spec}(H), one similarly definesC​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)I:=C​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>a/C​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>b,CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{I}:=CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}/CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>b},

and its associated cohomology byH​F​(H)IHF(H)^{I}.

Note that for alla∈ℝa\in{\mathbb{R}}anda≥ba\geq bthere are mapsιa:H​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>a→H​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ),\displaystyle\ \iota_{a}:HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}\rightarrow HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}),\ιa,b:H​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>a→H​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>b.\displaystyle\ \iota_{a,b}:HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}\to HF(H;\Lambda_{{\mathbb{K}}}^{\Gamma})^{>b}.

Important quantitative information of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms is contained in theFloer barcodes. In the current generality, one cannot apply the usual approach of defining barcodes via persistence modules. Instead, we follow the approach of Usher–Zhang[52]. Converting to the universal Novikov fieldΛ𝕂univ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ},C​F​(H;Λ𝕂univ)CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ})is a Floer-type complex (where theℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-grading is collapsed) in the sense of[52, Definition 4.1], hence has well-defined barcodes. While the number of infinite bars coincides with the dimension of the cohomology overΛ𝕂univ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}, the lengths of finite bars can be arranged in the non-decreasing order, which is called thebar-length spectrumofC​F​(H;Λ𝕂univ)CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ})0<β1​(H)≤⋯≤βK​(H).0<\beta_{1}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{K}(H).

One can prove that the barcode spectrum only depends on the time-one mapϕ=ϕH1\phi=\phi_{H}^{1}generated byHH; hence we also denote it by0<β1​(ϕ)≤⋯≤βK​(ϕ).0<\beta_{1}(\phi)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{K}(\phi).

The bar-length spectra can also be defined in a different way. Such an idea originated from the Lagrangian Floer setting in[15]. Suppose𝒪​(H)={x1,…,xN}{\mathcal{O}}(H)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{N}\}. Choose their cappingsx¯1,…,x¯N∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{N}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(H). Then the freeΛ0,𝕂univ\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}-moduleC​F​(H;Λ0,𝕂univ):=spanΛ0,𝕂univ​{q−𝒜H​(x1¯)​x1¯,…,q−𝒜H​(xN¯)​xN¯}⊆C​F​(H;Λ𝕂univ).CF(H;\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}):={\rm span}_{\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}}\Big\{q^{-{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\overline{x_{1}})}\overline{x_{1}},\dots,q^{-{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\overline{x_{N}})}\overline{x_{N}}\Big\}\subseteq CF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\rm univ}).(2.4)

is canonically associated toHH. The corresponding Floer cohomologyH​F​(H;Λ0,𝕂univ)HF(H;\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ})splits asΛ0,𝕂univ\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}-modules into the free and torsion partsH​F​(H;Λ0,𝕂univ)≅ℱ⊕𝒯≅ℱ⊕⨁1≤j≤KΛ0,𝕂univ/qβj​(H)​Λ0,𝕂univHF(H;\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ})\cong\mathcal{F}\oplus\mathcal{T}\cong{\mathcal{F}}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq K}\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}/q^{\beta_{j}(H)}\Lambda_{0,{\mathbb{K}}}^{\rm univ}

with0<β1​(H)≤⋯≤βK​(H)0<\beta_{1}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{K}(H). Then[52, Theorem 4.13](see also[48, Lemma 9]) shows thatβ1​(H)≤⋯≤βK​(H)\beta_{1}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{K}(H)also gives rise to the bar-length spectrum ofϕ\phi.

For our applications, we use the following two invariants constructed from the bar-length spectra. The first isβ1​(ϕ)\beta_{1}(\phi), the length of the shortest finite bar. The second isβtot=β1​(ϕ)+⋯+βK​(ϕ),\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}=\beta_{1}(\phi)+\cdots+\beta_{K}(\phi),

called thetotal bar-length.

## 2.1.4.Spectral invariants

One can define important invariants of filtered Floer cohomology, known as spectral invariants. Recall that by[10, Theorem A and B], the Floer cohomologyH​F​(M,ω;Λ𝕂Γ)HF(M,\omega;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})is a well-defined symplectic invariant and there is a canonical isomorphismH​F​(M,ω;Λ𝕂Γ)≅H​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)HF(M,\omega;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})\cong HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}).

## Definition 2.3(Spectral invariants).

Supposeα∈H​F​(M;Λ𝕂Γ)\alpha\in HF(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma}_{\mathbb{K}}). We define thespectral invariantfor a nondegenerate HamiltonianHHbyc(α,H):=sup{a∈ℝ|α∈im(ιa:HF(H;Λ𝕂Γ)>a→HF(M;Λ𝕂Γ))}.c(\alpha,H):=\text{sup}\Big\{a\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ \alpha\in\text{im}(\iota_{a}:HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{>a}\to HF(M;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}))\Big\}.

By[10, Theorem L], the spectral invariants satisfy the usual properties verified in other settings. In particular, the Lipschitz continuity allows us to extendc​(α,H)c(\alpha,H)to degenerateHH.

## 2.1.5.Local Floer cohomology

By “local”, we mean the following: suppose we fix some HamiltonianHH, and consider an isolated Hamiltonian orbitxxofHH(not necessarily non-degenerate). If we restrict to a small neighborhoodUUofxx, and perturbHHover that small neighborhood, then there is a well-defined notion of (ℤ/2{\mathbb{Z}}/2-graded) local Floer cohomology ofHHatxxwith integer coefficientsH​Floc​(H,x;ℤ)HF^{{\rm loc}}(H,x;{\mathbb{Z}}). Ifϕ=ϕH1\phi=\phi_{H}^{1}is the associated Hamiltonian diffeomorphism, we also use the notationH​Floc​(ϕ,x;ℤ)HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi,x;{\mathbb{Z}}). The idea is that one is conducting Floer cohomology “near”xx, by locally perturbingHHso thatxxsplits into some collection of non-degenerate orbits. We will give some details, but leave the bulk of the technical description to, e.g.,[23, Section 3.2].

More formally, we choose some non-degenerateC2C^{2}-small perturbationGGofHH, such thatG=HG=Houtside of some small isolating neighbourhood (i.e. there are no other critical points ofHHwithin) ofxx. This perturbation fromHHtoGG“splits”xxinto nondegenerate 1-periodic orbitsx1,…,xj∈𝒪​(G)x_{1},\dots,x_{j}\in{\mathcal{O}}(G). Moreover, any cappingx¯\overline{x}ofxxcanonically induces cappingsx¯1,…,x¯j\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{j}. Then define the chain complexC​Floc​(H,G,x¯;ℤ):=ℤ​⟨x¯1,⋯,x¯j⟩.CF^{{\rm loc}}(H,G,\overline{x};{\mathbb{Z}}):={\mathbb{Z}}\langle\overline{x}_{1},\cdots,\overline{x}_{j}\rangle.

One observes that ifGGandHHare sufficiently close, then action bounds ensure the moduli space of flow lines forGGbetween anyxi1x_{i_{1}}andxi2x_{i_{2}}which are contained in this isolating neighborhood is compact up to breaking.
Hence there is a well defined notion ofH​Floc​(H,G,x¯;ℤ)HF^{{\rm loc}}(H,G,\overline{x};{\mathbb{Z}}). One can also show (by a standard continuation map argument) that this is independent of the choice ofGG(as long as they remain close toHH). Hence, we label the resulting local Floer cohomologyH​Floc​(H,x¯;ℤ)HF^{{\rm loc}}(H,\overline{x};{\mathbb{Z}}). Regarded as aℤ/2{\mathbb{Z}}/2-graded module,H​Floc​(H,x¯;ℤ)HF^{{\rm loc}}(H,\overline{x};{\mathbb{Z}})is independent of the capping and only dependsxxas a fixed point of the time-one mapϕ\phi. We denote this byH​Floc​(ϕ,x;ℤ).HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,x;{\mathbb{Z}}).

By tensoring with𝕂{\mathbb{K}}for any base field𝕂{\mathbb{K}}on the chain complexes, one obtains the resulting local Floer cohomology with𝕂{\mathbb{K}}-coefficients.

On the other hand, we can defineC​Floc​(H,G,x;Λ𝕂Γ)CF^{\rm loc}(H,G,x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})to be the action-completed direct sum⨁x¯^​C​Floc​(H,G,x¯;𝕂)\widehat{\bigoplus_{\overline{x}}}CF^{\rm loc}(H,G,\overline{x};{\mathbb{K}})

over all cappingsx¯\overline{x}ofxx, which is a module overΛ𝕂Γ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}; its homology is denoted byH​Floc​(H,x;Λ𝕂Γ)HF^{\rm loc}(H,x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})

which has a well-definedℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-grading.

We explain how the usual approach to local Floer cohomology is compatible with the Hamiltonian Floer theory construction in[10]. For more details, we refer the reader to the proof of[10, Theorem G](which compares the virtual approach therein with the classical approach for semipositive symplectic manifolds). It is known that for anyω\omega-compatibleJJ, there exists anℏ>0\hbar>0such that for any non-constantJJ-holomorphic spherev:S2→Mv:S^{2}\to M, its energy satisfiesE​(v)≥ℏE(v)\geq\hbar(see, e.g.,[34, Proposition 4.1.4]). Therefore, for a sufficiently small perturbationGG, the moduli spaces of Floer trajectories definingC​Floc​(H,G,x)CF^{{\rm loc}}(H,G,x)do not have sphere bubbles in their compactification. Furthermore, the classical approach to Hamiltonian Floer theory ensures that for a generic choice ofGG, the relevant moduli spaces are cut out transversely. In this setting, running through the technical construction from[10], one sees that one can take the FOP perturbations over the flow categories to be the unperturbed Kuranishi sections, whose zero loci are exactly these transverse moduli spaces. Therefore, when discussing local Floer cohomology, we do not distinguish the two approaches.

## 2.1.6.Filtered Floer cohomology for degenerate Hamiltonians

We have discussed filtered Floer cohomology for nondegenerate Hamiltonians. For degenerate Hamiltonians, we use the work of Hein[25]to define their filtered Floer cohomology as follows.

Suppose we are given a degenerate HamiltonianHHon a
symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega), anda<b∉Spec​(H)a<b\notin\text{Spec}(H). K<K^{\prime}\ &\ {\rm if}\ K(t,x)<K^{\prime}(t,x)\ \forall(t,x)\in S^{1}\times M\end{split}

Further, denote byPa,b​(H)P_{a,b}(H)the set of HamiltoniansKKsatisfying

KKis nondegenerate,

a,b∉Spec​(K)a,b\notin{\rm Spec}(K).

Pa,b​(H)P_{a,b}(H)has a partial order defined byK≤K′K\leq K^{\prime}pointwise. Then by[10, Theorem B, Theorem K], there is a well-defined continuation mapH​F​(K;Λ𝕂Γ)(a,b)→H​F​(K′;Λ𝕂Γ)(a,b)HF(K;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{(a,b)}\rightarrow HF(K^{\prime};\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{(a,b)}

induced from any monotone homotopy fromKKtoK′K^{\prime}. Therefore, we can defineH​F​(H;Λ𝕂Γ)(a,b):=lim→K∈Pa,b​(H)⁡H​F​(K;Λ𝕂Γ)(a,b).HF(H;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{(a,b)}:=\varinjlim_{K\in P_{a,b}(H)}HF(K;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})^{(a,b)}.(2.5)

The details are provided in the cited[25, Section 2.3], which only uses formal properties of (filtered) continuation maps. In particular, one can show that this definition reduces to the previous definition in the case ofHHnondegenerate or(M,ω)(M,\omega)rational, so we will take (2.5) to be the general definition.

## 2.2.Equivariant cohomology

In this subsection, we recall some classical topological and algebraic constructions ofℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-equivariant cohomology.333The algebraic constructions presented here all have a characteristic22version, which takes slightly different forms.

## 2.2.1.The algebraic Borel construction

We first describe the algebraic way of defining equivariant cohomology from aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action on a complex. Note that for any odd primepp,H∗​(B​ℤ/p;𝔽p)≅𝔽p​[[t]]​[θ]/(θ2),H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/p;\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong\mathbb{F}_{p}[\![t]\!][\theta]/(\theta^{2}),

wheretthas degree22andθ\thetahas degree11. In this paper, the following notations will be used frequently. Denote𝒦0,p:=𝔽p​[[t]],\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}:={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!],\𝒦p:=𝔽p​((t));\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{K}_{p}}:={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}(\!(t)\!);E​(θ):=𝔽p​[θ]/⟨θ2⟩,\displaystyle\ E(\theta):={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\theta]/\langle\theta^{2}\rangle,\ℛp:=𝔽p​[[t]]⊗E​(θ),\displaystyle\ {\mathcal{R}_{p}}:={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]\otimes E(\theta),

so we haveH∗​(B​ℤ/p;𝔽p)≅ℛp.H^{*}(B\mathbb{Z}/p;\mathbb{F}_{p})\cong{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

## Definition 2.4(Algebraic Borel Construction).

Let(C,d)(C,d)be a cochain complex over𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}with aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action generated by a chain mapι:C→C\iota:C\to C. TheBorel equivariant cochain complexassociated toCCandι\iotais the graded cochain complexCℤ/p:=C⊗ℛp,C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:=C\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}},(2.6)

whose generators are of the formx​tα​θβxt^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}forx∈Cx\in C,α≥0\alpha\geq 0andβ=0,1\beta=0,1. The equivariant differentialdℤ/pd_{\mathbb{Z}/p}onCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}istt-linear and determined by the following rulesdℤ/p​(x⊗ti)=(d​x)⊗ti+(−1)|x|​(ι​x−x)⊗θ​tidℤ/p​(x⊗θ​ti)=(d​x)⊗θ​ti+(−1)|x|​(1+ι+⋯+ιp−1)​x⊗ti+1.\begin{array}[]{lclll}d_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(x\otimes t^{i})&=&(dx)\otimes t^{i}&+&(-1)^{|x|}(\iota x-x)\otimes\theta t^{i}\\
d_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(x\otimes\theta t^{i})&=&(dx)\otimes\theta t^{i}&+&(-1)^{|x|}(1+\iota+\cdots+\iota^{p-1})x\otimes t^{i+1}.\end{array}(2.7)

The equivariant cohomologyHℤ/p​(C)H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C)is the cohomology of(Cℤ/p,dℤ/p)(C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}},d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}).

## Remark 2.5.

Notice that the equivariant differential is not linear inθ\theta; henceCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}is only a complex of𝒦0,p{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}-modules. However, the cohomology ofCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}is a module overℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}. Indeed, a cochain mapθ~:Cℤ/p∗→Cℤ/p∗+1\tilde{\theta}:C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{*}\to C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{*+1}is defined in[41, Section 2d]byθ~​(x​tk)=(−1)|x|​x​tk​θ,θ~​(x​tk​θ)=(−1)|x|​(ι​x+2​ι2​x+⋯+(p−1)​ιp−1​x)​tk+1.\begin{split}\tilde{\theta}(xt^{k})=&\ (-1)^{|x|}xt^{k}\theta,\\
\tilde{\theta}(xt^{k}\theta)=&\ (-1)^{|x|}\Big(\iota x+2\iota^{2}x+\cdots+(p-1)\iota^{p-1}x\Big)t^{k+1}.\end{split}

On cohomology level it induces a well-defined “multiplication byθ\theta” map and henceHℤ/p​(C)H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C)becomes anℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module.

There are two special cases we will use in this paper.

## Example 2.6.

IfCCis equipped with the trivialℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action, then the equivariant differentialℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-linearly extends the differential onCCand the equivariant cohomology isHℤ/p​(C)≅H​(C)⊗ℛp.H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C)\cong H(C)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Notice that this is an isomorphism ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules.

## Example 2.7.

For a general cochain complexCC, thepp-fold tensor powerC⊗p=C⊗⋯⊗CC^{\otimes p}=C\otimes\dots\otimes Chas a naturalℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action by cyclically permuting the factors, resulting in an equivariant cochain complex with underlying spaceCℤ/p⊗p:=C⊗p⊗ℛp.C^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:=C^{\otimes p}\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

The case of complexes over Novikov ring/fields deserves more careful treatment. We work over the universal Novikov field abbreviated asΛ=Λuniv\Lambda=\Lambda^{\rm univ}where we suppress the base field𝕂=𝔽p{\mathbb{K}}={\mathbb{F}}_{p}from the notation; the cases with other versions of Novikov rings are similar. LetCCbe a complex of modules overΛ\Lambdaequipped with a filtration𝒜C:C∖{0}→ℝ{\mathcal{A}}_{C}:C\setminus\{0\}\to{\mathbb{R}}

satisfying𝒜C​(qλ​x)=𝒜C​(x)+λ.{\mathcal{A}}_{C}(q^{\lambda}x)={\mathcal{A}}_{C}(x)+\lambda.

If(C,𝒜C)(C,{\mathcal{A}}_{C})and(D,𝒜D)(D,{\mathcal{A}}_{D})are two such complexes, then the naive tensor productC⊗DC\otimes DoverΛ\Lambdahas a completionC​⊗^Λ​DC\widehat{\otimes}_{\Lambda}D

with respect to𝒜C+𝒜D{\mathcal{A}}_{C}+{\mathcal{A}}_{D}, which is again aΛ\Lambda-module. Notice that when bothCCandDDare finitely generated, the completed tensor product agrees with the algebraic one; this is the only case we consider in this paper. ThenC⊗p:=C​⊗Λ​⋯​⊗Λ​C⏞pC^{\otimes p}:=\overbrace{C\underset{\Lambda}{\otimes}\cdots\underset{\Lambda}{\otimes}C}^{p}

denotes thepp-fold (completed) tensor product ofCC, which is still aΛ\Lambda-module with a filtration𝒜C⊗p{\mathcal{A}}_{C}^{{\otimes}p}. Moreover, we can form the tensor product overC⊗p⊗ℛp=C⊗p⊗𝔽p​[[t]]⊗E​(θ)C^{\otimes p}{\otimes}{\mathcal{R}_{p}}=C^{{\otimes}p}{\otimes}{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]{\otimes}E(\theta)

which is a module over the (algebraic) tensor productΛ⊗𝔽p​[[t]]=Λ⊗𝒦0,p\Lambda\otimes{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]=\Lambda\otimes{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}. The tensor productΛ⊗𝔽p​[[t]]\Lambda\otimes{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]can be completed toΛ𝒦0,p=Λ𝔽p​[[t]].\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}=\Lambda_{{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]}.

Then defineCℤ/p⊗p:=(C⊗p​⊗𝔽p​ℛp)​⊗Λ⊗𝒦0,p​Λ𝒦0,pC^{{\otimes}p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:=\left(C^{{\otimes}p}\underset{{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}{\otimes}{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\right)\underset{\Lambda\otimes{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}

which has aΛ𝒦0,p\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}-linear differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}which takes the same form as (2.7). Finally, the corresponding definition ofCℤ/p⊗pC^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}for the case thatCCis a finitely generated freeΛ0univ\Lambda_{0}^{\rm univ}-module is the same, which gives a complex of modules overΛ0,𝒦0,puniv\Lambda_{0,{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\rm univ}. The two versions of equivariant cohomology will be distinguished in notations asHℤ/p​(C⊗p;Λ𝒦0,p),\displaystyle\ H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C^{\otimes p};\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}),\Hℤ/p​(C⊗p;Λ0,𝒦0,puniv).\displaystyle\ H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C^{\otimes p};\Lambda_{0,{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\rm univ}).

Now we introduce the so-calledquasi-Frobenius map. We explain the case without Novikov coefficients. LetCCbe a complex of𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-modules with the trivialℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. For any cocyclex∈Cx\in C, thepp-fold powerx⊗px^{\otimes p}is an equivariant cocycle inCℤ/p⊗pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\otimes p}. The associationx↦x⊗px\mapsto x^{\otimes p}induces a well-defined mapq​F:H​(C)→Hℤ/p​(C⊗p)qF:H(C)\to H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C^{\otimes p})

(see[41, Lemma 2.5]). The map is only additive after inverting the variablett. In other words, it is additive after turning to the Tate version of equivariant cohomology, which is introduced right below.

## 2.2.2.Tate cohomology

To discuss equivariant localizations, we work with the Tate construction as follows. As above, consider a cochain complexCCover𝔽p\mathbb{F}_{p}, equipped with aℤ/p\mathbb{Z}/p-action generated byι:C→C\iota:C\to C. Algebraically, the Tate complex is the graded cochain complexC^ℤ/p:=Cℤ/p​⊗𝒦0,p​𝒦p≅Cℤ/p​[t−1]\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}}/p}:=C_{{\mathbb{Z}}/p}\underset{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}{\otimes}{\mathcal{K}_{p}}\cong C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}[t^{-1}]

withCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}}/p}defined in (2.6), equipped with the differential which is the𝒦p{\mathcal{K}_{p}}-linear extension of (2.7). We denote the cohomology byH^ℤ/p​(C)\widehat{H}_{{\mathbb{Z}}/p}(C).

WhenCCis a finitely generated module over Novikov rings, the Tate cohomology is defined similarly. For example, supposeCCis defined over the universal Novikov ringΛuniv\Lambda^{\rm univ}. Then one definesC^ℤ/p:=C​⊗Λuniv​Λ𝒦puniv\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:=C\underset{\Lambda^{\rm univ}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\rm univ}

which contains the Borel versionCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}. The differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}extends to aΛ𝒦puniv\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\rm univ}-linear one and leads to the Tate cohomologyH^ℤ/p​(C;Λ𝒦puniv)\widehat{H}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(C;\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\rm univ}).

Regarding applications in Hamiltonian dynamics, one considers the quasi-Frobenius map for modules over the universal Novikov ringΛ0univ\Lambda_{0}^{\rm univ}.

## 2.3.Equivariant cohomology via Morse theory

It is often more convenient to realize the Borel construction on the chain level using equivariant Morse theory. We choose theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant Borel model as follows. ConsiderS∞:={v=(v0,v1,⋯)|vi∈ℂ,i≫0⟹vi=0,∑i=0∞|vi|2=1}S^{\infty}:=\Big\{v=(v_{0},v_{1},\cdots)\ |\ v_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}},\ i\gg 0\Longrightarrow v_{i}=0,\ \sum_{i=0}^{\infty}|v_{i}|^{2}=1\Big\}

which has a freeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action by simultaneously multiplying coordinatesviv_{i}bypp-th roots of unity. The quotient space is a classifying space forℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}, denoted byB​ℤ/pB{\mathbb{Z}/p}. On the other hand, notice that there is a freeℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-action generated byτ​(v0,v1,⋯)=(0,v0,v1,⋯)\tau(v_{0},v_{1},\cdots)=(0,v_{0},v_{1},\cdots)

which commutes with theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action.

S∞S^{\infty}admits a finite-dimensional approximation using smooth manifoldsS2​n−1S^{2n-1}. Any object onS∞S^{\infty}is called smooth if its restriction to each truncationS2​n−1S^{2n-1}is smooth in the usual sense. Now we choose a special Morse function onS∞S^{\infty}; we do not intend to compare it with other choices. Defineg:S∞→ℝ(v0,v1,⋯)↦∑l≥0(l​|vl|2+ϵ⋅Re​(vlp))\begin{split}g:S^{\infty}\to&\ \mathbb{R}\\
(v_{0},v_{1},\cdots)\mapsto&\ \sum_{l\geq 0}\Big(l|v_{l}|^{2}+\epsilon\cdot\text{Re}(v_{l}^{p})\Big)\end{split}(2.9)

which was the choice used in[45, Section 4]. Notice thatggis invariant under theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action and satisfiesg​(τ​(v))=g​(v)+1g(\tau(v))=g(v)+1. Define the Morse index in the usual way, which is the number of negative eigenvalues of the Hessian.

One can explicitly write down the coordinates of critical points. For each even degree2​l2l, theppcritical points of index2​l2larew2​lk=(0,…,0⏟l,e2​k​π​𝐢p−π​𝐢p,0,⋯),k=1,2,…,p;w_{2l}^{k}=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l},e^{\frac{2k\pi{\bf i}}{p}-\frac{\pi{\bf i}}{p}},0,\cdots),\ k=1,2,\ldots,p;(2.10)

for each odd degree2​l+12l+1, theppcritical points of index2​l+12l+1arew2​l+1k=(0,…,0⏟l,e2​k​π​𝐢p,0,⋯),k=1,2,…,p.w_{2l+1}^{k}=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{l},e^{\frac{2k\pi{\bf i}}{p}},0,\cdots),\ k=1,2,\ldots,p.(2.11)

Then one obtains a Morse cochain complex associated toggand a suitably chosen Riemannian metric invariant underℤ/p×ℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}/p}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-action. More precisely, one definesC​M​(g)={∑i=1∞ai​wi|ai∈𝔽p,wi∈crit​(g),limi→∞g​(wi)=+∞}CM(g)=\Big\{\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}a_{i}w_{i}\ |\ a_{i}\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\ w_{i}\in{\rm crit}(g),\ \lim_{i\to\infty}g(w_{i})=+\infty\Big\}

which is a formal completion of the usual Morse cochain complex of finite sums. For each degreek≥0k\geq 0, there are exactlyppgenerators. Theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part of the chain complex recovers theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant cohomology of a point; indeed, in𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-coefficients, theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part has a trivial differential and is isomorphic to the groupℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

## 2.3.1.Ring structure ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}

We also realize the ring structure ofℛp≅Hℤ/p∗​(pt){\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong H_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{*}({\rm pt})using the Morse model. LetT2,1T_{2,1}denote the tree which is the disjoint union of two incoming edgesT1≅T2≅(−∞,0]T_{1}\cong T_{2}\cong(-\infty,0]and an outgoing edgeT∞≅[0,+∞)T_{\infty}\cong[0,+\infty). Lets∈T2,1s\in T_{2,1}denote the coordinate on each edge; when there is no ambiguity,sscorresponds to a real number. To achieve transversality, we choose aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant family of vector fieldsYsY_{s}parametrized bys∈T2,1s\in T_{2,1}
- (1)

Y​(s)=0Y(s)=0when|s|≫0|s|\gg 0.
- (2)

For eachnn,Y|S2​n−1Y|_{S^{2n-1}}is tangent toS2​n−1⊂S∞S^{2n-1}\subset S^{\infty}and∇Y\nabla Yis sufficiently small in the normal direction ofS2​n−1S^{2n-1}.

LetYiY_{i}denote the restriction ofYYtoTi⊂T2,1T_{i}\subset T_{2,1}. AYY-perturbed flow treeis a tripleζ=(ζ1,ζ2,ζ∞):T2,1→S∞\zeta=(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2},\zeta_{\infty}):T_{2,1}\to S^{\infty}which solves the equationζ′​(s)=∇g​(ζ​(s))+Ys​(ζ​(s))\zeta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla g(\zeta(s))+Y_{s}(\zeta(s))

subject to the incidence relationζ1​(0)=ζ2​(0)=ζ∞​(0).\zeta_{1}(0)=\zeta_{2}(0)=\zeta_{\infty}(0).

Each flow tree converges to a triple of critical points ofggat∞\inftyof the edges.

Given a triple of critical pointsw1,w2,w∞∈crit​(g)w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}\in{\rm crit}(g), letS2​n−1⊂S∞S^{2n-1}\subset S^{\infty}be the smallest truncation which contains all of them. One can see that a flow tree connectingw1,w2,w∞w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}is contained inS2​n−1S^{2n-1}. We say that aYY-perturbed flow tree is regular for(w1,w2,w∞)(w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty})if it is regular as a flow tree contained inS2​n−1S^{2n-1}.666In fact if it is regular inS2​n−1S^{2n-1}, then it is regular in any larger truncation.

For a generic perturbationYYand each triple of critical pointsw1,w2,w∞w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}, one obtains a regular moduli space of solutions, whose modppcount induces aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant mapC​M​(g)⊗C​M​(g)→C​M​(g).CM(g)\otimes CM(g)\to CM(g).

Hence after restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part, one obtainsℛp⊗ℛp≅C​Mℤ/p​(g)⊗C​Mℤ/p​(g)→C​Mℤ/p​(g)≅ℛp,{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(g)\otimes CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(g)\to CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(g)\cong{\mathcal{R}_{p}},

from which one obtains a bilinear mapℛp×ℛp→ℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\times{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\to{\mathcal{R}_{p}}. The usual Morse theoretic technique can be used to show that the bilinear map is independent of the choices ofYY, and satisfies associativity. Henceℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}is equipped with a structure of aℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-graded, associative and supercommutative algebra over𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}.

Using any finite truncation ofS∞S^{\infty}, and because we are passing to the formal completion when defining the cochain complex, we see that the ring structure defined by Morse cohomology agrees with the standard ring structure onℛp≅𝔽p​[[t]]⊗E​(θ){\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}[\![t]\!]\otimes E(\theta)(whenp>2p>2) orℛp≅𝔽2​[[θ]]{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong{\mathbb{F}}_{2}[\![\theta]\!](whenp=2p=2).

## 2.3.2.Equivariant Morse cohomology when equivariant transversality holds

Recall that given a cochain complexCCwith aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action, one has defined an algebraic version of theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant cochain complexCℤ/pC_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}as shown. WhenCCis realized as a Morse cochain complex for aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse–Smale pair(f,h)(f,h)on a smooth manifoldMMwith aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action, this algebraic construction can be realized Morse-theoretically. Indeed, the product functionf×gf\times g, by which we meanπM∗​f+πS∞∗​g\pi_{M}^{*}f+\pi_{S^{\infty}}^{*}gwhereπM,πS∞\pi_{M},\pi_{S^{\infty}}are the projections from the product, and the product metric onM×S∞M\times S^{\infty}form a Morse–Smale pair and hence a Morse complexC​M​(f×g)≅C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g)CM(f\times g)\cong CM(f)\otimes CM(g). Theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part is a subcomplex, denoted byC​Mℤ/p​(f):=C​M​(f×g)Inv.CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f):=CM(f\times g)^{{\rm Inv}}.

Notice that this complex is isomorphic to the algebraically defined one, but the isomorphism is not canonical. For example, the followingC​M​(f)⊗ℛp→C​M​(f×g)Invx⊗ti↦∑l=1p(ιl​x)⊗w2​il,x⊗ti​θ↦∑l=1p(ιl​x)⊗w2​i+1l\begin{split}CM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}&\ \to CM(f\times g)^{\rm Inv}\\
x\otimes t^{i}&\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{p}(\iota^{l}x)\otimes w_{2i}^{l},\\
x\otimes t^{i}\theta&\mapsto\sum_{l=1}^{p}(\iota^{l}x)\otimes w_{2i+1}^{l}\end{split}

is an isomorphism of complexes, whereι∈ℤ/p\iota\in{\mathbb{Z}/p}is the generator.

Notice that whenℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}acts trivially onMM, the equivariant Morse complex is then isomorphic toC​M​(f)⊗ℛpCM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}and the equivariant Morse cohomology is isomorphic toH​M​(f)⊗ℛpHM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

In the current situation, the structure ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules on the equivariant cohomologyH​Mℤ/p​(f)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)can be defined both algebraically and Morse-theoretically. Indeed they coincide.

## Proposition 2.9.

Asℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules, the equivariant Morse cohomologyH​Mℤ/p​(f)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)and the equivariant cohomology of the complexC​M​(f)CM(f)coincide. In particular, whenℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}acts trivially onMM, asℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules, one hasH​Mℤ/p​(f)≅H​M​(f)⊗ℛp.HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)\cong HM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

## Proof.

Indeed, the algebraic definition ofCℤ/p​(C​M​(f))C_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(CM(f))uses a specificℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant cellular decomposition ofS∞S^{\infty}(see details in[41]) and theℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module structure (indeed only the cochain mapθ~\tilde{\theta}) depends on aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant cellular approximation of the diagonal map ofS∞S^{\infty}. The Morse-theoretic definition, which counts Y-shaped flow trees, gives another equivariant cellular approximation. As the cellular approximations are (chain) homotopic, the induced multiplications agree on the cohomology level.
∎

## 2.3.3.Equivariant Morse cohomology without assuming equivariant transversality

Now letMMbe a manifold with aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action andf:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}}be aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse function.777In general, by perturbation one can achieve the Morse condition equivariantly but not the Morse–Smale condition.In order to define the equivariant Morse cochain complex, we use the following Borel type construction. Consider again the functionf×g:M×S∞→ℝf\times g:M\times S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}. Now theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action onM×S∞M\times S^{\infty}is free, allowing us to achieve equivariant transversality.

There are many concrete choices of perturbations. For example, one can perturb the functionffto allow it to depend onv∈S∞v\in S^{\infty}, i.e., aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant function(v,x)↦fv​(x)(v,x)\mapsto f_{v}(x)onS∞×MS^{\infty}\times Msuch thatfv​(x)=f​(x)f_{v}(x)=f(x)when(v,x)(v,x)is close to a critical point off×gf\times g. One can further ensure that the set of critical points is unchanged under the perturbation provided that the perturbation is sufficiently small inC1C^{1}-norm. Then one can consider the coupled flow line equation for(η,ζ):ℝ→M×S∞(\eta,\zeta):{\mathbb{R}}\to M\times S^{\infty}:η′​(s)=∇fζ​(s)​(η​(s)),\displaystyle\ \eta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla f_{\zeta(s)}(\eta(s)),\ζ′​(s)=∇g​(ζ​(s)),\displaystyle\ \zeta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla g(\zeta(s)),

which has again an obvious translation symmetry. Define the Morse cochain complex as the (completed) tensor productC​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g)CM(f)\otimes CM(g)

whose differential counts rigid perturbed flow lines. DefineC​Mℤ/p​(f):=(C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g))InvCM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f):=(CM(f)\otimes CM(g))^{\rm Inv}

as a subcomplex, whose chain homotopy type is independent of the perturbation.

By using perturbed flow trees inS∞S^{\infty}, one can also define a module structure ofH​Mℤ/p​(f)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)overℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}induced by a chain-level bilinear mapC​Mℤ/p​(f)⊗C​Mℤ/p​(g)→C​Mℤ/p​(f).CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)\otimes CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(g)\to CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f).

parametrized bys∈ℝs\in{\mathbb{R}}andv∈S∞v\in S^{\infty}such that 1)fs,v=ff_{s,v}=fnears=0s=0and 2)fs,v=fvf_{s,v}=f_{v}for|s|≫0|s|\gg 0and 3)fs,γ​v​(γ​x)=fs,v​(x)f_{s,\gamma v}(\gamma x)=f_{s,v}(x)for allγ∈ℤ/p\gamma\in{\mathbb{Z}/p}. The coefficients of the equation onξ\xiare smooth. We then impose critical point asymptoticsξ​(±∞)∈crit​(f)\xi(\pm\infty)\in{\rm crit}(f). Its restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part givesC​Mℤ/p​(f)⊗C​Mℤ/p​(g)↪(C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g)⊗C​M​(g))ℤ/p→C​Mℤ/p​(f)CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)\otimes CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(g)\hookrightarrow(CM(f)\otimes CM(g)\otimes CM(g))^{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\to CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f)

## 2.4.The quantum Steenrod power operation

There are a fewa prioridifferent definitions of the quantum Steenrod operation𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}involved in this paper as well as related literature. For (weakly) monotone(M,ω)(M,\omega), Seidel–Wilkins[41]defined𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}using the cellular chain model of the classifying spaceB​ℤ/pB{\mathbb{Z}/p}, which matches the one used in[43]. In this paper, for our purpose, we will combine the construction in[41]with the Morse model ofB​ℤ/pB{\mathbb{Z}/p}to give an alternate definition for (weakly) monotone(M,ω)(M,\omega), which will be used only in proving the Kunneth property (Theorem1.11) of𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}in the monotone case. In AppendixAwe will show that the two definitions agree. On the other hand, the first and the fourth authors[10]defined𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}for general compact(M,ω)(M,\omega), which will be recalled and used in other applications in this paper. In[10], it was shown that the general definition agrees with the above more traditional definitions for (weakly) monotone targets.

We first give a formal description of the quantum Steenrod operation using the Morse model, including the particular moduli spaces, without discussing perturbations. Then we recall the theorem in[10]for the construction in the general case. The construction using inhomogeneous perturbations will be recalled in the section where we prove the Kunneth property of the quantum Steenrod operation for monotone targets.

Consider a closed symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega)which has the associated Novikov ringΛΓ\Lambda^{\Gamma}(see (2.1)) with base field𝕂=𝔽p{\mathbb{K}}={{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}. Choose a Morse functionffand a Morse–Smale metric onMM, which give rise to a Morse chain complexC​M​(f)CM(f). Choose anω\omega-compatible almost complex structureJJ.

We use a specific domain for defining the moduli space. On the other hand, consider thepp-fold coverz↦zpz\mapsto z^{p}fromS2→S2S^{2}\to S^{2}. Denote the sourceS2S^{2}byΣp\Sigma_{p}, which has aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Specify the special pointszk=e2​π​k​𝐢p,k=1,2,…,p,z∞=∞∈Σp.z_{k}=e^{\frac{2\pi k{\bf i}}{p}},\ k=1,2,\ldots,p,\ z_{\infty}=\infty\in\Sigma_{p}.

Now we describe the moduli spaces. For each collection of critical pointsy1,…,yp,y∞∈crit​(f)y_{1},\ldots,y_{p},y_{\infty}\in{\rm crit}(f)and a given homology classA∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}), one can consider tuples(u,γ1,…,γp,γ∞)(u,\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p},\gamma_{\infty})whereu:Σp→Mu:\Sigma_{p}\to Mis aJJ-holomorphic map in classAA,γ1,…,γp:(−∞,0]→M\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p}:(-\infty,0]\to Mare gradient lines offfwhich are asymptotic toy1,…,ypy_{1},\ldots,y_{p}at−∞-\inftyandγ∞:[0,+∞)→M\gamma_{\infty}:[0,+\infty)\to Mis a gradient line offfwhich is asymptotic toy∞y_{\infty}at+∞+\infty; they are also required to satisfy the matching conditionu​(zk)=γk​(0),k=1,…,p,∞.u(z_{k})=\gamma_{k}(0),\ k=1,\ldots,p,\infty.

To count such objects equivariantly, we need to use the Morse-theoretic Borel construction and achieveℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant transversality. To this end, we couple the mapuuand the gradient raysγ1,…,γp,γ∞\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p},\gamma_{\infty}with (parametrized) gradient lines inS∞S^{\infty}. More precisely, givenyk∈crit​(f)y_{k}\in{\rm crit}(f),k=1,…,p,∞k=1,\ldots,p,\infty,A∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}), andw±∈crit​(g)⊂S∞w_{\pm}\in{\rm crit}(g)\subset S^{\infty}, consider the moduli spaceℳw−,w+​(A;y1,…,yp,y∞){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(A;y_{1},\ldots,y_{p},y_{\infty})

of tuples(u,γ1,…,γp,γ∞,w)(u,\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p},\gamma_{\infty},w)where(u,γ1,…,γp,γ∞)(u,\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{p},\gamma_{\infty})are described as above whilew:ℝ→S∞w:{\mathbb{R}}\to S^{\infty}is a gradient flow line ofggwhich converges tow±w_{\pm}at±∞\pm\infty. Notice that as theℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action onS∞S^{\infty}is free, there is a free action on the disjoint union of these moduli spaces over a fixedA∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})and all possible asymptotic limits of the Morse flow lines/rays. This is the reason why equivariant transversality is possible.

Using either inhomogeneous terms or abstract FOP perturbations, one can define𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-valued signed counts of the moduli spacesℳw−,w+​(A;y1,…,yp,y∞){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(A;y_{1},\ldots,y_{p},y_{\infty})(which vanish by definition if the expected dimension is not zero). One then obtains a𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-linear chain mapC​M​(f)⊗p⊗C​M​(g)→C​M​(f×g)≅C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g)CM(f)^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\to CM(f\times g)\cong CM(f)\otimes CM(g)

whose value on the generatory1⊗⋯⊗yp⊗w−y_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes y_{p}\otimes w_{-}is∑w+#​y∞,w+w−,w+​(A;y1,…,yp,y∞)​(y∞⊗w+).\sum_{w_{+}}\#{y_{\infty},w_{+}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(A;y_{1},\ldots,y_{p},y_{\infty})(y_{\infty}\otimes w_{+}).

Theℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariance on the perturbation implies that the above chain map admits a well-defined restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part (with respect to the action onC​(g)C(g)while permuting theppfactors inC​(f)⊗pC(f)^{\otimes p}), defining an𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-linear cochain map𝑄𝑆𝑡~p,A:C​M​(f)ℤ/p⊗p→(C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g))Inv≅C​M​(f)⊗ℛp\widetilde{{\it QSt}}_{p,A}:CM(f)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\to\Big(CM(f)\otimes CM(g)\Big)^{\rm Inv}\cong CM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}

which induces a map on cohomology𝑄𝑆𝑡~p,A:H​(C​M​(f)ℤ/p⊗p)→H​M​(f)⊗ℛp.\widetilde{{\it QSt}}_{p,A}:H(CM(f)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})\to HM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

The degreeAApart of the quantum Steenrod operation is the composition of the above map with the quasi-Frobenius mapq​F:H​(M;𝔽p)≅H​M​(f)→H​(C​M​(f)ℤ/p⊗p).qF:H(M;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\cong HM(f)\to H(CM(f)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}).

Define𝑄𝑆𝑡p,A:=𝑄𝑆𝑡~p,A∘q​F:H​(M;𝔽p)→H​(C​M​(f))⊗ℛp≅H​(M)⊗ℛp.{\it QSt}_{p,A}:=\widetilde{{\it QSt}}_{p,A}\circ qF:H(M;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\to H(CM(f))\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong H(M)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Finally, the following weighted sum is well-defined after suitable completion:𝑄𝑆𝑡p:=∑A∈H2​(M;ℤ)qA​𝑄𝑆𝑡p,A:H​(M;ΛΓ)→H​(M;ΛℛpΓ).{\it QSt}_{p}:=\sum_{A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})}q^{A}{\it QSt}_{p,A}:H(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma})\to H(M;\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}^{\Gamma}).

## Theorem 2.10.

([10, Theorem H]) For any compact symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega), the quantum Steenrod operation𝑄𝑆𝑡p:H​(M;ΛΓ)→H∗​(M;ΛℛpΓ){\it QSt}_{p}:H(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma})\to H^{*}(M;\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}^{\Gamma})

is well-defined. Moreover, its classical part coincides with the total Steenrod operationS​tp:H​(M;𝔽p)→H​(M;𝔽p)⊗ℛp.St_{p}:H(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\to H(M;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

When(M,ω)(M,\omega)is weakly monotone, this definition agrees with that of[41].

## Remark 2.11.

The comparison result[10, Theorem 24.6]is established by taking the Morse-theoretic model in the definition of𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}in the weakly semi-positive setting, which is different from the cellular models in[41]. The claimed result follows by combining[10, Theorem 24.6]and the results in AppendixA.

## 2.5.Equivariant Floer cohomology

To study prime iterations of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms, we need to study equivariant Hamiltonian Floer cohomology. In this subsection, we review the abstract construction of[10, Part 4]in the general setting using virtual perturbations. We also review the definition of the local equivariant cohomology (which is equivalent to the definition of[45]).

We quickly set up the relevant notations. Letppbe a prime. Given the HamiltonianH:M×S1→ℝH:M\times S^{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}, define itspp-th iterationH(p):M×S1→ℝH^{(p)}:M\times S^{1}\to{\mathbb{R}}byH(p)​(x,t):=p​H​(x,p​t),H^{(p)}(x,t):=pH(x,{pt}),

whose associated time-1 map is the Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕH(p)1=(ϕH1)p\phi^{1}_{H^{(p)}}=(\phi^{1}_{H})^{p}. Then the action functional𝒜H(p){\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}is invariant under theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-action onL​M~\widetilde{LM}whose generator acts by(x​(t),x¯​(z))↦(x​(t+1p),x¯​(e2​π​ip⋅z)).(x(t),\overline{x}(z))\mapsto(x(t+\frac{1}{p}),\overline{x}(e^{\frac{2\pi i}{p}}\cdot z)).

The equivariant Floer cohomologyH​Fℤ/p​(H(p))HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})is formally the equivariant Morse cohomology of𝒜H(p){\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}, similar to the viewpoint that the ordinary Floer cohomology is formally the Morse cohomology of the symplectic action functional.

Now we review the definition of the equivariant Floer cohomology provided in[10]. Assume thatH(p)H^{(p)}is nondegenerate. Choose a compatible almost complex structureJJ. Given a pair of capped orbitsy¯±∈𝒪~​(H(p))\overline{y}_{\pm}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})and a pair of critical pointsw±∈crit​(g)⊂S∞w_{\pm}\in{\rm crit}(g)\subset S^{\infty}, consider the moduli space of pairsu:ℝ×S1→M,w:ℝ→S∞u:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to M,\ w:{\mathbb{R}}\to S^{\infty}

whereuuis a Floer cylinder for the HamiltonianH(p)H^{(p)}which connectsy¯±\overline{y}_{\pm}andwwis a gradient line ofggwhich connectsw±w_{\pm}. Letℳw−,w+​(y¯−,y¯+){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(\overline{y}_{-},\overline{y}_{+})(2.12)

be the moduli space of such pairs modulosimultaneous translation. Notice that the disjoint union over all possible asymptotic limits has a freeℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Similar to the case of quantum Steenrod operation, this is the reason why equivariant transversality is possible.

The differential of the equivariant Floer complex is defined via virtual counts of moduli spaces (2.12). First, one can compactify the moduli space by allowing breaking and sphere bubbles. The disjoint union of compactifications still carries a freeℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Then the FOP perturbation method allows us to define integer, hence modppcounts of the moduli spaces (2.12) which are invariant under the action. One considers the cochain group{∑iaiy¯i⊗wi|ai∈𝔽p,y¯i∈𝒪~​(H(p)),wi∈crit​(g)infi𝒜H(p)​(y¯i)>−∞,limi→∞(g​(wi)+𝒜H(p)​(y¯i))=+∞}≅CF(H(p))⊗^CM(g)\left\{\sum_{i}a_{i}\overline{y}_{i}\otimes w_{i}\ \left|\begin{array}[]{l}a_{i}\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}},\ \overline{y}_{i}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)}),w_{i}\in{\rm crit}(g)\\
\displaystyle\inf_{i}{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\overline{y}_{i})>-\infty,\\
\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\big(g(w_{i})+{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\overline{y}_{i})\big)=+\infty\end{array}\right.\right\}\cong CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)

which is a certain completion of the tensor productC​F​(H(p))⊗C​M​(g)CF(H^{(p)})\otimes CM(g). It has aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant module structure overΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}where the formal variablettacts via theℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-action onS∞S^{\infty}. Then the counts give aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant,Λ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-linear mapdℤ/p:C​F​(H(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​F​(H(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g).d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g).

Theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part is a subcomplex, denoted byC​Fℤ/p​(H(p)):=(C​F​(H(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g))Inv.CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}):=\left(CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\right)^{\rm Inv}.(2.13)

Its cohomology is theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant Floer cohomology ofH(p)H^{(p)}, denoted byH​Fℤ/p​(H(p))HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}).A prioriit is a module overΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}. By coupling with Y-shaped gradient trees inS∞S^{\infty}, one can define a canonicalΛℛpΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}-module structure on the cohomology.

On the other hand, one can also define equivariant continuation maps for two different sets of choices. In particular, for two different choices of HamiltoniansH1H_{1}andH2H_{2}, one can also build a continuation map fromC​Fℤ/p​(H1(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{1}^{(p)})toC​Fℤ/p​(H2(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{2}^{(p)})and prove that it is a chain homotopy equivalence. We summarize the relevant results in[10]below.

## Theorem 2.12.

([10, Theorem I])
Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a compact symplectic manifold.
- (1)

LetHHbe a11-periodic Hamiltonian such thatH(p)H^{(p)}is nondegenerate. Then there exists aℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-graded cochain complex of modules overΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}, called theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-equivariant Floer cochain complex,(C​Fℤ/p​(H(p)),dℤ/p)(CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}),d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})

with underlying spaceC​Fℤ/p​(H(p))=(C​F​(H(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g))InvCF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})=\Big(CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\Big)^{\rm Inv}

wheredℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}depends on anω\omega-compatible almost complex structureJJand a system of choices. Moreover, its cohomology has a well-definedΛℛpΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}-module structure which extends theΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-module structure888The proof inloc.cit.establishes theΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-module structure in the Morse setting, but the same argument applies to the Floer setting..
- (2)

For HamiltoniansH1,H2H_{1},H_{2}with bothH1(p)H_{1}^{(p)}andH2(p)H_{2}^{(p)}nondegenerate, there is aΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-linear chain homotopy equivalence(C​Fℤ/p​(H1(p)),dℤ/p)→(C​Fℤ/p​(H2(p)),dℤ/p)(CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{1}^{(p)}),d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})\to(CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{2}^{(p)}),d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})

(called an equivariant continuation map) whose homotopy class does not depend on any choices. Moreover, the resulting map on cohomology is linear overℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}. Therefore, there is a well-defined invariant ofMM, called theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant Floer cohomology, denoted byH​Fℤ/p​(M;Λ𝒦0,pΓ).HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M;\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}).
- (3)

For any Morse complexC​M​(f)CM(f)onMMand a HamiltonianHHsuch thatH(p)H^{(p)}is nondegenerate, there existΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-linear cochain maps𝑃𝑆𝑆ℤ/p:C​M​(f)⊗Λ𝒦0,pΓ⊗E​(θ)→C​Fℤ/p​(H(p)){\it PSS}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:CM(f)\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}\otimes E(\theta)\to CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)})

and𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p:C​Fℤ/p​(H(p))→C​M​(f)⊗ΛℛpΓ{\it SSP}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})\to CM(f)\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}

(the equivariant PSS and SSP maps) whose homotopy classes are well-defined. Moreover, the induced maps on cohomology are isomorphisms as gradedΛℛpΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}-modules. In particular,H​Fℤ/p​(M)≅H​(M)⊗ΛℛpΓ.HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(M)\cong H(M)\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}.
- (4)

(Notice that the equivariant PSS and SSP maps are not necessarily inverses to each other.) The difference𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p∘𝑃𝑆𝑆ℤ/p−Id:H​(M)⊗ΛℛpΓ→H​(M)⊗ΛℛpΓ.{\it SSP}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}\circ{\it PSS}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}-{\rm Id}:H(M)\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}\to H(M)\otimes\Lambda_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}.

strictly increases either the energy filtration or the(t,θ)(t,\theta)-grading.

The above formulation of the equivariant Floer cochain complex looks different from the usual description, for example, in[45][41]. We give a reformulation of the complex. We focus on thep>2p>2case. Note that the cohomological grading onC​M​(g)CM(g)induces an identification of graded module overΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}C​Fℤ/p​(H(p))≅C​F​(H(p))⊗ℛp≅(C​F​(H(p))⊕C​F​(H(p))⊗θ)⊗𝒦0,p.CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)})\cong CF(H^{(p)})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\cong\Big(CF(H^{(p)})\oplus CF(H^{(p)})\otimes\theta\Big)\otimes{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}.

Ifwk1,…,wkp∈S∞w_{k}^{1},\ldots,w_{k}^{p}\in S^{\infty}are the indexkkcritical points ofgg, then a concrete correspondence isx¯⊗tl↦∑α=1pια​(x¯)⊗w2​lα,\displaystyle\ \overline{x}\otimes t^{l}\mapsto\sum_{\alpha=1}^{p}\iota^{\alpha}(\overline{x})\otimes w_{2l}^{\alpha},\x¯⊗tl​θ↦∑α=1pια​(x¯)⊗w2​l+1α.\displaystyle\ \overline{x}\otimes t^{l}\theta\mapsto\sum_{\alpha=1}^{p}\iota^{\alpha}(\overline{x})\otimes w_{2l+1}^{\alpha}.

However, such a correspondence is not unique as only the cyclic order of(wk1,…,wkp)(w_{k}^{1},\ldots,w_{k}^{p})is well-defined. On the other hand, fixing such a correspondence, one can translate the equivariant differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}to a familiar form. Namely,dℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}istt-linear and is determined bydℤ/p​(x¯⊗1)=∑l=0∞dℤ/p2​l​(x¯)⊗tl+∑l=0∞dℤ/p2​l+1​(x¯)⊗tl​θ=dℤ/p0​(x¯)⊗1+dℤ/p1​(x¯)⊗θ+higher​order​terms,dℤ/p​(x¯⊗θ)=∑l=0∞dℤ/p2′l​(x¯)⊗tl​θ+∑l=0∞dℤ/p2′l+1​(x¯)⊗tl+1=dℤ/p0′​(x¯)⊗θ+higher​order​terms\begin{split}d_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(\overline{x}\otimes 1)=&\ \sum_{l=0}^{\infty}d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{2l}(\overline{x})\otimes t^{l}+\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{2l+1}(\overline{x})\otimes t^{l}\theta\\
=&\ d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{0}(\overline{x})\otimes 1+d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{1}(\overline{x})\otimes\theta+{\rm higher\ order\ terms},\\

=&\ d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{{}^{\prime}0}(\overline{x})\otimes\theta+{\rm higher\ order\ terms}\end{split}

where “higher order terms” are measured in terms of(t,θ)(t,\theta)-degrees. Thendℤ/p0d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{0}anddℤ/p0′d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{{}^{\prime}0}are both differentials on the ordinary Floer cochain groupC​F​(H(p))CF(H^{(p)}). In[10]the following additional features ofdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}are proved.

given some intervalI=(a,b)⊂ℝI=(a,b)\subset{\mathbb{R}}, one similarly has the quotient complexC​Fℤ/p​(H(p))I:=C​Fℤ/p​(H(p))>a/C​Fℤ/p​(H(p))>b.CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})^{I}:=CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})^{>a}/CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})^{>b}.
