and the differential is theΛ𝒦pΓ\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}_{p}}^{\Gamma}-linear extension of the equivariant Floer differential. We denote the resulting cohomology group byH​F^ℤ/p​(H(p);Λ𝒦pΓ).\widehat{HF}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)};\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}}_{p}}^{\Gamma}).

## Remark 2.14.

In the weakly monotone setting, if we use geometric perturbations to construct the equivariant Floer cochain complex, due to the infinite dimensionality ofS∞S^{\infty}, in order to achieve transversality, the resulting equivariant Floer differential may no longer respect the action filtration (induced fromH(p)H^{(p)}) because of the “largeness” of the perturbations. In the monotone or symplectically aspherical setting, this issue can be resolved by either using perturbations ofJJor using the polynomial dependence ofdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}on thettvariable as a result of the index-energy relation. In the strictly weakly monotone setting, Sugimoto[51]introduced the notions ofXKX_{K}- andX∞X_{\infty}-modules to transfer the equivariant differentials defined over finite approximationsS2​K+1S^{2K+1}ofS∞S^{\infty}that respect the action filtrations successively via algebraic tools.

## 2.5.2.Local equivariant Floer cohomology

The local version of the equivariant Floer cohomology was firstly defined in[45]using classical methods; this is possible as the local consideration does not involve sphere bubbling. However, we will define the local version of equivariant Floer cohomology using abstract perturbations which fits into the construction of[10]as well as our homological perturbation given later in Proposition3.3. Letx∈𝒪​(H)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H)be an isolated 1-periodic orbit such thatx(p)∈𝒪​(H(p))x^{(p)}\in{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})is also isolated. Choose a small perturbationH1H_{1}ofHHsuch thatH1(p)H_{1}^{(p)}is also nondegenerate. Then by Theorem2.12, one can define theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant differentialdℤ/p:C​F​(H1(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​F​(H1(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g)d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:CF(H_{1}^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF(H_{1}^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)

whoseℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part is the complexC​Fℤ/p​(H1(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{1}^{(p)}). Moreover, one can identify those “small” differentials among orbits which are close tox(p)x^{(p)}, giving rise todℤ/ploc:C​Floc​(H1(p),x(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​Floc​(H1(p),x(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g).d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\rm loc}:CF^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},x^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},x^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g).

Itsℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part is then a complexC​Fℤ/ploc​(H1(p),x(p)).CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},x^{(p)}).

One can use the continuation map argument to prove (which we omit) that the chain homotopy type is independent of perturbations and the choice of the small perturbationH1H_{1}. Hence the cohomology, denoted byH​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x(p))HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},x^{(p)})

is a well-defined gradedΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}-module. Moreover, there is a well-definedℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module structure whose structural coefficients are counts of moduli spaces coupling Y-shaped gradient trees inS∞S^{\infty}.

We compare our definition with the definition of[45]using perturbations (which also needs choosing a nondegenerate perturbationH1H_{1}).

## Lemma 2.15.

The above definition ofH​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x¯(p))HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}^{(p)})agrees with that of[45]. More precisely, ifC​Fℤ/ploc​(H1(p),x¯(p))SZCF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},\overline{x}^{(p)})_{\rm SZ}denotes the complex defined in[45], then there exists a𝒦0,p{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}-linear chain homotopy equivalenceCFℤ/ploc(H1(p),x¯)(p)SZ→CFℤ/ploc(H1(p),x¯)(p)CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)})_{\rm SZ}\to CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)})

which induces an isomorphism ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules999Theℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module structure was claimed in[45]without explicit construction.HFℤ/ploc(H(p),x¯)(p)SZ≅HFℤ/ploc(H(p),x¯)(p).HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)})_{\rm SZ}\cong HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)}).

## Sketch of proof.

The idea is the same as the comparison in the nonequivariant case as discussed in[10, Theorem G]. At the level of moduli spaces, the construction in[45]differs from[10]in that the former achieves transversality by choosing a certain family of time-dependent almost complex structures parametrized byS∞S^{\infty}while the latter constructs the moduli spaces using a fixed almost complex structure, followed by abstract perturbations on Kuranishi charts of these moduli spaces. Nevertheless, the global Kuranishi chart construction in[10, Section 25]applies equally well to the former situation. Then, following the argument in[10, Theorem G], one sees that the induced Kuranishi sections on the derived orbifold charts are already FOP transverse. By passing to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-invariant part of the resulting cochain complex, one can directly identify the differential with the formulas in[45, Section 6.1]. Therefore, we have shown that the equivariant cochain complex produced from the procedure in[10]for this varying family of almost complex structures strictly agrees withCFℤ/ploc(H1(p),x¯)(p)SZCF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H_{1}^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)})_{\rm SZ}. Finally, the desired chain map is obtained by a continuation map argument, which is a chain homotopy equivalence because we can also use continuation maps to construct a chain homotopy inverse.
∎

In general the local equivariant Floer cohomology need not be a freeℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module. However, under certain local conditions it has a simple structure. Recall that a fixed pointxxofϕ\phiis calledpp-admissibleif for each eigenvalueλ≠1\lambda\neq 1ofd​ϕxd\phi_{x},λp≠1\lambda^{p}\neq 1. In other words,d​ϕxd\phi_{x}andd​ϕxpd\phi_{x}^{p}have the same generalized eigenspace for the eigenvalue 1.

## Proposition 2.16.

Whenxxispp-admissible and an isolated fixed point ofϕp\phi^{p}, there is an isomorphism ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modulesH​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x(p))≅H​Floc​(H(p),x(p))⊗ℛp.HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},x^{(p)})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},x^{(p)})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.(2.16)

## Proof.

The proof contains several steps. First, one uses a certain invariance property of the local (equivariant) Floer cohomology to reduce the consideration to certain normal forms of the germ of the Hamiltonian. Second, the normal form can be further deformed to a situation where theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-actions on the moduli spaces are all trivial.

We first consider the invariance property of the local (equivariant) Floer cohomology we will need. Letϕs\phi_{s},s∈[0,1]s\in[0,1]be a continuous family of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms andx∈Fix​(ϕs)x\in{\rm Fix}(\phi_{s})for allss. We say thatxxisuniformly isolatedfor the familyϕs\phi_{s}if there exists a fixed open neighborhoodU⊂MU\subset Mofxxsuch that for allss,Fix​(ϕs)∩U={x}{\rm Fix}(\phi_{s})\cap U=\{x\}.

Now we follow a local argument of Ginzburg–Gurel[21]. Letxxbe an isolated andpp-admissible fixed point ofϕ\phi. LetX0⊂Tx​MX_{0}\subset T_{x}Mbe the generalized eigenspace ofd​ϕxd\phi_{x}for eigenvalue11andX1⊂Tx​MX_{1}\subset T_{x}Mbe the direct sum of other generalized eigenspaces. BothX0X_{0}andX1X_{1}are symplectic subspaces and one can identify a neighborhood ofxxwith an open ball ofX0⊕X1X_{0}\oplus X_{1}. Then by the argument of[21, Section 4.5], one can construct a family of Hamiltonian diffeomorphismsϕs\phi_{s}defined in such a neighborhood satisfying
- (1)

ϕ0=ϕ\phi_{0}=\phi.
- (2)

xxis uniformly isolated for both the familyϕs\phi_{s}and the familyϕsp\phi_{s}^{p}.
- (3)

d​ϕsd\phi_{s}remains constant atxx.
- (4)

ϕ1\phi_{1}is the product of Hamiltonian diffeomorphismsψ0\psi_{0}onX0X_{0}andψ1\psi_{1}onX1X_{1}such that0∈X00\in X_{0}is totally degenerate forψ0\psi_{0}and0∈X10\in X_{1}is nondegenerate forψ1\psi_{1}. Moreover,ψ0\psi_{0}is autonomous, generated by a smooth functionf0:X0→ℝf_{0}:X_{0}\to{\mathbb{R}}.

Then by the persistence of local (equivariant) Floer cohomology, one can reduce the proof to the case ofϕ1\phi_{1}which has the split form. This is the situation where equivariant transversality can be achieved by using a constant almost complex structure. Hence one can see thatC​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ1,x)≅C​Fℤ/ploc​(ψ0×ψ1,(0,0))≅C​Floc​(ψ0×ψ1,(0,0))⊗ℛp.CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{1},x)\cong CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\psi_{0}\times\psi_{1},(0,0))\cong CF^{\rm loc}(\psi_{0}\times\psi_{1},(0,0))\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

One also does not need to perturb the Floer equation to achieve transversality for the moduli spaces defining theℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-module structure on the cohomology. Hence one has the isomorphism (2.16) asℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules.
∎

## 2.5.3.Local equivariant Floer cohomology using Hamiltonian perturbations

When(M,ω)(M,\omega)is weakly monotone, the equivariant Floer cochain complex can be constructed using geometric perturbations. Though we will not need such a construction in its full generality, we will use it for the discussion of local equivariant Floer cohomology.

Fix a HamiltonianHHsuch thatH(p)H^{(p)}is nondegenerate. Now we consider a perturbationℋe​q:S∞×S1×M→ℝ{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}:S^{\infty}\times S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}}

satisfying the following conditions.
- (1)

For eachv∈S∞v\in S^{\infty},ℋe​q​(v,⋅,⋅){\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(v,\cdot,\cdot)is sufficiently close toH(p)H^{(p)}and agrees withH(p)H^{(p)}near all 1-periodic orbits ofH(p)H^{(p)}such that for anyv∈S∞v\in S^{\infty}, the Hamiltonianℋe​q​(v,⋅,⋅){\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(v,\cdot,\cdot)has the same set of capped 1-periodic orbits as that ofH(p)H^{(p)}.
- (2)

Letσ∈ℤ/p\sigma\in{\mathbb{Z}/p}be the generator. Thenℋe​q​(σ​v,t+1p,x)=ℋe​q​(v,t,x).{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}\Big(\sigma v,t+\frac{1}{p},x\Big)={\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(v,t,x).
- (3)

Letτ:S∞→S∞\tau:S^{\infty}\to S^{\infty}be the shifting map. Thenℋe​q​(τ​v,t,x)=ℋe​q​(v,t,x).{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(\tau v,t,x)={\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(v,t,x).

Then for each pairy¯±∈𝒪~​(H(p))\overline{y}_{\pm}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})andw±∈crit​(g)⊂S∞w_{\pm}\in{\rm crit}(g)\subset S^{\infty}, consider the equation on pairs(u,w)(u,w)solving∂su+J​(∂tu−Xℋe​q​(w​(s),t)​(u))=0,\displaystyle\ \partial_{s}u+J(\partial_{t}u-X_{{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}(w(s),t)}(u))=0,\w′​(s)+∇g​(w​(s))=0.\displaystyle\ w^{\prime}(s)+\nabla g(w(s))=0.

The equation is obviously translation invariant. Moreover, finite energy solutions converge to 1-periodic orbits ofH(p)H^{(p)}as well as critical points ofgg. Then one can again define the moduli spaceℳw−,w+​(y¯−,y¯+;ℋe​q){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(\overline{y}_{-},\overline{y}_{+};{\mathscr{H}}^{eq})

of solutions with the prescribed asymptotic limits modulo translation.

Transversality can be achieved by taking genericℋe​q{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}while maintaining the required symmetry. The only problem is the issue of bubbling off spheres, which obstructs the construction beyond the weakly monotone case. If we assume(M,ω)(M,\omega)is weakly monotone, then moduli spaces of expected dimension zero consist of finitely many points and moduli spaces of expected dimension one can be compactified by adding once-broken trajectories. Hence one can define modppcount of these moduli spaces, which eventually lead to the construction of the equivariant Floer cochain complexC​Fℤ/p​(H(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}).

Following Lemma2.15, one can similarly show that in the weakly monotone setting, the above construction leads to equivariant Floer cochain complexes which are chain homotopy equivalent to the general version we use from[10].

The perturbative approach is only used in this paper when discussing the local equivariant Floer theory. Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold andHHbe a 1-periodic Hamiltonian with an isolated 1-periodic orbitxxsuch that itspp-th iterationx(p)x^{(p)}is isolated in𝒪​(H(p)){\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)}). Choose a sufficiently small perturbationH1H_{1}ofHHsuch thatH1(p)H_{1}^{(p)}is nondegenerate. ThenC​Fℤ/p​(H1(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{1}^{(p)})can be defined as described above. Moreover, one can identify a “local” differential which restricts to a differential of the subspace generated by orbits nearx(p)x^{(p)}, giving the definition of the local equivariant Floer cochain complex, denoted byC​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},x^{(p)})

## 2.6.The equivariant pair-of-pants

Next, we recall the construction of the
equivariant pair-of-pants product following[42,45], and the generalization in[10]. As in the previous discussion of the quantum Steenrod power operation, we first describe the relevant moduli spaces without discussing details of the perturbation.

Recall we used the domainΣp\Sigma_{p}, which branches overS2S^{2}, as the domain of the quantum Steenrod operation. LetΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}be the complement of thep+1p+1points atpp-th roots of unity and∞\infty, which branches overℂ∗≅ℝ×S1{\mathbb{C}}^{*}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}. ThenΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}still carries aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Moreover, the branched coverΣp∘→ℝ×S1\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}given byz↦zpz\mapsto z^{p}pulls backppnegative cylindrical ends and one positive cylindrical end. LetHHbe a Hamiltonian on(M,ω)(M,\omega)such that bothHHandH(p)H^{(p)}are nondegenerate. Then the flat Hamiltonian connectionH​d​tHdtonℝ×S1{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}is pulled back via the coveringΣp∘→ℝ×S1\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}to a Hamiltonian connectionσH\sigma_{H}onΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}which isH​d​tHdtat each negative end andH(p)​d​tH^{(p)}dtat the positive end. Choose a compatible almost complex structureJJ. For each tuple(x1,…,xp)(x_{1},\ldots,x_{p})of 1-periodic orbits ofHHandy∞∈𝒪​(H(p))y_{\infty}\in{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)}), one can consider the Floer equation(∇σHu)0,1=0,u:Σp∘→M(\nabla^{\sigma_{H}}u)^{0,1}=0,\ u:\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to M

such thatuuis asymptotic toxjx_{j}at thejj-th negative end and asymptotic toy∞y_{\infty}at the positive end. Here∇σH\nabla^{\sigma_{H}}is the covariant derivative associated to the Hamiltonian connectionσH\sigma_{H}. By fixing cappingsx¯1,…,x¯p\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p}andy¯∞\overline{y}_{\infty}one can further restrict the homotopy type of solutionsuu.

The equivariant pair-of-pants product is, roughly speaking, defined byℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant virtual counts of the above moduli spaces. To achieve equivariant transversality, one couples the above equation with parametrized gradient lines inS∞S^{\infty}. For each tuple(x¯1,…,x¯p;y¯∞)(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p};\overline{y}_{\infty})and a pair of critical pointsw±∈crit​(g)⊂S∞w_{\pm}\in{\rm crit}(g)\subset S^{\infty}, letℳw−,w+​(x¯1,…,x¯p;y¯∞){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p};\overline{y}_{\infty})

be the moduli space of pairs(u,w)(u,w)whereu:Σ→Mu:\Sigma\to Mis a solution to the above equation with asymptotic datax¯1,…,x¯p;y¯∞\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p};\overline{y}_{\infty}at cylindrical ends andwwis a gradient flow line inS∞S^{\infty}connectingw−w_{-}andw+w_{+}. One compactifies the moduli space by adding broken and bubbled configurations. Here we consider breakings at negative and positive ends in different ways. At negative ends, the breakings at theppcylindrical ends ofΣ\Sigmaand the negative end of the domaindom​(w)=ℝ{\rm dom}(w)={\mathbb{R}}are treated independently. On the other hand, the breaking at the positive end ofΣ\Sigmaand the positive end of the domain ofwwis regarded assimultaneous. In other words, breakings at negative ends need to take quotient by the translation ofℝp+1{\mathbb{R}}^{p+1}while the breakings at positive ends are quotiented by a singleℝ{\mathbb{R}}.

By using the FOP perturbation method, one can define integral, hence𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-valued counts of the compactified moduli spaces. As theℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action is free, one can choose such perturbations equivariantly. These counts can be assembled to chain mapsC​F​(H;ΛΓ)⊗p​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​F​(H(p);ΛΓ)⊗C​M​(g).CF(H;\Lambda^{\Gamma})^{\otimes p}\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF(H^{(p)};\Lambda^{\Gamma})\otimes CM(g).

We summarize the algebraic consequences in the following theorem.

The first important property is the equivalence between the Floer-theoretic quantum Steenrod operation and the quantum Steenrod operation. In[10]the following theorem is proved. Remember that the ordinary and the equivariant Floer cohomology are both invariants of the symplectic manifold.

which is a chain homotopy equivalence.

For any primeppand any HamiltonianHHon(M,ω)(M,\omega)such that bothHHandH(p)H^{(p)}are nondegenerate, there holdsβtot​(H(p))≥p⋅βtot​(H).\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}(H^{(p)})\geq p\cdot\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}(H).(2.20)

in the formd^ℤ/p=[dH(p)Sp0dH(p)]+t​K\widehat{d}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}d_{H^{(p)}}&S_{p}\\
0&d_{H^{(p)}}\end{array}\right]+tK

whereKKdoes not decrease the(t,θ)(t,\theta)-degree. The leading2×22\times 2matrix is the cone ofSpS_{p}, whose differential is denoted temporarily bydConed_{\rm Cone}. Letx∈𝒪​(H)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H)be an isolated orbit such thatx(p)∈𝒪​(H(p))x^{(p)}\in{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})is also isolated. by energy consideration, whose restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part gives𝐹𝑆𝑡~:p,xlocCFloc(H1,x)ℤ/p⊗p→CFℤ/ploc(H1(p),x(p)).\widetilde{{\it FSt}}{}_{p,x}^{\rm loc}:CF^{{\rm loc}}(H_{1},x)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\to CF^{\rm loc}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H_{1}^{(p)},x^{(p)}).

## 2.6.2.Local version using Hamiltonian perturbations

For the purpose of this paper, we also need to review the construction using classical methods for the weakly monotone case as well as the local version of the equivariant pants product. This will be used in Section5.4to compute the equivariant pants product in local Floer cohomology. We note that one can also use the approach in[45]to perturbJJinstead, and the upshot on cohomology is evidently equivalent.

Consider thepp-fold coveringz↦zpz\mapsto z^{p}fromS2S^{2}toS2S^{2}branched over0and∞\infty. LetS~\tilde{S}be the domain of the branched covering with∞\inftyas well as the preimages of11removed. Thenℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}acts freely onS~\tilde{S}which cyclically permutes thepp-th roots of unityz1,…,zpz_{1},\ldots,z_{p}. Choose cylindrical ends at thepp-th roots of unity as well as∞\inftyin aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant fashion. Choose also aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant Hamiltonian connectionσ\sigmaonS~\tilde{S}which is asymptotic toH​d​tHdton each of theppnegative ends and asymptotic toH(p)​d​tH^{(p)}dton the positive end. Then the equivariant pair-of-pants product is defined, roughly speaking, by counting solutions to the Floer equation onS~\tilde{S}with such a Hamiltonian perturbation, with suitable further perturbations to achieve transversality. More precisely, consider pairs(u,ζ)(u,\zeta)whereu:S~→M,(∇σu)0,1=0,\displaystyle\ u:\tilde{S}\to M,\ (\nabla^{\sigma}u)^{0,1}=0,\ζ:ℝ→S∞,ζ′​(s)=∇g​(ζ​(s)).\displaystyle\ \zeta:{\mathbb{R}}\to S^{\infty},\ \zeta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla g(\zeta(s)).

The chain-level equivariant pair-of-pants product should be defined via appropriately counting solutions to the above equation.

To achieve equivariant transversality, we choose to perturb the Hamiltonian connection which is allowed to vary with a variablev∈S∞v\in S^{\infty}. First, choose a family of Hamiltoniansℋve​q:S1×M→ℝ,v∈S∞{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}_{v}:S^{1}\times M\to{\mathbb{R}},\ v\in S^{\infty}

which can be used to define the equivariant Floer complexC​Fℤ/p​(H(p))CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}). Then choose a family of Hamiltonian connectionsσve​q,v∈S∞\sigma_{v}^{eq},\ v\in S^{\infty}

onS~\tilde{S}satisfying the following conditions.
- (1)

Its restriction to the positive end coincides withℋve​q​d​t{\mathscr{H}}^{eq}_{v}dt.
- (2)

Its restriction to each negative end coincides withH​d​tHdt.
- (3)

It is invariant under theℤ/p×ℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}/p}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-action.

Then for any given tuple of capped orbitsx¯1,…,x¯p∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H),y¯∈𝒪~​(H(p))\overline{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})and critical pointsw±∈crit​gw_{\pm}\in{\rm crit}g, one considers the moduli spaceℳw−,w+​(x¯1,…,x¯p;y¯){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(\overline{x}_{1},\ldots,\overline{x}_{p};\overline{y})

of solutions(u,ζ)(u,\zeta)to the equation(∇σζ​(0)e​qu)0,1=0,\displaystyle\ (\nabla^{\sigma_{\zeta(0)}^{eq}}u)^{0,1}=0,\ζ′​(s)=∇g​(ζ​(s))\displaystyle\ \zeta^{\prime}(s)=\nabla g(\zeta(s))

such thatuuis asymptotic tox¯k\overline{x}_{k}along thekk-th negative end and asymptotic toy¯\overline{y}along the positive end, and such thatζ​(s)\zeta(s)converges tow±w_{\pm}at±∞\pm\infty. Notice that the disjoint union of moduli spaces over all combinations of asymptotic data has a natural freeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Therefore, by choosing a genericσve​q\sigma_{v}^{eq}, one can achieveℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant transversality. Then under the weakly monotone assumption, when the expected dimension of the moduli space is zero, one obtains a modppcount. When the expected dimension of the moduli space is one, it can be compactified by adding configurations with at most one breaking; notice that the breaking at the positive end should be regarded as simultaneously breaking both a Floer trajectory and a gradient line inS∞S^{\infty}. Therefore, one obtains a linear mapF​S​t~~p:C​F​(H)⊗p​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​F​(H(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g)\widetilde{\widetilde{FSt}}_{p}:CF(H)^{\otimes p}\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF(H^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)

which isℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant. Here⊗^\widehat{\otimes}is a completion of the ordinary tensor product so both sides are modules overΛ𝒦0,pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}. The restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part is then a chain mapF​S​t~p:C​F​(H)ℤ/p⊗p→C​Fℤ/p​(H(p)).\widetilde{FSt}_{p}:CF(H)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\to CF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}).

Composing with the quasi-Frobenius map, it induces the𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-linear map on cohomologyF​S​tp:H​F​(H)→H​Fℤ/p​(H(p);Λ𝒦0,pΓ).FSt_{p}:HF(H)\to HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)};\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}).

It was proved in[10]that in the weakly monotone case, the two definitions agree.

The approach using geometric perturbations in particular works for the construction of the local version of the equivariant pants product for isolated fixed points. Consider a general closed symplectic manifold(M,ω)(M,\omega), a HamiltonianHHwith an isolated capped orbitx∈𝒪~​(H)x\in\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)such thatx(p)x^{(p)}is also an isolated orbit ofH(p)H^{(p)}. LetGGbe a sufficiently small perturbation ofHH. Then the above construction gives a local pair-of-pants operation𝐹𝑆𝑡p,xloc:H​Floc​(H,x)→H​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x(p)).{\it FSt}^{{\rm loc}}_{p,x}:HF^{\rm loc}(H,x)\to HF^{{\rm loc}}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)},x^{(p)}).

## 3.Homological perturbations for degenerate Hamiltonians

In this section, we provide preliminary technical results which will be used in the proofs of results about pseudo-rotations and periodic points of Hamiltonian diffeomorphisms.

## 3.1.Hamiltonians with isolated fixed points

First, we use homological perturbation techniques to extend our quantitative discussion of Floer theory to degenerate Hamiltonians with isolated fixed points.

To state the main constructions, we first equip the local Floer cohomology with a natural energy filtration. LetHHbe a 1-periodic Hamiltonian andx∈𝒪​(H)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H)be an isolated contractible 1-periodic orbit. Let𝕂{\mathbb{K}}be any ground field. Recall that to construct the local Floer cohomologyH​Floc​(H,x;Λ𝕂Γ)HF^{\rm loc}(H,x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}), one chooses a small nondegenerate perturbationH1H_{1}ofHH. Then one can write down the Floer cochain groupC​F​(H1;Λ𝕂Γ)CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})which contains aΛ𝕂Γ\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}-submoduleC​Floc​(H1,x;Λ𝕂Γ)⊂C​F​(H1;Λ𝕂Γ)CF^{\rm loc}(H_{1},x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})\subset CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})

generated by nondegenerate elements in𝒪​(H1){\mathcal{O}}(H_{1})which are close toxx. ifx¯′\overline{x}^{\prime}is a capped 1-periodic orbit ofH1H_{1}which is close to a cappingx¯\overline{x}ofxx. Notice that this does not change the convergence condition required for chains inC​Floc​(H1,x;Λ𝕂Γ)CF^{\rm loc}(H_{1},x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma}).

Then the Floer differentialdH1d_{H_{1}}onC​F​(H1;Λ𝕂Γ)CF(H_{1};\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})(given by Theorem2.1) restricts to a local differentialdlocd^{\rm loc}onC​Floc​(H1,x;Λ𝕂Γ)CF^{\rm loc}(H_{1},x;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})which preserves this modified filtration. We extend[46, Theorem D]to the general setting and prove it in AppendixB.

## Corollary 3.6.

Under the hypothesis of Proposition3.3, suppose in addition that the time-1 mapϕ\phiofHHsatisfiesFixc​(ϕ)=Fixc​(ϕp),\displaystyle\ {\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)={\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p}),\N​(ϕ;𝔽p)=N​(ϕp;𝔽p)=dim𝔽p​H∗​(M;𝔽p).\displaystyle\ N(\phi;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})=N(\phi^{p};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})={\rm dim}_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p}).

Indeed, ifdℤ/p≠0d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\neq 0, then the Tate versionC^ℤ/p​(H(p))\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)})has a nontrivial differential. Asdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(which is also the differential ofC^ℤ/p​(H(p))\widehat{C}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)})) strictly increases the filtration, the Tate bar-length spectrum has a nonzero elementβ^1​(H(p))\widehat{\beta}_{1}(H^{(p)}). By Corollary3.5, one hasβ^1​(H(p))=p​β1​(H)>0\widehat{\beta}_{1}(H^{(p)})=p\beta_{1}(H)>0. However, this contradicts Corollary3.2.
∎

On the other hand, one has a comparison on the shortest bar-lengths.

## 4.Proofs of the main results

Using the technical results presented above, we can prove our main results on Hamiltonian dynamics.

## 4.1.The divisibility lemma

For our applications, we require the following key lemma. We first recall the homological perturbation construction in the context of Proposition3.3and Proposition3.4. For an isolatedx∈𝒪​(H)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H)withx(p)∈𝒪​(H(p))x^{(p)}\in{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})also being isolated, choosing a cappingx¯\overline{x}, one has the local equivariant pants productP~x¯loc:HFloc(H,x¯)⊗p→HFℤ/ploc(H(p),x¯)(p).\widetilde{P}_{\overline{x}}^{\rm loc}:HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{x})^{\otimes p}\to HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)}).

## Lemma 4.1(The divisibility lemma).

Under the assumption of Proposition3.1,3.3,3.4, suppose in addition thatxxispp-admissible. Hence by Proposition2.16, one hasH​Fℤ/ploc​(H(p),x¯(p))≅H​Floc​(H(p),x¯(p))⊗ℛp.HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}^{(p)})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}^{(p)})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Then the restriction of the local equivariant pants productP~loc\widetilde{P}^{\rm loc}atx¯\overline{x}given by Proposition3.4has a local decomposition written asP~x¯loc=∑2​k≥0tk​P~2​kloc+∑2​k+1≥1tk​θ​P~2​k+1loc\widetilde{P}_{\overline{x}}^{\rm loc}=\sum_{2k\geq 0}t^{k}\widetilde{P}^{\rm loc}_{2k}+\sum_{2k+1\geq 1}t^{k}\theta\widetilde{P}^{\rm loc}_{2k+1}

whereP~iloc:HFloc(H,x¯)⊗p→HFloc(H(p),x¯)(p)\widetilde{P}_{i}^{\rm loc}:HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{x})^{\otimes p}\to HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}{}^{(p)})

are the components. Then for anyy∈H​Floc​(H,x¯)y\in HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{x})withP~loc​(y⊗⋯⊗y)≠0\widetilde{P}^{\rm loc}(y\otimes\cdots\otimes y)\neq 0, one hasinf{i|P~iloc​(y⊗⋯⊗y)≠0}≤2​n​(p−1).\inf\Big\{i\ |\ \widetilde{P}_{i}^{\rm loc}(y\otimes\cdots\otimes y)\neq 0\Big\}\leq 2n(p-1).

Since the proof is rather long and technical, we first outline the strategy. The key idea is to reduce the consideration to a local normal form of the fixed point also used in the proof of Proposition2.16. For different reasons, the divisibility condition is true for both the nondegenerate direction and the totally degenerate direction. One then uses the Kunneth property of the local equivariant pants product established in Section5to obtain the product case. Another key property, which is easily neglected, is the fact that the local equivariant Kunneth map is bilinear over the ringℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}(in particular, linear over the odd variableθ\theta) proved as Proposition5.4.

## Proof of Lemma4.1.

We first reduce the consideration to the case with a local normal form as in the proof of Proposition2.16. Recall that the local (equivariant) Floer cohomology is invariant under deformation as long as the fixed point remains uniformly isolated through the deformation. Moreover, the identifications between different moments through the deformation are given by certain continuation maps. As the local equivariant pants product is compatible with continuation maps, one can assume thatHHhas a product type. In other words, letϕ\phibe the time-1 map ofHH. Then we may assume thatM=X1×X2M=X_{1}\times X_{2}andϕ=ϕ1×ϕ2\phi=\phi_{1}\times\phi_{2}are products. Ifx=(x1,x2)x=(x_{1},x_{2}), thenx1x_{1}is a nondegenerate fixed point ofϕ1\phi_{1}andx2x_{2}is a totally degenerate and isolated fixed point ofϕ2\phi_{2}.

To save notations, abbreviateH​Floc​(ϕ,x)HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,x)byH​Floc​(ϕ)HF^{\rm loc}(\phi)(and in other similar occasions) as the critical points in the consideration are clear from the context. LetP~xiloc:=𝐹𝑆𝑡~:p,xilocHFloc(ϕi)⊗p→HFℤ/ploc(ϕip)≅HFloc(ϕip)⊗ℛp\widetilde{P}_{x_{i}}^{\rm loc}:=\widetilde{{\it FSt}}{}_{p,x_{i}}^{\rm loc}:HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{i})^{\otimes p}\to HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{i}^{p})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{i}^{p})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}

be the local equivariant pants product and letPxiloc:H​Floc​(ϕi)→H​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕip)P_{x_{i}}^{\rm loc}:HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{i})\to HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{i}^{p})

be their compositions with the quasi-Frobenius mapy↦y⊗py\mapsto y^{\otimes p}.

The basic strategy in the rest of the proof is to use the Kunneth property of the local equivariant pants product (Proposition5.6) and the leading order bounds of the equivariant pants product on the two factors. Then by Proposition5.4and Proposition5.6proved below, the following diagram commutes up to the Koszul sign.H​Floc​(ϕ)\textstyle{HF^{\rm loc}(\phi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Ploc\scriptstyle{P^{\rm loc}}H​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕp)\textstyle{HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi^{p})}H​Floc​(ϕ1)⊗H​Floc​(ϕ2)\textstyle{HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{1})\otimes HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Px1loc⊗Px2loc\scriptstyle{P_{x_{1}}^{\rm loc}\otimes P_{x_{2}}^{\rm loc}}κloc\scriptstyle{\kappa^{\rm loc}}H​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ1p)​⊗ℛp​H​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ2p)\textstyle{HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{1}^{p})\underset{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}{\otimes}HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{2}^{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}κℤ/ploc\scriptstyle{\kappa_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}}

Notice that the non-equivariant Kunneth mapκloc\kappa^{\rm loc}is an isomorphism. On the other hand, from the proof of Proposition2.16one knows thatκℤ/ploc\kappa_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}is also an isomorphism, although it wouldn’t be true if we drop thepp-admissibility condition.

Now we prove that bothPx1locP_{x_{1}}^{\rm loc}andPx2locP_{x_{2}}^{\rm loc}satisfy the bound we would like to prove. We first analyze the totally degenerate piecePx2locP_{x_{2}}^{\rm loc}. Asx2x_{2}is alsopp-admissible, by Proposition2.16, one has an isomorphism ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modulesH​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ2p)≅H​Floc​(ϕ2p)⊗ℛp.HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi_{2}^{p})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(\phi_{2}^{p})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Claim.If we decomposePx2loc=∑2​k≥0tk​Px2,2​kloc+∑2​k+1≥1tk​θ​Px2,2​k+1loc,P_{x_{2}}^{\rm loc}=\sum_{2k\geq 0}t^{k}P_{x_{2},2k}^{\rm loc}+\sum_{2k+1\geq 1}t^{k}\theta P_{x_{2},2k+1}^{\rm loc},

thenPx2,iloc=0​when​i>2​(p−1)​n2=(p−1)​dimℝ​X2.P_{x_{2},i}^{\rm loc}=0\ {\rm when}\ i>2(p-1)n_{2}=(p-1){\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}X_{2}.

Proof of the claim.The basic idea is to reduce the consideration to local Steenrod operations on local Morse cohomology. By the argument of Proposition2.16, the local equivariant cohomology can be computed using an autonomous Hamiltonianhhand the isomorphism is provided by a continuation map. given by[43, Equation (1.6)]. On the other hand, the nondegenerate piece is an easy consequence of Theorem2.22. ## 4.2.Technical results on symplectically degenerate maxima

We need a small digression on filtered Floer cohomology of iterates of a Hamiltonian near a symplectically degenerate maximum, which will be used towards the proof of Theorem1.2, Theorem1.13, and Theorem1.17. Notice that the statement of the following lemma depends on Proposition3.1.

## Lemma 4.2.

LetHHbe a 1-periodic Hamiltonian such thatx¯∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)is a symplectically degenerate maximum (whose mean index, denoted byΔ\Delta, is necessarily an integer). Denotec=𝒜H​(x¯)c={\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\overline{x}). Then for every sufficiently smallϵ>0\epsilon>0, there exists a sufficiently large natural numberrϵ>0r_{\epsilon}>0such that for allr≥rϵr\geq r_{\epsilon}there existsδ=δϵ,r∈(0,ϵ)\delta=\delta_{\epsilon,r}\in(0,\epsilon)such that the following connecting homomorphismH​Fr​Δ+n​(H(r))(r​c+δ,r​c+ϵ)→H​Fr​Δ+n+1​(H(r))(r​c−δ,r​c+δ),HF^{r\Delta+n}(H^{(r)})^{(rc+\delta,rc+\epsilon)}\to HF^{r\Delta+n+1}(H^{(r)})^{(rc-\delta,rc+\delta)},(4.2)

associated to the short exact sequence0\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}C​(H(r))(r​c−δ,r​c+δ)\textstyle{C(H^{(r)})^{(rc-\delta,rc+\delta)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}C​(H(r))(r​c−δ,r​c+ϵ)\textstyle{C(H^{(r)})^{(rc-\delta,rc+\epsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}C​(H(r))(r​c+δ,r​c+ϵ)\textstyle{C(H^{(r)})^{(rc+\delta,rc+\epsilon)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}0\textstyle{0}

is nontrivial.

## Proof.

The assertion follows from the methods of proof of[23, Proposition 4.7],[19, Theorem 1.18], and[25, Theorem 1.3]. Indeed,[25]adapts the methods of[23]to show that on an arbitrary closed symplectically Calabi–Yau manifold, there holdsH​Fr​Δ+n+1​(H(r))(r​c+δ,r​c+ϵ)≠0.HF^{r\Delta+n+1}(H^{(r)})^{(rc+\delta,rc+\epsilon)}\neq 0.

The argument in[25]applies equally well for an arbitrary closed symplectic manifold, as it relies on a general decomposition theorem for filtered Floer homology and Ginzburg’s arguments[23]applied to the “local summand” of the decomposition.

Furthermore, Ginzburg proves that the above connecting map is non-trivial in the aspherical case, and this extends, by applying Hein’s decomposition, to the case of an arbitrary closed symplectic manifold.
∎

To continue, we also need the following lemma for our applications. When(M,ω)(M,\omega)is rational in the sense that⟨[ω],π2​(M)⟩=ρ​ℤ{\left<[\omega],\pi_{2}(M)\right>}=\rho{\mathbb{Z}}for a constantρ≥0,\rho\geq 0,Lemma4.3follows immediately from[19, Theorem 1.18]. When(M,ω)(M,\omega)is not rational, additional arguments, which use more of the techniques of symplectically degenerate maxima, are required. We present the proof below.

## Lemma 4.3.

Letϕ\phibe an𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}Hamiltonian pseudo-rotation generated by a HamiltonianHH. Then for allk≥0k\geq 0,𝒪~​(H(pk))\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p^{k})})contains no symplectically degenerate maximum.

## Proof.

Suppose on the contrary, for somek≥0k\geq 0,𝒪~​(H(pk))\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p^{k})})contains some symplectically degenerate maximum. Asϕ\phiis an𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}pseudo-rotation,ϕpk\phi^{p^{k}}is also an𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}pseudo-rotation. Hence we may assume thatk=0k=0. By Lemma4.2, ifx¯∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)is a symplectically degenerate maximum, then for every sufficiently smallϵ>0\epsilon>0, there existsrϵ∈ℤ≥0r_{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}such that for allr≥rϵr\geq r_{\epsilon}we can findδ∈(0,ϵ)\delta\in(0,\epsilon)such that the connecting homomorphism (4.2) is nonzero. However, it follows from Corollary3.2that whenr=pkr=p^{k}, the differential ofC​(H(pk))C(H^{(p^{k})}), as well as that ofC​(H(pk))(a,b)C(H^{(p^{k})})^{(a,b)}for an arbitrary window(a,b)(a,b)vanishes. Hence the connecting homomorphism (4.2) also vanishes, which is a contradiction. This finishes the proof.
∎

## Remark 4.4.

The same argument applies in the setting of Theorem1.13.

## Remark 4.5.

The proof by contradiction in the above argument generalizes to the setting of a Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ\phiwith finitely many periodic points. The argument itself does not use the interplay between quantum Steenrod operations and equivariant pants product; we therefore defer the proof of this more general version, which leads to the proof of Theorem1.17, to the end of this Section.

Letϕ\phibe an𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}Hamiltonian pseudo-rotation. Choose a HamiltonianHHthat generatesϕ\phi. Theorem1.2follows from the following proposition, whose proof resembles that in[13].

Therefore,𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)≠S​tp​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)\neq St_{p}(\mu).

>\inf_{1\leq i\leq m}p{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})=pc(\mu,H).

On the other hand, since the (Tate version) operation𝐹𝑆𝑡^p\widehat{{\it FSt}}_{p}precisely rescales the energy filtration bypp, we havep​c​(μ,H)=c^​(𝐹𝑆𝑡^p​(μ))=𝒜^H(p)​(Ploc​(μ)+Pbig​(μ))=𝒜^H(p)​(Ploc​(μ))=𝒜^H(p)​(∑i=1mPloc​(μi(1)))=inf1≤i≤m𝒜^H(p)​(Ploc​(μi(1))).pc(\mu,H)=\widehat{c}(\widehat{{\it FSt}}_{p}(\mu))=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(P^{\rm loc}(\mu)+P^{\rm big}(\mu))\\
=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(P^{\rm loc}(\mu))=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}P^{\rm loc}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})\Big)=\inf_{1\leq i\leq m}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(P^{\rm loc}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})).

As the local equivariant pair-of-pants product rescales the local action bypptimes,i∈I(1)⟹𝒜^H(p)​(Ploc​(μi(1)))=p​𝒜H​(μi(1))=p​c​(μ,H).i\in I^{(1)}\Longrightarrow\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(P^{\rm loc}(\mu_{i}^{(1)}))=p{\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})=pc(\mu,H).

On the other hand, using (4.4), one hasp​c​(μ,H)=c^​(tn​(p−1)​μℤ/p,H(p))=inf1≤i≤m𝒜^H(p)​(μℤ/p,i(p)​(t,θ)).pc(\mu,H)=\widehat{c}(t^{n(p-1)}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}},H^{(p)})=\inf_{1\leq i\leq m}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p)}(t,\theta)).(4.7)

Claim D.For eachi∉I(1)i\notin I^{(1)}, one has𝒜^H(p)​(μℤ/p,i(p)​(t,θ))>p​c​(μ,H).\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p)}(t,\theta))>pc(\mu,H).

Proof of Claim D.Choosei∉I(1)i\notin I^{(1)}. So𝒜H​(μi(1))>c​(μ,H){\mathcal{A}}_{H}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})>c(\mu,H). Notice thatμℤ/p,i(p)​(t,θ)\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p)}(t,\theta), if nonzero, either comes fromPloc​(μi(1))P^{\rm loc}(\mu_{i}^{(1)})orPbig​(μ)P_{\rm big}(\mu). Both of them have action strictly greater thanp​c​(μ,H)pc(\mu,H).End of proof of Claim D.

Now we can prove Claim C. First, Claim D and (4.7) imply thatp​c​(μ,H)=infi∈I(1)𝒜^H(p)​(μℤ/p,i(p)​(t,θ)).pc(\mu,H)=\inf_{i\in I^{(1)}}\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p)}(t,\theta)).

Now thei0i_{0}-th component of (4.3) readsPloc​(μi0(1))+[Pbig​(μ)]i0=tn​(p−1)​μℤ/p,i0(p)​(t,θ)∈H​Floc,2​n​(H(p),xi0(p))⊗ℛp.P^{\rm loc}(\mu_{i_{0}}^{(1)})+\Big[P^{\rm big}(\mu)\Big]_{i_{0}}=t^{n(p-1)}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i_{0}}^{(p)}(t,\theta)\in HF^{{\rm loc},2n}(H^{(p)},x_{i_{0}}^{(p)})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

By Claim A, this also agrees with𝒜H(p)​(μi0(p)){\mathcal{A}}_{H^{(p)}}(\mu_{i_{0}}^{(p)}). This finishes the proof of Claim C and hence the proposition.
∎

## 4.4.Proof of Theorem1.3

The proof is a combination of the proof of Theorem1.2and the corresponding result that has been established in the monotone setting[4].

## Proof of Theorem1.3.

Without loss of generality, we may assume thatG=ℤ/p′G={\mathbb{Z}}/p^{\prime}for a primep′p^{\prime}, generated byϕ∈Ham​(M,ω)\phi\in{\rm Ham}(M,\omega). We also assume by contradiction that for some primep≠p′p\neq p^{\prime},𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)=S​tp​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)=St_{p}(\mu), which can be translated to (4.3), i.e.𝐹𝑆𝑡p​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ))=tn​(p−1)​μℤ/p=tn​(p−1)​𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p−1​(μ)∈H​Fℤ/p​(M).{\it FSt}_{p}({\it PSS}(\mu))=t^{n(p-1)}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}=t^{n(p-1)}{\it SSP}^{-1}_{{\mathbb{Z}}/p}(\mu)\in HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M).

We focus on the casep≠2p\neq 2; the argument for thep=2p=2case is similar. Choose a HamiltonianHHgeneratingϕ\phi. By PropositionC.17, for allk≥0k\geq 0, one has a canonical decompositionH​F​(M;ΛΓ)=⨁ℱ∈π0​(Fixc​(ϕ))H​Floc​(H(pk),ℱ(pk);ΛΓ).HF(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma})=\bigoplus_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi))}HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p^{k})},{\mathcal{F}}^{(p^{k})};\Lambda^{\Gamma}).

Letℱ1,…,ℱm{\mathcal{F}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathcal{F}}_{m}be all the connected components ofFixc​(ϕ){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi). Then one can decompose𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ)=μ1(pk)+⋯+μm(pk)​where​μi(pk)∈H​Floc​(H(pk),ℱi(pk);ΛΓ).{\it PSS}(\mu)=\mu_{1}^{(p^{k})}+\cdots+\mu_{m}^{(p^{k})}\ {\rm where}\ \mu_{i}^{(p^{k})}\in HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p^{k})},{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{(p^{k})};\Lambda^{\Gamma}).

PropositionC.17also provides a decomposition ofμℤ/p\mu_{\mathbb{Z}/p}written asμℤ/p=∑i=1mμℤ/p,i(pk)​(t,θ),\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p^{k})}(t,\theta),

whereμℤ/p,i(pk)​(t,θ)∈H​Floc​(H(pk),ℱi(pk);ΛℛpΓ).\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},i}^{(p^{k})}(t,\theta)\in HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p^{k})},{\mathcal{F}}_{i}^{(p^{k})};\Lambda_{\mathcal{R}_{p}}^{\Gamma}).

The rest of the proof is completely parallel to that of Theorem1.2. The key is an analogue of Proposition4.6, which says that there exists a capped componentℱ¯\overline{\mathcal{F}}such that for all sufficiently largekk,HFloc,2​n(H(pk),ℱ¯;(pk)𝔽p)≠0.HF^{{\rm loc},2n}(H^{(p^{k})},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p^{k})};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\neq 0.

Notice that the divisibility lemma holds in this case as the local equivariant pair-of-pants product coincides with the classical Steenrod operation (PropositionC.13) where the roles of Proposition3.3and Proposition3.4are replaced by PropositionC.15.
∎

## 4.5.Proof of Theorrem1.5

The proof here follows from the same ideas as in the proof of[4, Theorem H]using the results in AppendixC.

## Proof of Theorrem1.5.

Let’s first consider the case whenϕpk=Id\phi^{p^{k}}=\mathrm{Id}. Using PropositionC.14and the PSS isomorphism[10, Theorem E], ifHHis the Hamiltonian generatingϕ\phi, we see that∑ℱ∈π0​(𝒪​(H)cont)dimH​Floc​(H,ℱ;Λ𝔽pΓ)≥dimH∗​(M;Λ𝔽pΓ),\sum_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(H)^{\rm{cont}})}\dim HF^{\rm{loc}}(H,{\mathcal{F}};\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}^{\Gamma})\geq\dim H^{*}(M;\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}^{\Gamma}),

where we only sum overcontractiblecomponents of the fixed points. Using PropositionC.9and the identification between local Floer cohomology under different cappings, we see that∑ℱ∈π0​(𝒪​(H)cont)dimH∗​(ℱ;𝔽p)≥dimH∗​(M;𝔽p).\sum_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(H)^{\rm{cont}})}\dim H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\geq\dim H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p}).

On the other hand, by applying the classical Smith inequality iteratively, we have∑ℱ∈π0​(𝒪​(H))dimH∗​(ℱ;𝔽p)≤dimH∗​(M;𝔽p).\sum_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(H))}\dim H^{*}({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\leq\dim H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{F}}_{p}).

If there were noncontractible components of𝒪​(H){\mathcal{O}}(H), sinceϕ\phiis Morse–Bott, the above two inequalities cannot hold simultaneously.

In general, we haveϕp1k1​⋯​plkl=Id\phi^{p_{1}^{k_{1}}\cdots p_{l}^{k_{l}}}=\rm{Id}wherep1,…,plp_{1},\dots,p_{l}are distinct prime numbers. Then, we can induct onllfollowing[4, Section 5.10]. This finishes the proof.
∎

Then by (4.8) and (4.9), one has the following equality inH​Fℤ/p​(M)HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M)c​tr​(p−1)/2​𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p−1​(u)=𝐹𝑆𝑡p​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(u))=Ploc​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(u))+Pbig​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(u))=∑x𝐹𝑆𝑡p,xloc​(ax​x¯)+Pbig​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(u))=∑xcx​axp​tn​(p−1)/2​x¯(p)+Pbig​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(u)).ct^{r(p-1)/2}{\it SSP}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{-1}(u)={\it FSt}_{p}({\it PSS}(u))\\

## Proof of Corollary1.7.

A standard argument (see[20,50]), which is a local argument, shows that for aC2C^{2}-generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphism either all of its periodic points are hyperbolic or it has infinitely many periodic points by a variant of the Birkhoff-Lewis theorem[36]. However, if all the periodic points ofϕ\phiare hyperbolic and there are finitely many of them, then an iterationψ=ϕk0\psi=\phi^{k_{0}}as above must be a pseudo-rotation with all periodic points hyperbolic. Theorem1.6now applies and finishes the proof.
∎

## Proof of Corollary1.9.

We work over characteristic22and use quantum Steenrod squares in the monotone setting. Letμ∈H4​(X;𝔽2)\mu\in H^{4}(X;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})be the point class. By[41, Theorem 1.7], one has𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(μ)=θ4​μ{\it QSt}_{2}(\mu)=\theta^{4}\mu

whereθ∈H1​(B​ℤ/2;𝔽2)\theta\in H^{1}(B{\mathbb{Z}}/2;{\mathbb{F}}_{2})is the generator. On the other hand, letκ:H∗​(X)⊗H∗​(X)⊗H∗​(ℂ​ℙ2)→H∗​(M)\kappa:H^{*}(X)\otimes H^{*}(X)\otimes H^{*}(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\to H^{*}(M)

be the usual Kunneth isomorphism. Consideru=κ​(μ⊗μ⊗1)∈H8​(M).u=\kappa(\mu\otimes\mu\otimes 1)\in H^{8}(M).

Then by the Kunneth property of the quantum Steenrod squares (Theorem1.11) we have𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(u)=(κ⊗mp)​(𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(μ)⊗𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(μ)⊗Q​S​t2​(1))=(κ⊗mp)​(θ4​μ⊗θ4​μ⊗1)=κ​(μ⊗μ⊗1)⊗θ8.{\it QSt}_{2}(u)=(\kappa\otimes m_{p})({\it QSt}_{2}(\mu)\otimes{\it QSt}_{2}(\mu)\otimes QSt_{2}(1))\\
=(\kappa\otimes m_{p})(\theta^{4}\mu\otimes\theta^{4}\mu\otimes 1)=\kappa(\mu\otimes\mu\otimes 1)\otimes\theta^{8}.

On the other hand, the classical Steenrod square satisfies the same Kunneth property. HenceS​t2​(u)=θ8​u=𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(u).St_{2}(u)=\theta^{8}u={\it QSt}_{2}(u).

Now we prove that aC2C^{2}-generic Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ\phionMMhas infinitely many periodic points. Recall that aC2C^{2}-genericϕ\phieither has infinitely many periodic points or all periodic points are hyperbolic. If the claim is not true, thenϕ\phihas finitely many periodic points all of which are hyperbolic. Then the calculation𝑄𝑆𝑡2​(u)=S​t2​(u){\it QSt}_{2}(u)=St_{2}(u)and the factr=8>6=nr=8>6=ncontradict Theorem1.6, see Remark1.10.∎

## 4.7.Proof of Theorem1.13

We first prove a variant of Lemma4.3.

## Lemma 4.7.

Under the assumption of Theorem1.13, assumeϕ\phihas only finitely many periodic points. Then no contractible fixed point ofϕ\phiis a symplectically degenerate maximum.

## Proof.

The argument is very similar to that of Lemma4.3.
First, as the local Floer cohomology is defined over integers, by the universal coefficient theorem,
there existsp0​(ϕ)p_{0}(\phi)such that for all primesp≥p0​(ϕ)p\geq p_{0}(\phi)and contractible fixed pointsx∈Fixc​(ϕ)x\in{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi), there holdsH​Floc​(ϕ,x;𝔽p)≅H​Floc​(ϕ,x;ℤ)⊗𝔽p.HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,x;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,x;{\mathbb{Z}})\otimes{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}.

Hence forp≥p0​(ϕ)p\geq p_{0}(\phi), one hasN​(ϕ;𝔽p)=N​(ϕ;ℚ).N(\phi;{\mathbb{F}}_{p})=N(\phi;{\mathbb{Q}}).

Further, by[21, Theorem 1.1], forppsufficiently large and all contractible fixed pointsx∈Fixc​(ϕ)x\in{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi), one hasdimH​Floc​(ϕp,x;𝔽p)=dimH​Floc​(ϕ,x;𝔽p).\dim HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi^{p},x;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})=\dim HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi,x;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}).

Asϕ\phihas only finitely many periodic points, whenppis sufficiently large, one hasFixc​(ϕ)=Fixc​(ϕp){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)={\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p}). HenceN​(ϕp;𝔽p)=∑y∈Fixc​(ϕp)dim​H​Floc​(ϕp,y;𝔽p)=∑x∈Fixc​(ϕ)dim​H​Floc​(ϕ,x;𝔽p)=N​(ϕ;𝔽p)=N​(ϕ;ℚ)=dim​H∗​(M;ℚ)=dim​H∗​(M;𝔽p).N(\phi^{p};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})=\sum_{y\in{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p})}{\rm dim}HF^{\rm loc}(\phi^{p},y;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})=\sum_{x\in{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)}{\rm dim}HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,x;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\\
=N(\phi;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})=N(\phi;{\mathbb{Q}})={\rm dim}H^{*}(M;{\mathbb{Q}})={\rm dim}H^{*}(M;{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}).(4.11)

IfHHis a 1-periodic Hamiltonian whose time-1 map isϕ\phi, the above identity implies that the differential of the complexC​(H(p))C(H^{(p)})constructed in Proposition3.1vanishes. Replacingpkp^{k}in the proof of Lemma4.3by a sufficiently largepp, one can obtain the same conclusion.
∎

We then prove a variant of Proposition4.6. The proof idea is the same but certain details need to be modified.

## Lemma 4.8.

Under the assumption of Theorem1.13, supposeϕ\phihas only finitely many periodic points, then there existx¯∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)and infinitely many primesppsuch thatH​Floc,2​n​(H,x¯;𝔽p)≠0,\displaystyle\ HF^{{\rm loc},2n}(H,\overline{x};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\neq 0,\H​Floc,2​n​(H(p),x¯(p);𝔽p)≠0.\displaystyle\ HF^{{\rm loc},2n}(H^{(p)},\overline{x}^{(p)};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\neq 0.

## Proof.

We can run the same argument as proving Proposition4.6. Notice that one no longer has an induction frompkp^{k}topk+1p^{k+1}. First, sinceϕ\phihas only finitely many periodic points, by considering large primespp, one hasFixc​(ϕ)=Fixc​(ϕp).{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)={\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p}).

Then, the equation𝑄𝑆𝑡p​(μ)=𝑆𝑡p​(μ){\it QSt}_{p}(\mu)={\it St}_{p}(\mu)can be translated, via the ordinary PSS map and the equivariant SSP map, to𝐹𝑆𝑡p​(𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ))=tn​(p−1)​𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p−1​(μ)∈H​Fℤ/p​(M).{\it FSt}_{p}({\it PSS}(\mu))=t^{n(p-1)}{\it SSP}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{-1}(\mu)\in HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M).

Letx1,…,xmx_{1},\ldots,x_{m}be all contractible fixed points ofϕ\phi. Then by Proposition3.1, one can decompose𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ){\it PSS}(\mu)as𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ)=∑i=1mμi,where​μi∈H​Floc​(H,xi;ΛΓ){\it PSS}(\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i},\ {\rm where}\ \mu_{i}\in HF^{\rm loc}(H,x_{i};\Lambda^{\Gamma})

and𝑃𝑆𝑆​(μ)=∑i=1mμi(p),where​μi(p)∈H​Floc​(H(p),xi(p);ΛΓ).{\it PSS}(\mu)=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mu_{i}^{(p)},\ {\rm where}\ \mu_{i}^{(p)}\in HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},x_{i}^{(p)};\Lambda^{\Gamma}).

Another ingredient in Sugimoto’s argument is the persistence of local Floer homology. We provide a proof for the convenience of the reader.

## Lemma 4.11(Persistence of local Floer cohomology: noncontractible case).

Letx¯\overline{x}be an isolatedγ\gamma-capped 1-periodic orbit ofHH. Letk≥1k\geq 1. Supposex¯\overline{x}iskk-admissible. ThenH​Fγloc​(H,x¯;𝕂)≠0⟹H​Fk​γloc​(H(k),x¯(k);𝕂)≠0.HF^{\rm loc}_{\gamma}(H,\overline{x};{\mathbb{K}})\neq 0\Longrightarrow HF^{\rm loc}_{k\gamma}(H^{(k)},\overline{x}^{(k)};{\mathbb{K}})\neq 0.

## Proof.

Letϕ\phibe the time-1 map ofHH. Thenx=x​(0)x=x(0)is an isolated fixed point ofϕ\phi. Notice that the local Floer cohomologyH​Fγloc​(H,x¯;𝕂)HF_{\gamma}^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{x};{\mathbb{K}})only depends on the germ of the symplectomorphismϕ\phinearxx. Moreover, it is well-known that the germ of a symplectomorphism near a fixed point is Hamiltonian (see[21, Remark 4.1]). Then one can reduce the consideration to a local problem where the fixed point is contractible. Then the lemma follows from the contractible case[21, Theorem 1.1].

Therefore, there existsk1k_{1}such thatβ1​(H(pk))≥1\beta_{1}(H^{(p^{k})})\geq 1fork≥k1.k\geq k_{1}.Then forϵ<1/3\epsilon<1/3andk≥max⁡{k1,logp⁡(rϵ)},k\geq\max\{k_{1},\log_{p}(r_{\epsilon})\},the connecting homomorphism (4.2) must vanish. Indeed, if the connecting homomorphism (4.2) were non-zero, then there would be a bar in the barcode ofH(pk)H^{(p^{k})}which starts in the interval(r​c−δ,r​c+δ)(rc-\delta,rc+\delta)and ends in the interval(r​c+δ,r​c+ϵ).(rc+\delta,rc+\epsilon).In particular, its length would be smaller than2​δ+ϵ<1.2\delta+\epsilon<1.This creates a contradiction to results of[23,25,19]as in the proof of Lemma4.3.

## 5.Kunneth property of the quantum Steenrod operations

In this section we prove that the quantum Steenrod operation respects the ordinary and the equivariant Kunneth maps in the monotone setting. We also prove an analogous version in the Floer-theoretic setting. Throughout this section, all homology is taken with𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}-coefficients unless otherwise stated.

## 5.1.Equivariant Kunneth maps via Morse theory

We first need to describe the equivariant Kunneth map on the chain level, using the Morse model. Fori=1,2i=1,2, letXiX_{i}be a smoothℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-manifold andfi:Xi→ℝf_{i}:X_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}be aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse function. In Section2.3we described the construction of the equivariant Morse cochain complex. Now we describe how to define the equivariant Kunneth mapκℤ/p:H​Mℤ/p​(f1)⊗H​Mℤ/p​(f2)→H​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2)\kappa_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1})\otimes HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{2})\to HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})

on the chain level.

Recall that we used a specificℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant functiong:S∞→ℝg:S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}, see (2.9), together with aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant metric to give a cochain model forB​ℤ/pB{\mathbb{Z}/p}. for which the equivariant Morse complexC​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2)CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})can be defined.

The union of moduli spaces over all possible asymptotic limits has a freeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. Hence by choosingℱs,v{\mathscr{F}}_{s,v}generically, one can achieve equivariant transversality. The moduli space is shown in Figure1.Figure 1.Moduli space defining the equivariant Kunneth map for the tensor products. The coiled tree is a flow tree inS∞S^{\infty}.

On the other hand, the moduli spaceℳw1,w2,w∞​(x1,−,x2,−;x1,+,x2,+){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}}(x_{1,-},x_{2,-};x_{1,+},x_{2,+})can be compactified by suitably adding broken configurations. More precisely, the breaking along the legT1T_{1}resp.T2T_{2}is regarded as a trajectory inX1×S∞X_{1}\times S^{\infty}resp.X2×S∞X_{2}\times S^{\infty}, while the breaking along the legT∞T_{\infty}is regarded as a map intoX1×X2×S∞X_{1}\times X_{2}\times S^{\infty}. Then the counts of the moduli spacesℳw1,w2,w∞​(x1,−,x2,−;x1,+,x2,+){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{1},w_{2},w_{\infty}}(x_{1,-},x_{2,-};x_{1,+},x_{2,+})define a chain mapκℤ/p:C​Mℤ/p​(f1)⊗𝔽pC​Mℤ/p​(f2)→C​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2)\kappa_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1})\otimes_{{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}}CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{2})\to CM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})(5.1)

which descends to the equivariant Kunneth map. One can also prove that on the cohomology level, the equivariant Kunneth map isℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-bilinear, whereH​Mℤ/p​(f1)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}),H​Mℤ/p​(f2)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{2}), andH​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2)HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})areℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules. We only summarize the results and omit the details.

## Proposition 5.1.

The chain map (5.1) descends to anℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-bilinear mapκℤ/p:H​Mℤ/p​(f1)⊗ℛpH​Mℤ/p​(f2)→H​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2).\kappa_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}:HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1})\otimes_{{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{2})\to HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2}).

## Remark 5.2.

When one can achieve equivariant transversality onXiX_{i}, i.e., one can choose aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse–Smale pair(fi,hi)(f_{i},h_{i})onXiX_{i}, then one can also achieve equivariant transversality onX1×X2X_{1}\times X_{2}. In particular, one can takef∞,v=f1×f2f_{\infty,v}=f_{1}\times f_{2}and choose the product metric. Then one can also chooseℱ{\mathscr{F}}to be basicallyf1×f2f_{1}\times f_{2}. Therefore, the equations defining the moduli spaces are decoupled.

## 5.1.1.Two special cases

There are two special cases (in the finite-dimensional setting) considered in this paper. The first case is whenℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}acts trivially onX1X_{1}andX2X_{2}. In this case, given any Morse functionsfi:Xi→ℝf_{i}:X_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}with an associated Morse complexC​M​(fi)CM(f_{i}), asℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modules one hasH​Mℤ/p​(fi)≅H​M​(fi)⊗ℛp,\displaystyle\ HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{i})\cong HM(f_{i})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}},\H​Mℤ/p​(f1×f2)≅H​M​(f1×f2)⊗ℛp.\displaystyle\ HM_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(f_{1}\times f_{2})\cong HM(f_{1}\times f_{2})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

The equivariant Kunneth map reduces to the ordinary one. Namely,κℤ/p=κ⊗mp\kappa_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}=\kappa\otimes m_{p}

whereκ:H​M​(f1)⊗H​M​(f2)→H​M​(f1×f2)\kappa:HM(f_{1})\otimes HM(f_{2})\to HM(f_{1}\times f_{2})is the ordinary Kunneth isomorphism andmp:ℛp⊗ℛp→ℛpm_{p}:{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\to{\mathcal{R}_{p}}is the ring multiplication.

Another special case is thepp-fold tensor power. Supposeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}acts trivially onXiX_{i},i=1,2i=1,2. Letfi:Xi→ℝf_{i}:X_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}be Morse functions. We have described the Morse model for the equivariant cochain complexC​M​(fi)ℤ/p⊗pCM(f_{i})^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}, which is theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part of the product Morse chain complexC​M​(fi×p×g)CM(f_{i}^{\times p}\times g). Then the cochain-level equivariant Kunneth map is a special case of the general situation.

## 5.1.2.Kunneth property of quasi-Frobenius map

Now consider two Morse cochain complexesC​M​(f1)CM(f_{1}),C​M​(f2)CM(f_{2})onX1X_{1}andX2X_{2}respectively.

The following diagram commutes up to Koszul signs.H​M​(f1)⊗H​M​(f2)\textstyle{HM(f_{1})\otimes HM(f_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}q​F⊗q​F\scriptstyle{qF\otimes qF}κ\scriptstyle{\kappa}H​(C​M​(f1)ℤ/p⊗p)⊗H​(C​M​(f2)ℤ/p⊗p)\textstyle{H(CM(f_{1})^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})\otimes H(CM(f_{2})^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}κℤ/p\scriptstyle{\kappa_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}}H​M​(f1×f2)\textstyle{HM(f_{1}\times f_{2})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}q​F\scriptstyle{qF}H​(C​M​(f1×f2)ℤ/p⊗p)\textstyle{H(CM(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})}

## Sketch of proof.

Choose classesai∈H​M​(fi)a_{i}\in HM(f_{i})and choose representing cocyclesxi∈C​M​(fi)x_{i}\in CM(f_{i}). Recall thatq​F​(ai)∈H​(C​M​(fi)ℤ/p⊗p)qF(a_{i})\in H(CM(f_{i})^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}})is represented by the equivariant cocyclexi⊗p⊗1∈C​M​(fi)⊗⋯⊗C​M​(fi)⊗ℛp.x_{i}^{\otimes p}\otimes 1\in CM(f_{i})\otimes\cdots\otimes CM(f_{i})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Using the Morse model onS∞S^{\infty}, letw1,…,wp∈crit​(g)⊂S∞w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}\in{\rm crit}(g)\subset S^{\infty}be theppcritical points ofggof index0. Then the above cocycle is equivalent to the Morse cocyclexi⊗p⊗(w1+⋯+wp)∈(C​M​(fi)⊗p⊗C​M​(g))Inv.x_{i}^{\otimes p}\otimes(w_{1}+\cdots+w_{p})\in\left(CM(f_{i})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\right)^{\rm Inv}.

We claim that the equivariant Kunneth mapκ~ℤ/p:(C​M​(f1)⊗p⊗C​M​(g))⊗(C​M​(f2)⊗p⊗C​M​(g))→C​M​(f1×f2)⊗p⊗C​M​(g)\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}:\Big(CM(f_{1})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\Big)\otimes\Big(CM(f_{2})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\Big)\to CM(f_{1}\times f_{2})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)

givesκ~ℤ/p​((x1⊗p⊗(w1+⋯+wp))⊗(x2⊗p⊗(w1+⋯+wp)))=±(κ​(x1⊗x2))⊗p⊗(w1+⋯+wp).\widetilde{\kappa}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}\Big((x_{1}^{\otimes p}\otimes(w_{1}+\cdots+w_{p}))\otimes(x_{2}^{\otimes p}\otimes(w_{1}+\cdots+w_{p}))\Big)=\pm(\kappa(x_{1}\otimes x_{2}))^{\otimes p}\otimes(w_{1}+\cdots+w_{p}).

Here the sign comes from switching certain copies ofx1x_{1}andx2x_{2}. This claim essentially follows from the fact that in this situation equivariant transversality holds. Hence the flow line equation inX1×X2X_{1}\times X_{2}and the Y-shaped gradient tree equation inS∞S^{\infty}are decoupled (see Remark5.2). This verifies the commutativity (up to signs) of the diagram.

## Proposition 5.4.

The local equivariant Kunneth mapκℤ/ploc\kappa_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}on local equivariant Floer cohomology is bilinear overℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

## 5.2.Quantum Steenrod operation via inhomogeneous term perturbations

The definition of quantum Steenrod operation given in[10]and recalled in Subsection2.4is based on abstract perturbations. In the monotone setting, one can use an alternate approach by geometrically perturbing the equation, as done in[45]and[41]. Here we provide such a definition under the monotonicity assumption using the Morse model. Notice that[41]uses a particular cellular model for the equivariant cohomology. The comparison between the cellular and the Morse-theoretic approaches is carried out in AppendixA.

Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a positively monotone closed symplectic manifold. Choose a Morse functionf:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}}together with a Morse–Smale metric. Letg:S∞→ℝg:S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}be theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse function used throughout this paper.

We choose the inhomogeneous term as follows. Recall thatΣp→S2\Sigma_{p}\to S^{2}is thepp-fold cover branched over0and∞\infty. Consider a smooth family of complex-antilinear mapsνv,z,xe​q:Tz​Σp→Tx​M\nu^{eq}_{v,z,x}:T_{z}\Sigma_{p}\to T_{x}M

parametrized by(v,z,x)∈S∞×Σp×M(v,z,x)\in S^{\infty}\times\Sigma_{p}\times Mwhich satisfies the two equivariance conditions:
- (1)

Theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariance: for the generatorι∈ℤ/p\iota\in{\mathbb{Z}/p}which acts on bothS∞S^{\infty}andΣp\Sigma_{p}νι​(v),z,xe​q=νv,ι​(z),xe​q∘D​ιz:Tz​Σp→Tx​M.\nu^{eq}_{\iota(v),z,x}=\nu^{eq}_{v,\iota(z),x}\circ D\iota_{z}:T_{z}\Sigma_{p}\to T_{x}M.(5.2)

Theℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-equivariance: for the generatorτ∈ℤ≥0\tau\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}which acts onS∞S^{\infty}ντ​(v),z,xe​q=νv,z,xe​q.\nu_{\tau(v),z,x}^{eq}=\nu_{v,z,x}^{eq}.

We also choose a genericω\omega-compatibleJJsuch that[41, Assumption 3.2]is satisfied, namely, the moduli spaces of certain configurations of simpleJJ-holomorphic spheres (chains) are regular and certain evaluation maps on such moduli spaces are transverse to stable and unstable manifolds of the chosen Morse functionf:M→ℝf:M\to{\mathbb{R}}.

Now we describe the moduli spaces; the moduli spaces considered in Section2.4are the special cases forνv,z,xe​q≡0\nu_{v,z,x}^{eq}\equiv 0. ForA∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}),w−,w+∈crit​(g)w_{-},w_{+}\in{\rm crit}(g), andx1,…,xp,y∈crit​(f)x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y\in{\rm crit}(f), letℳw−,w+​(A;x1,…,xp,y){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y)

Now one definesQ​S​t~~:p,ACM(f)⊗p⊗CM(g)→CM(f)⊗CM(g)\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}:CM(f)^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\to CM(f)\otimes CM(g)

by linearly extendingQ​S​t~~(x1⊗⋯⊗xp⊗w−)p,A=∑y,w+#pℳw−,w+(A;x1,…,xp,y)⋅y⊗w+.\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p}\otimes w_{-})=\sum_{y,w_{+}}\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y)\cdot y\otimes w_{+}.(5.6)

AsMMis monotone andJJis chosen to make simple holomorphic spheres regular, 1-dimensional moduli spaces can be compactified by adding only configurations with smooth spheres and possibly broken Morse trajectories. Such a structure of the boundary of 1-dimensional moduli spaces implies thatQ​S​t~~p,A\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}is a chain map.

Notice that by (5.5), this map isℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant. NamelyQ​S​t~~(x2⊗⋯⊗xp⊗x1⊗ι(w−))p,A=ι(Q​S​t~~(x1⊗⋯⊗xp⊗w−)p,A).\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}(x_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p}\otimes x_{1}\otimes\iota(w_{-}))=\iota(\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}(x_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{p}\otimes w_{-})).

Therefore, its restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part of the domain gives a chain mapQ​S​t~:p,ACM(f)ℤ/p⊗p≅(CM(f)⊗p⊗CM(g))ℤ/p→CM(f)⊗CM(g)ℤ/p≅CM(f)⊗ℛp.\widetilde{QSt}{}_{p,A}:CM(f)^{\otimes p}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\cong(CM(f)^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g))^{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\to CM(f)\otimes CM(g)^{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\cong CM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

By abuse of notation, the induced map on homology is denoted byQ​S​t~:p,AHℤ/p(CM(f)⊗p)→HM(f)⊗ℛp.\widetilde{QSt}{}_{p,A}:H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(CM(f)^{\otimes p})\to HM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

Recall we also have the quasi-Frobenius map in𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}-coefficientsq​F:H​M​(f)→Hℤ/p​(C​M​(f)⊗p).qF:HM(f)\to H_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(CM(f)^{\otimes p}).

Then we can define the degreeAApart of the quantum Steenrod operation asQ​S​tp,A:=Q​S​t~p,A∘q​F:H​M​(f)→H​M​(f)⊗ℛp.QSt_{p,A}:=\widetilde{QSt}_{p,A}\circ qF:HM(f)\to HM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

A standard argument using continuation maps shows that it induces a well-defined mapQ​S​tp,A:H​(M)→H​(M)⊗ℛpQSt_{p,A}:H(M)\to H(M)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}(5.7)

which is independent of the choices offf,JJandνv,z,xe​q\nu^{eq}_{v,z,x}. The total quantum Steenrod operation is thenQ​S​tp:=∑A∈H2​(M;ℤ)qA​Q​S​tp,A:H​(M)→H​(M;ΛΓ)⊗ℛp.QSt_{p}:=\sum_{A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})}q^{A}QSt_{p,A}:H(M)\to H(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.(5.8)

Here notice that the monotonicity condition guarantees it lands in the above target instead ofΛ𝒦0,pΓ⊗E​(θ)\Lambda^{\Gamma}_{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}\otimes E(\theta).

## Theorem 5.5.

[10, Theorem H]The above definition agrees with the definition using abstract perturbations for monotone targets.

## 5.3.Proof of Theorem1.11

The proof is based on the TQFT/cobordism argument which is described in Figure3.Figure 3.The cobordism leading to the Kunneth property of the quantum Steenrod operation. The coiled trees are flow trees inS∞S^{\infty}while the straight segments are flow lines inX1X_{1}orX2X_{2}. We only consider moduli spaces of expected dimension zero (without deformingρ\rho). Then including the deformation ofρ\rho, defineℳ±:=⨆ρ∈ℝ±ℳρ.{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}:=\bigsqcup_{\rho\in{\mathbb{R}}_{\pm}}{\mathcal{M}}_{\rho}.

Then one can see that bothℳ±{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}are the zero loci of some Fredholm section of index11defined over a certain Banach manifold. The union of all such moduli spaces admits a natural freeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action. One can perturb the family of inhomogeneous termsνX1×X2,ρe​q\nu_{X_{1}\times X_{2},\rho}^{eq}to achieveℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant transversality. In addition, one can make the moduli spaceℳ0{\mathcal{M}}_{0}transverse.

One can compactifyℳ±{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}by allowing sphere bubbles, trajectory breakings, and the parameterρ→±∞\rho\to\pm\infty. As we only consider the index11case, the monotonicity condition and the transversality property of simple holomorphic spheres imply that the compactification contains, besidesℳ±{\mathcal{M}}_{\pm}, either configurations having at most one Morse trajectory breaking with finiteρ\rho, or configurations atρ=±∞\rho=\pm\inftyshown in Figure3.

Therefore, one obtains aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant chain homotopy between the two chain mapsκ~∘ℤ/p(Q​S​t~⊗pX1Q​S​t~)pX2,\displaystyle\ \widetilde{\kappa}{}^{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}\circ(\widetilde{QSt}{}_{p}^{X_{1}}\otimes\widetilde{QSt}{}_{p}^{X_{2}}),\Q​S​t~∘pX1×X2(κ~⊗mℛp).\displaystyle\ \widetilde{QSt}{}_{p}^{X_{1}\times X_{2}}\circ(\widetilde{\kappa}\otimes m_{{\mathcal{R}}_{p}}).

We first choose data so that all objects and arrows appearing in the commutative diagram can be defined on the chain level.
