## Appendix ATechnical discussions on the quantum Steenrod operation

In this appendix, we compare the definition of the quantum Steenrod power operation𝑄𝑆𝑡p{\it QSt}_{p}in[41]and the one recalled in Section2.4restricted to the monotone situation. Throughout this section, all cohomology is taken with𝔽p{\mathbb{F}}_{p}-coefficients unless otherwise stated. We first notice that by Theorem5.5, the latter agrees with the one used in Section5. Hence we will not use abstract perturbation but only the inhomogeneous perturbation incorporated with the Morse model.

The idea is as follows. To define each individual count which leads to the definition of the quantum Steenrod operation, one considers the moduli spaces ofJJ-holomorphic spheres perturbed by an inhomogeneous termνv,z,xe​q\nu_{v,z,x}^{eq}parametrized byvvcontained in a certain chain inS∞S^{\infty}. In Subsection5.2, the parameterν\nuis contained in some Morse chain associated to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant Morse functiong:S∞→ℝg:S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}. In[41], the parameterν\nuis chosen to be contained in a singular chain. The comparison is basically reduced to the comparison between corresponding chains insideS∞S^{\infty}.

## A.1.Setup

Now we start the comparison.
LetMMbe a monotone symplectic manifold. In the version of[41], it was not required that the inhomogeneous termνe​q\nu^{eq}is invariant under theℤ≥0{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}-action; however, imposing this condition in the setup of[41]does not affect that construction. Hence we can choose such a symmetric inhomogeneous termνe​q\nu^{eq}. We also choose a generic almost complex structureJJto ensure that the counts are well-defined. To temporarily distinguish the two definitions, let𝑄𝑆𝑡pMorse{\it QSt}^{\rm Morse}_{p}be the quantum Steenrod operation defined in Subsection5.2and let𝑄𝑆𝑡pcell{\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p}be the one defined in[41].

The comparison is done on the level of moduli spaces. Choose a Morse functionfftogether with a Morse–Smale metric. For any subsetQ⊂S∞Q\subset S^{\infty}, denote byℳQ​(A;x1,…,xp,y){\mathcal{M}}_{Q}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y)

the set of pairs(u,v)(u,v)wherev∈Qv\in Qandu:Σp→Mu:\Sigma_{p}\to Msolves the equation∂¯J​u​(z)=νv,z,u​(z)e​q\overline{\partial}_{J}u(z)=\nu_{v,z,u(z)}^{eq}

and satisfies the constraints at thep+1p+1marked points imposed by the unstable/stable manifolds ofx1,…,xp,yx_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y.

The cochain groups used in the two approaches are also different. We choose the following identification. DefineΨ:C​M​(f)⊗ℛp→C​M​(f)⊗C​M​(g)x⊗tk↦∑l=1px⊗w2​kl,x⊗tk​θ↦∑l=1px⊗w2​k+1l,\begin{split}\Psi:CM(f)\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}\to&CM(f)\otimes CM(g)\\
x\otimes t^{k}\mapsto&\ \sum_{l=1}^{p}x\otimes w_{2k}^{l},\\
x\otimes t^{k}\theta\mapsto&\ \sum_{l=1}^{p}x\otimes w_{2k+1}^{l},\end{split}

cf. Equations (2.10) and (2.11). Then the image ofΨ\Psiis contained in theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part and defines an isomorphism onto its image.

## A.2.The comparison

We first briefly recall the cells inS∞S^{\infty}used in the paper[41]. In each nonnegative degreeii, we specify a cellΔi⊂S∞\Delta_{i}\subset S^{\infty}which projects down to a singular cycle inB​ℤ/pB{\mathbb{Z}/p}representing a generator ofHi​(B​ℤ/p;𝔽p)H_{i}(B{\mathbb{Z}/p};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}).
For an even degreei=2​ki=2k, it isΔ2​k={v=(vj)j≥0∈S∞|vk≥0,vk+1=vk+2=⋯=0};\Delta_{2k}=\Big\{v=(v_{j})_{j\geq 0}\in S^{\infty}\ |\ v_{k}\geq 0,\ v_{k+1}=v_{k+2}=\cdots=0\Big\};

for an odd degreei=2​k+1i=2k+1, it isΔ2​k+1={v=(vj)j≥0∈S∞|arg​(vk)∈[0,2​π/p],vk+1=vk+2=⋯=0}.\Delta_{2k+1}=\Big\{v=(v_{j})_{j\geq 0}\in S^{\infty}\ |\ {\rm arg}(v_{k})\in[0,2\pi/p],v_{k+1}=v_{k+2}=\cdots=0\Big\}.

We have∂Δ2​k=Δ2​k−1+τ​Δ2​k−1+⋯+τp−1​Δ2​k−1,\displaystyle\ \partial\Delta_{2k}=\Delta_{2k-1}+\tau\Delta_{2k-1}+\dots+\tau^{p-1}\Delta_{2k-1},\∂Δ2​k+1=τ​Δ2​k−Δ2​k.\displaystyle\ \partial\Delta_{2k+1}=\tau\Delta_{2k}-\Delta_{2k}.(A.1)

We use the notations in[41]to define the chain-level maps. To this end, for eachi≥0i\geq 0,A∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}}), and critical pointsx1,…,xp,yx_{1},\ldots,x_{p},yofff, one has a well-defined modppcount#p​ℳΔi​(A;x1,…,xp,y)∈𝔽p\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Delta_{i}}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y)\in{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}

(which is zero by definition if the expected dimension is nonzero). These counts define linear maps𝑄𝑆𝑡p,i,Acell:C​M​(f)⊗p→C​M​(f).{\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p,i,A}:CM(f)^{\otimes p}\to CM(f).

Maps of this form assemble to define the quantum Steenrod operation𝑄𝑆𝑡pcell{\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p}. Ifα∈C​M​(f)\alpha\in CM(f)is a Morse cocycle, we define𝑄𝑆𝑡pcell​([α])=∑A∈H2​(M;ℤ)qA​Q​S​tp,Acell​([α]){\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p}([\alpha])=\sum_{A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})}q^{A}QSt^{\rm cell}_{p,A}([\alpha])

while𝑄𝑆𝑡p,Acell​([α])=∑k=0∞(𝑄𝑆𝑡p,2​k,Acell​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk+𝑄𝑆𝑡p,2​k+1,Acell​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk​θ){\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p,A}([\alpha])=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big({\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p,2k,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}+{\it QSt}^{\rm cell}_{p,2k+1,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}\theta\Big)

which is, by[41, Lemma 4.4], a cocycle whose cohomology class only depends on the class[α][\alpha].

Now we describe the cellular decomposition ofS∞S^{\infty}used in this paper, which arises from Morse theory. Recall the functiong:S∞→ℝg:S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}has critical points given in (2.10) and (2.11). LetWu​(wil)W^{u}(w_{i}^{l})resp.Ws​(wil)W^{s}(w_{i}^{l})be the unstable resp. stable manifolds of the critical pointwilw_{i}^{l}with respect to the ascending flow ofgg. Then definePil:=Wu​(w0l)∩Ws​(wi1)⊂S∞P_{i}^{l}:=W^{u}(w_{0}^{l})\cap W^{s}(w_{i}^{1})\subset S^{\infty}

which is anii-dimensional cell. DefinePi:=⋃lPil⊂S∞.P_{i}:=\bigcup_{l}P_{i}^{l}\subset S^{\infty}.

By counting zero-dimensional moduli spaces, one obtains a set of modppcounts#p​ℳPi​(A;x1,…,xp,y).\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{P_{i}}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y).

Then define a linear map𝑄𝑆𝑡p,i,AMorse:C​M​(f)⊗p→C​M​(f){\it QSt}^{\rm Morse}_{p,i,A}:CM(f)^{\otimes p}\to CM(f)

using these as coefficients.

## Lemma A.1.

The quantum Steenrod operation𝑄𝑆𝑡pMorse{\it QSt}^{\rm Morse}_{p}defined in Subsection5.2is determined by𝑄𝑆𝑡p,i,AMorse{\it QSt}^{\rm Morse}_{p,i,A}in the same way asQ​S​tp,i,AcellQSt^{\rm cell}_{p,i,A}determinesQ​S​tpcellQSt^{\rm cell}_{p}. More precisely, for any Morse cocycleα∈C​M​(f)\alpha\in CM(f), one hasQ​S​tp,AMorse​([α])=[Ψ​(∑k=0∞(Q​S​tp,2​k,AMorse​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk+Q​S​tp,2​k+1,AMorse​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk​θ))].QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,A}([\alpha])=\big[\Psi\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\Big(QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,2k,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}+QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,2k+1,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}\theta\Big)\right)\big].

## Proof.

To simplify notations, we omit the superscript “Morse” in this proof. We may assume thatα\alphais a single critical point; the case whenα\alphais a linear combination of critical points only adds notational complexities. Consider the chainα~p:=∑l=1pα⊗⋯⊗α⊗w0l∈C​M​(f)⊗p⊗C​M​(g).\tilde{\alpha}^{p}:=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha\otimes w_{0}^{l}\in CM(f)^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g).

Asα\alphais a cocycle, one can see thatα~p\tilde{\alpha}^{p}isdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}-closed. Now by the definition of the chain mapQ​S​t~~p,A\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}_{p,A}and theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariance of the counts, one hasQ​S​t~~(α~p)p,A=∑y∈crit​(f)∑l=1p∑w∈crit​(g)#p​ℳw0l,w​(A;α,…,α,y)⋅y⊗w=∑y∈crit​(f)∑i≥0(∑l=1p#p​ℳw0l,wi1​(A;α,…,α,y)⋅y)⊗∑l=1pτl−1​(wi1)=∑y∈crit​(f)∑i≥0(#pℳ(A;α,…,α,y)Pi⋅y)⊗∑l=1pwil=Ψ​(∑k=0∞Q​S​tp,2​k,A​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk+∑k=0∞Q​S​tp,2​k+1,A​(α⊗⋯⊗α)⊗tk​θ).\begin{split}\widetilde{\widetilde{QSt}}{}_{p,A}(\tilde{\alpha}^{p})=&\ \sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\sum_{l=1}^{p}\sum_{w\in{\rm crit}(g)}\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{w_{0}^{l},w}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\cdot y\otimes w\\
=&\ \sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{p}\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{w_{0}^{l},w_{i}^{1}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\cdot y\right)\otimes\sum_{l=1}^{p}\tau^{l-1}(w_{i}^{1})\\
=&\ \sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\sum_{i\geq 0}\left(\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}{}_{P_{i}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\cdot y\right)\otimes\sum_{l=1}^{p}w_{i}^{l}\\
=&\ \Psi\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}QSt_{p,2k,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}+\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}QSt_{p,2k+1,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\otimes t^{k}\theta\right).\end{split}

As the cohomology classes of both sides are well-defined, the lemma follows.
∎

As a consequence, the comparison is reduced to the following situation.

## Lemma A.2.

FixA∈H2​(M;ℤ)A\in H_{2}(M;{\mathbb{Z}})andi≥0i\geq 0. For any generic inhomogeneous termνv,z,xe​q\nu_{v,z,x}^{eq}, for any Morse cocycleα∈C​M​(f)\alpha\in CM(f), the two Morse cocyclesQ​S​tp,i,AMorse​(α⊗⋯⊗α),Q​S​tp,i,Acell​(α⊗⋯⊗α)QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha),\ QSt^{\rm cell}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)

are cohomologous.

This lemma will be proved in a moment. By combining the two above lemmas, one obtains the following theorem.

## Theorem A.3.

For a monotone closed symplectic manifoldMM, the definition of the quantum Steenrod operation given in Subsection5.2agrees with the definition ofQ​S​tpcellQSt^{\rm cell}_{p}.

## A.3.Proof of LemmaA.2

The proof is based on explicit comparisons of the involved chains inS∞S^{\infty}.

## Lemma A.4.

There exists a sequence of piecewise smooth chainsBi∈Ci​(Si;ℤ)⊂Ci​(S∞;ℤ)B_{i}\in C_{i}(S^{i};{\mathbb{Z}})\subset C_{i}(S^{\infty};{\mathbb{Z}})fori≥1i\geq 1satisfyingPi−Δi={∂B2​k+1,i=2​k,τ​(B2​k+1)−B2​k+1+∂B2​k+2,i=2​k+1.P_{i}-\Delta_{i}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\partial B_{2k+1},&\ i=2k,\\
\tau(B_{2k+1})-B_{2k+1}+\partial B_{2k+2},&\ i=2k+1.\end{array}\right.

## Proof.

Δ0=P0\Delta_{0}=P_{0}is obvious andΔ1=P1\Delta_{1}=P_{1}because they are both the unique positively oriented segment ofS1⊂S∞S^{1}\subset S^{\infty}betweenΔ0\Delta_{0}andτ​(Δ0)\tau(\Delta_{0}). Hence we can chooseB1=0B_{1}=0,B2=0B_{2}=0.

For any positive even degreei=2​ki=2k, by the differential of the Morse cochain complexC​M​(g)CM(g), one has∂P2​k=∑l=1pτl−1​(P2​k−1).\partial P_{2k}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\tau^{l-1}(P_{2k-1}).

Together with (A.1), one obtains∂(P2​k−Δ2​k)=∑l=1p(τl−1​(P2​k−1)−τl−1​(Δ2​k−1)).\partial(P_{2k}-\Delta_{2k})=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\big(\tau^{l-1}(P_{2k-1})-\tau^{l-1}(\Delta_{2k-1})\big).

Notice that as chains, the sphereS2​k−1S^{2k-1}can be written asS2​k−1=∑l=1pτl−1​(Δ2​k−1)=∑l=1pτl−1​(P2​k−1).S^{2k-1}=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\tau^{l-1}(\Delta_{2k-1})=\sum_{l=1}^{p}\tau^{l-1}(P_{2k-1}).

It follows that∂(P2​k−Δ2​k)=0\partial(P_{2k}-\Delta_{2k})=0. AsS∞S^{\infty}is contractible, there hence existsB2​k+1∈C2​k+1​(S2​k+1;ℤ)B_{2k+1}\in C_{2k+1}(S^{2k+1};{\mathbb{Z}})such thatP2​k−Δ2​k=∂B2​k+1.P_{2k}-\Delta_{2k}=\partial B_{2k+1}.

For any positive odd degreei=2​k+1i=2k+1, by the differential of the Morse cochain complexC​M​(g)CM(g), one has∂P2​k+1=τ​(P2​k)−P2​k.\partial P_{2k+1}=\tau(P_{2k})-P_{2k}.

Then together with (A.1), one has∂(P2​k+1−Δ2​k+1)=(τ−Id)​(P2​k−Δ2​k)=(τ−Id)​∂B2​k+1.\partial(P_{2k+1}-\Delta_{2k+1})=(\tau-{\rm Id})(P_{2k}-\Delta_{2k})=(\tau-{\rm Id})\partial B_{2k+1}.

Again, by contractibility ofS∞S^{\infty}, there existsB2​k+2∈C2​k+2​(S2​k+2,ℤ)B_{2k+2}\in C_{2k+2}(S^{2k+2},{\mathbb{Z}})such thatP2​k+1−Δ2​k+1=(τ−1)​B2​k+1+∂B2​k+2.∎P_{2k+1}-\Delta_{2k+1}=(\tau-1)B_{2k+1}+\partial B_{2k+2}.\qed

## Proof of LemmaA.2.

Again, we assume thatα\alphais a single critical point; the case whenα\alphais a linear combination of critical points follows from the special case with only adding more notations. We can choose the inhomogeneous termνe​q\nu^{eq}such that the moduli spacesℳQ​(A;x1,…,xp,y){\mathcal{M}}_{Q}(A;x_{1},\ldots,x_{p},y)are regular whenQQis equal to faces ofPiP_{i},Δi\Delta_{i}, orBiB_{i}. Then by using 1-dimensional moduli spaces of the formℳBi+1​(A;α,…,α,y){\mathcal{M}}_{B_{i+1}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y), by LemmaA.4, one can see the following holds.
- (1)

Wheni=2​ki=2kis even, one hasQ​S​tp,i,AMorse​(α⊗⋯⊗α)−Q​S​tp,i,Acell​(α⊗⋯⊗α)=∑y∈crit​(f)(#p​ℳPi​(A;α,…,α,y)−#p​ℳΔi​(A;α,…,α,y))⋅y=±d​(∑z∈crit​(f)#p​ℳB2​k+1​(A;α,…,α,z)⋅z).\begin{split}&\ QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)-QSt^{\rm cell}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\\
=&\ \sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\left(\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{P_{i}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)-\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Delta_{i}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\right)\cdot y\\
=&\ \pm d\Big(\sum_{z\in{\rm crit}(f)}\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{B_{2k+1}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,z)\cdot z\Big).\end{split}
- (2)

Wheni=2​k+1i=2k+1is odd, one hasQ​S​tp,i,AMorse​(α⊗⋯⊗α)−Q​S​tp,i,Acell​(α⊗⋯⊗α)=∑y∈crit​(f)(#p​ℳPi​(A;α,…,α,y)−#p​ℳΔi​(A;α,…,α,y))⋅y=±d​(∑z∈crit​(f)#p​ℳB2​k+2​(A;α,…,α,z)⋅z)+∑y∈crit​(f)(#p​ℳτ​(B2​k+1)​(A;α,…,α,y)−#p​ℳB2​k+1​(A;α,…,α,y))⋅y.\begin{split}&\ QSt^{\rm Morse}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)-QSt^{\rm cell}_{p,i,A}(\alpha\otimes\cdots\otimes\alpha)\\
=&\ \sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\Big(\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{P_{i}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)-\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Delta_{i}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\Big)\cdot y\\
=&\ \pm d\Big(\sum_{z\in{\rm crit}(f)}\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{B_{2k+2}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,z)\cdot z\Big)\\
&\ +\sum_{y\in{\rm crit}(f)}\Big(\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{\tau(B_{2k+1})}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)-\#_{p}{\mathcal{M}}_{B_{2k+1}}(A;\alpha,\ldots,\alpha,y)\Big)\cdot y.\end{split}

The last line vanishes by theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariance of the counts.

Therefore, the difference we are considering is a Morse coboundary.
∎

## Remark A.5.

We would like to point out that the definition ofQ​S​tpcellQSt_{p}^{\rm cell}as recalled above differs from the sign convention in[41, Equation (2.12)]because the generatorstk​θt^{k}\thetaandtkt^{k}ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}are chosen such that⟨tk​θ,[Δ2​k+1]⟩=⟨tk,[Δ2​k]⟩=(−1)k\langle t^{k}\theta,[\Delta_{2k+1}]\rangle=\langle t^{k},[\Delta_{2k}]\rangle=(-1)^{k}(A.2)

inloc.cit. Our convention agrees with the one in[31]. For our application Corollary1.9, because we work over𝔽2\mathbb{F}_{2}, the two conventions agree.

## Appendix BHomological perturbation arguments

In this appendix we explain how to use homological perturbation arguments in order to prove Proposition3.1and other useful results. For example we show that theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-equivariant Tate bar-length spectrum ofH(p)H^{(p)}coincides with the bar-length spectrum ofHHstretchedpp-times, assuming that(ϕH1)p(\phi^{1}_{H})^{p}has finitely many contractible fixed points. (Note that we work in the class of contractible orbits. In the non-contractible case, a similar result can be proven, see[51].)

## B.1.Crossing energy estimates

## B.1.1.Crossing energy estimate for fixed data

We state two lemmas about crossing energy estimates for both cylinders and pair-of-pants. Both are special cases of[45, Proposition 7.1]after translating to the fixed-point setting, and hence no longer need proofs. For an alternative treatment, see[51, Section 2.3].

## Lemma B.1.

LetH0H_{0}be a Hamiltonian on(M,ω)(M,\omega)with isolated 1-periodic orbits. LetJ0J_{0}be a compatible almost complex structure. Then there existsϵ0>0\epsilon_{0}>0such that all nonconstant Floer cylinders for(H0,J0)(H_{0},J_{0})have energy no less thanϵ0\epsilon_{0}.

To state the case for pair-of-pants, we need to recall the meaning of constant solutions. LetH0H_{0}be a Hamiltonian on(M,ω)(M,\omega)with an isolated set of 1-periodic orbits. For eachpp, recall thatΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}is the sphere punctured at∞\inftyas well as allpp-th roots of unity. Then there is app-fold branched coverπp:Σp∘→ℝ×S1\pi_{p}:\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}. For eachx∈𝒪​(H0)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{0}), letuxp:Σp∘→Mu_{x}^{p}:\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to Mbe the pullback of the mapux:ℝ×S1→M,ux​(s,t)=x​(t).u_{x}:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to M,\ u_{x}(s,t)=x(t).

Then{uxp|x∈𝒪​(H0)}\{u_{x}^{p}\ |\ x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{0})\}is a finite subset ofC0​(Σp∘,M)C^{0}(\Sigma_{p}^{\circ},M). Moreover,uxpu_{x}^{p}is a zero energy solution to the equation(∇σ0u)0,1=0(\nabla^{\sigma_{0}}u)^{0,1}=0

whereσ0\sigma_{0}is the flat Hamiltonian connection onΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}.

## Lemma B.2.

LetH0H_{0}be a Hamiltonian on(M,ω)(M,\omega)such that bothH0H_{0}andH0(p)H_{0}^{(p)}have isolated fixed 1-periodic orbits. LetJ0J_{0}be a compatible almost complex structure. Then there existsϵp>0\epsilon_{p}>0such that for any solutionu:Σp∘→Mu:\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to Mto(∇σ0u)0,1=0(\nabla^{\sigma_{0}}u)^{0,1}=0other thanuxpu_{x}^{p},E​(u)≥ϵpE(u)\geq\epsilon_{p}.

## B.1.2.Crossing energy estimate for perturbed Floer data

Then

Thenuuis contained in the neighborhoodU​(ux)U(u_{x}).

If the above assumption is not true, thenE​(u)≥12​ϵ0E(u)\geq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0}whereϵ0\epsilon_{0}is from LemmaB.1.

## Proof.

The proof is based on a compactness argument and LemmaB.1. Suppose the statement is not true. Then one can find a sequenceσi\sigma_{i}of Hamiltonian connection converging toH0​d​tH_{0}dtand a sequence of domain-dependent almost complex structuresJiJ_{i}converging toJ0J_{0}, both inC∞C^{\infty}-topology, as well as a sequence of solutionsuiu_{i}to the equation(∇σiui)0,1=0(\nabla^{\sigma_{i}}u_{i})^{0,1}=0which do not satisfy the claims. By choosing a subsequence (still indexed byii), one can argue in the following two situations.
- (1)

Suppose there existsx∈𝒪​(H0)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{0})such that for allii,uiu_{i}is asymptotic toxi,±∈𝒪​(Hi,±,x)x_{i,\pm}\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{i,\pm},x). Moreover, for a fixed cappingx¯\overline{x}ofxxwith induced onesx¯i,±\overline{x}_{i,\pm}, the concatenation ofx¯i,−\overline{x}_{i,-}withuiu_{i}coincides withx¯i,+\overline{x}_{i,+}. The condition on the integral of|Fσi||F_{\sigma_{i}}|implies thatE​(ui)→0E(u_{i})\to 0. Then LemmaB.1and the usual compactness argument imply thatuiu_{i}converges uniformly to the constant Floer cylinderuxu_{x}. Hence foriisufficiently large,uiu_{i}is contained in aC0C^{0}-neighborhood ofuxu_{x}.
- (2)

Suppose for alliiwe are not in the above situation for anyx∈𝒪​(H0)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{0}). By contradiction, assume thatE​(ui)≤12​ϵ0E(u_{i})\leq\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{0}. Again one can apply the compactness argument. LemmaB.1implies that any subsequential limit must be a constant Floer cylinderuxu_{x}and the convergence is uniform. This implies that foriisufficiently large, one is indeed in the first situation. ∎

We have the following version for pair-of-pants. Since we do not treat continuation maps, one only needs to consider flat Hamiltonian connections.

## Lemma B.4.

- (2)

If the above assumption is not true, then one hasE​(u)≥12​min⁡{ϵp,ϵ0​(H0(p))}E(u)\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\epsilon_{p},\epsilon_{0}(H_{0}^{(p)})\}

whereϵp\epsilon_{p}is from LemmaB.2andϵ0​(H0(p))\epsilon_{0}(H_{0}^{(p)})is from LemmaB.1while replacingH0H_{0}byH0(p)H_{0}^{(p)}.

## Proof.

We prove by contradiction. For simplicity assumeJi=J0J_{i}=J_{0}; the general case can be treated with only notational complexity. LetGiG_{i}be a sequence of nondegenerate Hamiltonians converging toH0H_{0}inC∞C^{\infty}-topology. Letui:Σp∘→Mu_{i}:\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}\to Mbe a sequence of solutions to the Floer equation(∇σiu)0,1=0(\nabla^{\sigma_{i}}u)^{0,1}=0forσi\sigma_{i}being the Hamiltonian connection induced fromGiG_{i}. We assume that we are in the situation of either (1) or (2) for allii.

Suppose we are in situation (1). By choosing a subsequence, we may assume that for the samex∈𝒪​(H0)x\in{\mathcal{O}}(H_{0}),uiu_{i}is asymptotic to an orbityi,j∈𝒪​(Gi,x)y_{i,j}\in{\mathcal{O}}(G_{i},x)for thejj-th negative puncture and asymptotic toyi,∞∈𝒪​(Gi(p),x(p))y_{i,\infty}\in{\mathcal{O}}(G_{i}^{(p)},x^{(p)})for the positive puncture. Then the condition together with the energy identity for solutions implies thatE​(ui)→0E(u_{i})\to 0. The Gromov compactness implies that a subsequence ofuiu_{i}(still indexed byii) converges to a solution to(∇σ0u)0,1=0(\nabla^{\sigma_{0}}u)^{0,1}=0onΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}, up to breakings at cylindrical ends. By LemmaB.2, the limit is one ofuxpu_{x}^{p}. LemmaB.1(applied to bothH0H_{0}andH0(p)H_{0}^{(p)}) also excludes breakings. Hence the convergenceui→uxpu_{i}\to u_{x}^{p}is uniform, implying that foriisufficiently large,ui∈U​(uxp)u_{i}\in U(u_{x}^{p}).

Suppose we are in situation (2) for the sequence. If the conclusion is false, then for a subsequence ofuiu_{i}(still indexed byii) one hasE​(ui)≤12​min⁡{ϵp,ϵ0​(H0(p))}.E(u_{i})\leq\frac{1}{2}\min\{\epsilon_{p},\ \epsilon_{0}(H_{0}^{(p)})\}.

Then by the same compactness argument, a subsequence ofuiu_{i}converges up to breaking to auxpu_{x}^{p}onΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}. LemmaB.1and the energy bound exclude breakings at both positive and negative ends. The convergence is uniform. Hence foriibeing sufficiently large one is indeed in situation (1), which is a contradiction.
∎

## Lemma B.5.

There existsϵH>0\epsilon_{H}>0such that foriisufficiently large, there is a decompositiondHi=diloc+Di,d_{H_{i}}=d_{i}^{\rm loc}+D_{i},(B.1)

We proceed to the construction ofdHd_{H}in Proposition3.1. It relies on the homological perturbation lemma. To start, we need a basic algebraic fact.

## Lemma B.6.

LetRRbe a principal ideal domain and let(V,d)(V,d)be aℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-graded cochain complex of freeRR-modules such thatH​(V)H(V)is free. Then there exist cochain mapsσV:H​(V)→V,\displaystyle\ \sigma_{V}:H(V)\to V,\πV:V→H​(V)\displaystyle\ \pi_{V}:V\to H(V)

and a chain homotopyΘV:V→V\Theta_{V}:V\to V

such thatπV∘σV=IdH​(V),σV∘πV=IdV+d∘ΘV+ΘV∘d,ΘV2=0,ΘV∘σV=0,πV∘ΘV=0.\begin{split}\pi_{V}\circ\sigma_{V}=&\ {\rm Id}_{H(V)},\\
\sigma_{V}\circ\pi_{V}=&\ {\rm Id}_{V}+d\circ\Theta_{V}+\Theta_{V}\circ d,\\
\Theta_{V}^{2}=&\ 0,\\
\Theta_{V}\circ\sigma_{V}=&\ 0,\\
\pi_{V}\circ\Theta_{V}=&\ 0.\end{split}

## Proof.

For each degreenn, letZn⊂VnZ^{n}\subset V^{n}be the kernel of differential andBn=d​(Vn−1)B^{n}=d(V^{n-1}). AsH​(V)H(V)is free, the exact sequence0\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Bn\textstyle{B^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Zn\textstyle{Z^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}Hn\textstyle{H^{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}0\textstyle{0}

splits. Choose a splittingVn=Zn⊕Bn+1=Bn⊕Hn⊕Bn+1.V^{n}=Z^{n}\oplus B^{n+1}=B^{n}\oplus H^{n}\oplus B^{n+1}.

Then defineσV\sigma_{V}to be the inclusion ofHnH^{n}with respect to this splitting andπV\pi_{V}to be the corresponding projection. Then defineΘV\Theta_{V}to be the identification ofBn⊂VnB^{n}\subset V^{n}withBn⊂Vn−1B^{n}\subset V^{n-1}, extended by0onHn⊕Bn+1H^{n}\oplus B^{n+1}. It is then easy to see that all the conditions are satisfied.
∎

Now change coefficients to𝔽p{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}. By LemmaB.6, one can choose a collection of chain homotopy equivalencesσi,x¯:H​Floc​(H,x¯)→C​Floc​(Hi,x¯)\sigma_{i,\overline{x}}:HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{x})\to CF^{\rm loc}(H_{i},\overline{x})

which is compatible with Novikov actions. Namely, for eachA∈ΓA\in\Gamma, the diagramH​Floc​(Hi,x¯)\textstyle{HF^{\rm loc}(H_{i},\overline{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}σi,x¯\scriptstyle{\sigma_{i,\overline{x}}}H​Floc​(Hi,A⋅x¯)\textstyle{HF^{\rm loc}(H_{i},A\cdot\overline{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}σi,A⋅x¯\scriptstyle{\sigma_{i,A\cdot\overline{x}}}C​Floc​(Hi,x¯)\textstyle{CF^{\rm loc}(H_{i},\overline{x})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}C​Floc​(Hi,A⋅x¯)\textstyle{CF^{\rm loc}(H_{i},A\cdot\overline{x})}

commutes. Here the horizontal arrows are induced by recapping by classAA. Then by taking direct sums, we obtain twoΛ𝔽pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}^{\Gamma}-linear mapsσi:C​(H)→C​F​(Hi),\displaystyle\ \sigma_{i}:C(H)\to CF(H_{i}),\πi:C​F​(Hi)→C​(H),\displaystyle\ \pi_{i}:CF(H_{i})\to C(H),

withΘi:C​F​(Hi)→C​F​(Hi)\Theta_{i}:CF(H_{i})\to CF(H_{i})such that the triple(σi,πi,Θi)(\sigma_{i},\pi_{i},\Theta_{i})satisfies the relations listed in LemmaB.6. ## Appendix CFloer theory for Morse–Bott Hamiltonians and Hamiltonian torsion

Hamiltonian Floer cohomology for degenerate Hamiltonians of Morse–Bott type has been constructed in[40], while a Lagrangian formulation was provided in[38]. The paper[4]discussed certain features of local Floer cohomology of such Hamiltonians while assuming that the symplectic form is rational. In this paper, in order to establish Theorem1.3in full generality, we provide a comprehensive discussion without any topological assumptions.

## Definition C.1.

[40, Definition 1.1]Let(M,ω)(M,\omega)be a closed symplectic manifold. Consider a Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕ∈Ham⁡(M,ω)\phi\in\operatorname{\mathrm{Ham}}(M,\omega)and a connected componentℱ{\mathcal{F}}ofFixc​(ϕ){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi). The connected componentℱ{\mathcal{F}}is said to be ofMorse–Bott type131313Notice that this condition is stronger thangraph​(ϕ){\rm graph}(\phi)intersects cleanly with the diagonal. The latter does not imply the equality between the eigenspace and generalized eigenspace of the eigenvalue11.ifℱ{\mathcal{F}}is a closed submanifold and for eachx∈ℱx\in{\mathcal{F}}Tx​ℱ=Ker​(d​ϕx−Id)=Ker​((d​ϕx−Id)dimℝ​M)⊂Tx​M.T_{x}{\mathcal{F}}={\rm Ker}(d\phi_{x}-{\rm Id})={\rm Ker}\big((d\phi_{x}-{\rm Id})^{{\rm dim}_{\mathbb{R}}M}\big)\subset T_{x}M.

Notice that ifϕ∈Ham​(M,ω)\phi\in{\rm Ham}(M,\omega)has finite order, then any connected componentℱ⊂Fixc​(ϕ){\mathcal{F}}\subset{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)is necessarily of Morse–Bott type.

In this appendix, we would like to do the following.

- (2)

Whenϕ\phiis a finite-order element ofHam​(M,ω){\rm Ham}(M,\omega)whose order is not divisible by a primepp, construct and compute the local equivariant Floer cohomologyH​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ(p),ℱ)HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{{\rm loc}}(\phi^{(p)},{\mathcal{F}}).
- (3)

Under the above setting, construct the local equivariant pair-of-pants product𝐹𝑆𝑡p,ℱloc:H​Floc​(ϕ,ℱ)→H​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ(p),ℱ){\it FSt}_{p,{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:HF^{{\rm loc}}(\phi,{\mathcal{F}})\to HF^{{\rm loc}}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(\phi^{(p)},{\mathcal{F}})

and identify it with the classical Steenrod operation onℱ{\mathcal{F}}.

## C.1.Crossing energy estimates in the Morse–Bott case

We establish a few important estimates which allow us to define corresponding algebraic objects. One first has an exponential decay estimate, which does not hold for general degenerate Hamiltonians.

For simplicity, our estimates are always referring to a fixed compatible almost complex structureJJ. It is not hard to remove the dependence by considering 1-parameter families of almost complex structures. The following statement is standard.

## Lemma C.2.

LetHHbe a Hamiltonian on(M,ω)(M,\omega)generating a diffeomorphismϕ\phiwith Morse–Bott fixed points. Then for eachω\omega-compatible almost complex structureJJ, there existsϵ0​(H,J)>0\epsilon_{0}(H,J)>0andδ0>0\delta_{0}>0satisfying the following conditions. Letu:[0,+∞)×S1→Mu:[0,+\infty)\times S^{1}\to Mbe a finite energy solution to the Floer equation for(H,J)(H,J). SupposeE​(u)≤ϵ0​(H,J)E(u)\leq\epsilon_{0}(H,J), then one haslim sups→+∞eδ0​s​|∂su​(s,t)|<+∞.\limsup_{s\to+\infty}e^{\delta_{0}s}|\partial_{s}u(s,t)|<+\infty.

The exponential decay estimate implies the lower bound of energy of nontrivial solutions.

LetHHandJJbe as above. Then there existsℏ0​(H,J)>0\hbar_{0}(H,J)>0such that for any solutionu:ℝ×S1→Mu:{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}\to Mto the Floer equation for(H,J)(H,J), one hasE​(u)>0⟹E​(u)≥ℏ0​(H,J).E(u)>0\Longrightarrow E(u)\geq\hbar_{0}(H,J).

for anyJJwhich has zero energy.

freely generated by elements in the cluster𝒪~​(G,ℱ¯)\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(G,\overline{\mathcal{F}}). ## Remark C.7.

Alternatively, one can construct the local Floer cohomology via classical perturbation method as done in[4]. We omit the details of such construction and the comparison with the current approach. However, to fit into our framework, especially to prove the corresponding homological perturbation results, we need a version which uses the general framework of[10]and extract the local differential from the total differential.

## C.2.1.Local PSS map

One uses a local PSS map to calculate the local Floer cohomology defined above.

On the other hand, choose a Morse–Smale pair(fℱ,hℱ)(f_{\mathcal{F}},h_{\mathcal{F}})onℱ{\mathcal{F}}with associated Morse complexC​M​(ℱ;ℤ)CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}}). Then for eachy∈crit​fℱy\in{\rm crit}f_{\mathcal{F}}and a generatorx¯∈𝒪~​(H)\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H)ofC​Floc​(H,ℱ¯;ℤ)CF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}}), consider moduli spaces of pairs(y,u)(y,u)where
- (1)

y:(−∞,0]→ℱy:(-\infty,0]\to{\mathcal{F}}is a solution toy′​(s)=∇fℱ​(y​(s))y^{\prime}(s)=\nabla f_{{\mathcal{F}}}(y(s))such thaty​(−∞)=yy(-\infty)=y.

Denote this moduli space byℳy;x¯loc{\mathcal{M}}_{y;\overline{x}}^{\rm loc}.

One can compactify this space by allowing (global) breakings anda prioribubblings. However, by choosingHHsufficiently close toH0H_{0}and choosingσ\sigmaappropriately, elements ofℳy;x¯loc{\mathcal{M}}_{y;\overline{x}}^{\rm loc}have arbitrarily small energy. Hence bubbling does not appear in the compactification. Moreover, CorollaryC.3and LemmaC.5imply that broken trajectories appearing in the compactification can only be the “local” ones ofHH.

From the above features of the compactification ofℳy;x¯loc{\mathcal{M}}_{y;\overline{x}}^{\rm loc}, one can use the classical method to achieve transversality, for example, by perturbingJJ. Such perturbations must be compatible with the one used to define the local Floer cohomology via classical perturbation method (see RemarkC.7). We omit the details.

There is an additional necessity of discussing orientations. In general, the linearized operator in the normal direction ofℱ{\mathcal{F}}induces a possibly nontrivial local system, denoted by𝔬{\mathfrak{o}}. To obtain an isomorphism overℤ{\mathbb{Z}}, one needs to replaceC​M​(ℱ;ℤ)CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}})by the twisted Morse complexC​M​(ℱ;𝔬)CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{o}}); whenϕ\phihas finite order,𝔬{\mathfrak{o}}is trivial (see the related discussion in[4]). Then, by counting solutions, one defines a chain map𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc:C​M​(ℱ;𝔬)→C​Floc​(H,ℱ¯;ℤ).{\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}:CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{o}})\to CF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}}).

One can similarly define the local SSP map𝑆𝑆𝑃ℱloc:C​Floc​(H,ℱ¯;ℤ)→C​M​(ℱ;𝔬).{\it SSP}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}:CF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}})\to CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{o}}).

## Remark C.8.

The local PSS and SSP maps can be extracted in a different way which fits in the general framework of[10]. Indeed, one can define a “pearly Floer cohomology” using the Morse–Bott Hamiltonian and Morse complexes on critical submanifolds. The construction forH≡0H\equiv 0is included in[10]. Then a global PSS/SSP map can be defined, while the local one can be extracted by considering the low-energy part.

## Proposition C.9.

The local PSS and local SSP maps are chain homotopy inverses to each other. As a consequence, one has a canonical isomorphismH​(ℱ;𝔬)≅H​Floc​(ϕ,ℱ¯;ℤ).H({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{o}})\cong HF^{\rm loc}(\phi,\overline{\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{Z}}).

Whenϕ\phihas finite order, the local system𝔬{\mathfrak{o}}is trivial.

## Proof.

We prove that the two-way compositions of𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc{\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}and𝑆𝑆𝑃ℱloc{\it SSP}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}are both identities up to homotopy. The composition𝑆𝑆𝑃ℱloc∘𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc{\it SSP}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}\circ{\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}is about gluing at periodic orbits of the nondegenerateHH. The smallness of the energy together with the crossing energy lemma for the cylinder (LemmaC.5) implies that this composition is homotopic to the map onC​M​(ℱ;𝔬)CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathfrak{o}})induced by a Morse continuation map. For the composition𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc∘𝑆𝑆𝑃ℱloc{\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}\circ{\it SSP}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}, the argument contains gluing at the Morse–Bott periodic orbits ofH0H_{0}. This can also be done by the standard method. The triviality of𝔬{\mathfrak{o}}was established in[4, Section 5.2].
∎

## C.3.Local equivariant pair-of-pants product

## C.3.1.Local equivariant Floer cohomology

Letϕ∈Ham​(M,ω)\phi\in{\rm Ham}(M,\omega)have finite order which is not divisible by a primepp. Then there is a bijection between the set of connected components ofFixc​(ϕ){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)and the set of connected components ofFixc​(ϕp){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p}). We viewℱ⊂Fixc​(ϕ){\mathcal{F}}\subset{\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi)as a component ofFixc​(ϕp){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi^{p}). We would like to define and identify the local equivariant cohomologyH​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ(p),ℱ).HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{{\rm loc}}(\phi^{(p)},{\mathcal{F}}).

LetH0H_{0}be a 1-periodic Hamiltonian generatingϕ\phi. Then for any generic small perturbationHH, the iterationH(p)H^{(p)}is still nondegenerate. Choose a cappingℱ¯\overline{\mathcal{F}}ofℱ{\mathcal{F}}, which induces a cappingℱ¯(p)\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}ofℱ(p){\mathcal{F}}^{(p)}. Then there is a well-defined subset𝒪~(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)⊂𝒪~(H(p))\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\subset\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(H^{(p)})

which generates a cochain groupCFloc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)CF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}). Consider theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}/p-invariant part of the tensor productCFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p):=(CFloc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)⊗CM(g))InvCF^{\rm loc}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}):=\big(CF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\otimes CM(g)\big)^{{\rm Inv}}

whereg:S∞→ℝg:S^{\infty}\to{\mathbb{R}}is the Morse function we have been using for the Morse-theoretic Borel construction. Then there is aℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action on this cochain group. We can then define an equivariant differentialdℤ/ploc:CFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)→CFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)d_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{\rm loc}:CF^{\rm loc}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\to CF^{\rm loc}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})

following the same approach as the isolated case. Notice that one can still use the same almost complex structureJ0J_{0}, hence the previous crossing energy estimate still applies here. The resulting cohomology, denoted byHFℤ/ploc(H0(p),ℱ¯)(p)HF^{\rm loc}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H_{0}^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})

can be proved to be well-defined and independent of the perturbationHH. By forgetting theℤ{\mathbb{Z}}-grading, this cohomology only depends onϕ\phiandℱ{\mathcal{F}}, hence denoted byH​Fℤ/ploc​(ϕ(p),ℱ).HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(\phi^{(p)},{\mathcal{F}}).

## Remark C.10.

Analogous to RemarkC.7, one can define the local Floer cohomology by using domain-dependent perturbations while utilizing the freeℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-action on the moduli spaces (see[4]). Such a construction provides homotopy equivalent complexes and isomorphic equivariant local Floer cohomology.

Now we prove the structural result on the local equivariant Floer cohomology. To be able to compute the local equivariant pants product, one uses the PSS map approach.

## Proposition C.11.

Letϕ\phibe a finite-order Hamiltonian diffeomorphism on(M,ω)(M,\omega)generated by a HamiltonianHHandppbe a prime which does not divide the order ofϕ\phi. Letℱ{\mathcal{F}}be a connected component ofFixc​(ϕ){\rm Fix}_{c}(\phi). Then up to a global degree shift there is an isomorphism ofℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-modulesHFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)≅H(ℱ;𝔽p)⊗ℛp.HF_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}^{{\rm loc}}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\cong H({\mathcal{F}};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}.

## Proof.

The proof is similar to that of PropositionC.9. Let(fℱ,hℱ)(f_{\mathcal{F}},h_{\mathcal{F}})be a Morse–Smale pair onℱ{\mathcal{F}}which induces a Morse complexC​M​(ℱ;𝔽p)CM({\mathcal{F}};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}}). LetGGbe aC∞C^{\infty}-close nondegenerate perturbation ofHHsuch thatG(p)G^{(p)}is also nondegenerate. Letσ\sigmabe a Hamiltonian connection onℝ×S1{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}interpolating fromHHtoGG, which induces a Hamiltonian connectionσ(p)\sigma^{(p)}interpolating fromH(p)H^{(p)}toG(p)G^{(p)}. Then for eachy∈crit​(fℱ)⊂ℱy\in{\rm crit}(f_{\mathcal{F}})\subset{\mathcal{F}}andx¯∈𝒪~​(G(p))\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(G^{(p)}), one can consider a similar moduli space as the one used in the proof of PropositionC.9, withσ\sigmareplaced byσ(p)\sigma^{(p)}.

Moreover, to count solutions equivariantly, couple the equation by parametrized gradient flow lines ofgginS∞S^{\infty}. Then forw−,w+∈crit​(g)w_{-},w_{+}\in{\rm crit}(g)andy∈crit​(fℱ)y\in{\rm crit}(f_{\mathcal{F}}),x¯∈𝒪~(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)\overline{x}\in\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}), one has a moduli spaceℳw−,w+​(y,x¯){\mathcal{M}}_{w_{-},w_{+}}(y,\overline{x})

of pairs. Asσ(p)\sigma^{(p)}has small curvature, the usual energy identity together with LemmaC.5implies that the moduli space can be compactified by only adding local broken configurations without sphere bubbles. Still, one does not have automaticℤ≥0×ℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}\times{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant transversality. One could, however, achieve transversality by virtual perturbations which respect the symmetry. As there are no sphere bubbles, one can use single-valued perturbations and obtain integral counts. Then one obtains aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant cochain mapCM(ℱ;𝔽p)⊗CM(g)→CFloc(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)⊗CM(g).CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathbb{F}}_{p})\otimes CM(g)\to CF^{{\rm loc}}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\otimes CM(g).

Its restriction to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part isCM(ℱ;ℛp)→CFℤ/ploc(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)CM({\mathcal{F}};{\mathcal{R}_{p}})\to CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})

and hence induces the PSS map𝑃𝑆𝑆p,ℱloc:H(ℱ;ℛp)→HFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p).{\it PSS}_{p,{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:H({\mathcal{F}};{\mathcal{R}_{p}})\to HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}).

The standard method proves that this map isℛp{\mathcal{R}_{p}}-linear.

One can also define a similar SSP map𝑆𝑆𝑃p,ℱloc:HFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)→H(ℱ;ℛp).{\it SSP}_{p,{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\to H({\mathcal{F}};{\mathcal{R}_{p}}).

To prove that the PSS and SSP maps have composition in either direction being invertible, one considers the two different gluings. As it is true for the non-equivariant versions, the equivariant version only differs by terms with positive(t,θ)(t,\theta)-degrees. Therefore, the claimed isomorphism can be induced by𝑃𝑆𝑆p,ℱloc{\it PSS}_{p,{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}.
∎

## C.3.2.Local equivariant pair-of-pants product

Previously we have constructed the local Floer cochain complexC​Floc​(G,ℱ¯)CF^{\rm loc}(G,\overline{\mathcal{F}})for any sufficiently close nondegenerate perturbationGGof the Morse–Bott HamiltonianHH. Letppbe a prime and supposeℱ(p){\mathcal{F}}^{(p)}is still a Morse–Bott fixed submanifold ofϕp\phi^{p}. We may chooseGGsuch thatG(p)G^{(p)}is still nondegenerate and can be used to define the local Floer cochain complexCFloc(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)CF^{\rm loc}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}). Then one can consider the flat Hamiltonian connection on the surfaceΣp∘\Sigma_{p}^{\circ}which is the pullback ofG​d​tGdtfrom the cylinder to define the equivariant pair-of-pants product, which firstly provides aℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant cochain map𝐹𝑆𝑡~~p:C​F​(G)⊗p​⊗^​C​M​(g)→C​F​(G(p))​⊗^​C​M​(g).\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}_{p}:CF(G)^{\otimes p}\widehat{\otimes}CM(g)\to CF(G^{(p)})\widehat{\otimes}CM(g).

Now by the crossing energy estimate for pair-of-pants (LemmaC.6), whenGGis sufficiently close toHH, one has a decomposition𝐹𝑆𝑡~~p=𝐹𝑆𝑡~~+ploc𝐹𝑆𝑡~~pbig\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}_{p}=\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}{}_{p}^{\rm loc}+\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}{}_{p}^{\rm big}

corresponding to those “local” pair-of-pants and “big” energy pair-of-pants. One can see that the local one restricts to maps𝐹𝑆𝑡~~:p,ℱ¯locCFloc(G,ℱ¯)⊗p⊗CM(g)→CFloc(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)⊗CM(g).\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}{}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:CF^{\rm loc}(G,\overline{\mathcal{F}})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\to CF^{\rm loc}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\otimes CM(g).

Using the crossing energy estimates again, one can see that this restriction is a cochain map with respect to the local differentials. Moreover, it is stillℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-equivariant. Hence one obtains a cochain map𝐹𝑆𝑡~:p,ℱ¯locCFloc(G,ℱ¯)ℤ/p⊗p→CFℤ/ploc(G(p),ℱ¯)(p).\widetilde{{\it FSt}}{}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:CF^{\rm loc}(G,\overline{\mathcal{F}})^{\otimes p}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}\to CF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}).

The induced map on cohomology, composed with the quasi-Frobenius map, gives a map𝐹𝑆𝑡p,ℱ¯loc:HFloc(H,ℱ¯)→HFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p).{\it FSt}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}:HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}})\to HF^{{\rm loc}}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)}).

We call this map thelocal Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation.

## Remark C.12.

Again, ifH​Floc​(H,ℱ¯)HF^{\rm loc}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}})andHFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})are defined via classical perturbation method, then one can define the product𝐹𝑆𝑡~p,ℱ¯loc\widetilde{{\it FSt}}{}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}by extending such perturbations. The crossing energy estimate for pair-of-pants still plays a role in such a construction.

Lastly we prove that the local Floer-theoretic Steenrod operation agrees with the classical Steenrod operation on the componentℱ{\mathcal{F}}.

## Proposition C.13.

The following diagram commutes.H​Floc​(H,ℱ¯)\textstyle{HF^{{\rm loc}}(H,\overline{\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝐹𝑆𝑡p,ℱ¯loc\scriptstyle{{\it FSt}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}}HFℤ/ploc(H(p),ℱ¯)(p)\textstyle{HF^{{\rm loc}}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝑆𝑆𝑃ℤ/p,ℱ¯loc\scriptstyle{{\it SSP}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}}H​(ℱ)\textstyle{H({\mathcal{F}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}S​tp\scriptstyle{St_{p}}𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc\scriptstyle{{\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc}}H​(ℱ;𝔽p)⊗ℛp\textstyle{H({\mathcal{F}};{{\mathbb{F}}_{p}})\otimes{\mathcal{R}_{p}}}

## Sketch of proof.

The proof is based on the crossing energy estimate and gluing (at nondegenerate Hamiltonian orbits). LetGGbe a nondegenerate perturbation ofHH. On the cochain level, one needs to verify the commutativity ofC​F​(G,ℱ¯)⊗p⊗C​M​(g)\textstyle{CF(G,\overline{\mathcal{F}})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝐹𝑆𝑡~~p,ℱ¯loc\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}{}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}}CF(G(p),ℱ¯)(p)⊗CM(g)\textstyle{CF(G^{(p)},\overline{\mathcal{F}}{}^{(p)})\otimes CM(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}𝑆𝑆𝑃~~ℤ/p,ℱloc\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it SSP}}}{}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}}C​M​(ℱ)⊗p⊗C​M​(g)\textstyle{CM({\mathcal{F}})^{\otimes p}\otimes CM(g)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}(𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc)⊗p⊗Id\scriptstyle{({\it PSS}_{{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc})^{\otimes p}\otimes{\rm Id}}C​M​(ℱ)⊗C​M​(g).\textstyle{CM({\mathcal{F}})\otimes CM(g).}(C.1)

Here the unlabelled arrow, which is supposed to induce the classical Steenrod operation onℱ{\mathcal{F}}, has not been explicitly defined yet.

One uses a typical TQFT+gluing argument to prove the commutativity. The triple composition𝑆𝑆𝑃~~∘ℤ/p,ℱloc𝐹𝑆𝑡~~p,ℱ¯loc∘((𝑃𝑆𝑆ℱloc)⊗p⊗Id)\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it SSP}}}{}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p},{\mathcal{F}}}^{\rm loc}\circ\widetilde{\widetilde{{\it FSt}}}^{{\rm loc}}_{p,\overline{\mathcal{F}}}\circ(({\it PSS}_{\mathcal{F}}^{\rm loc})^{\otimes p}\otimes{\rm Id})

can be identified, via gluings atppnegative ends resp. one positive end and at (nondegenerate) orbits ofGGresp.G(p)G^{(p)}as well as critical points ofgg, to the map defined via the count of configurations where one hasppincoming gradient rays and one outgoing gradient ray onℱ{\mathcal{F}}, between which is a solution to the Floer equation onΣ\Sigmabeingℂ{\mathbb{C}}removingpp-th roots of unity with flat Hamiltonian connection equal to the pullback ofH​d​tHdtfromℝ×S1{\mathbb{R}}\times S^{1}. The crossing energy estimate can guarantee the gluing happens “locally” and the Floer equation onΣ\Sigmamust be a constant one. Therefore, the configurations one finally obtains are those Morse gradient trees responsible for the classical Steenrod operation onℱ{\mathcal{F}}. The original commutative diagram is then obtained by 1) restricting (C.1) to theℤ/p{\mathbb{Z}/p}-invariant part, 2) reducing to cohomology, and 3) composing with the quasi-Frobenius maps.
∎

Choose a nondegenerate HamiltonianGGsatisfying the assumption of LemmaC.2. Then the chain complexC​F​(G;Λ𝕂Γ)CF(G;\Lambda_{\mathbb{K}}^{\Gamma})can be defined with a differentialdGd_{G}, which has a decompositiondG=dGloc+dGbigd_{G}=d_{G}^{\rm loc}+d_{G}^{\rm big}

into “local” partdGlocd_{G}^{\rm loc}and the big-energy partdGbigd_{G}^{\rm big}. Then the homological perturbation argument provides a construction of the desired complexC​(H)C(H). The construction of the differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}is very similar to the isolated case given in the proof of Proposition3.3. The component-wise decomposition ofCℤ/p​(H(p))C_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)})follows from PropositionC.11. LemmaB.6allows us to construct the chain homotopy equivalence between the local equivariant Floer complex and the local equivariant Floer cohomology, where the coefficient ring is𝒦0,p{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}. Then the first half of this proposition, i.e., the canonical isomorphismH​(Cℤ/p​(H(p)))≅H​Fℤ/p​(M)H(C_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)}))\cong HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M)and the claim thatdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}strictly increases the action by a positive constant, follows from the same argument as the isolated case.

∎

One can also use the Tate version of the above construction to obtain an analogue of Corollary3.5. AsC​(H)C(H)is a Floer-type complex, one has a well-defined bar-length spectrum0<β1​(H)≤⋯≤βN​(H).0<\beta_{1}(H)\leq\cdots\leq\beta_{N}(H).

One also considers the Tate version of the homologically perturbed equivariant Floer complex. DefineC^ℤ/p​(H(p)):=Cℤ/p​(H(p))​⊗Λ𝒦0,pΓ​Λ𝒦pΓ\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)}):=C_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(H^{(p)})\underset{\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}}{\otimes}\Lambda_{\mathcal{K}_{p}}^{\Gamma}

which is a Floer-type complex over the Novikov fieldΛ𝒦pΓ\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\Gamma}. Hence it has the Tate bar-length spectrum0<β^1​(H(p))≤⋯≤β^N(p)​(H(p)).0<\widehat{\beta}_{1}(H^{(p)})\leq\cdots\leq\widehat{\beta}_{N^{(p)}}(H^{(p)}).

## Corollary C.16.

Under the assumption of PropositionC.15, forppbeing an odd prime, one hasN(p)=2​NN^{(p)}=2Nand for eachi=1,…,Ni=1,\ldots,N, there holdsβ^2​i−1​(ϕp)=β^2​i​(ϕp)=p​βi​(ϕ).\widehat{\beta}_{2i-1}(\phi^{p})=\widehat{\beta}_{2i}(\phi^{p})=p\beta_{i}(\phi).

## Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of Corollary3.5. One can change the coefficient to the universal Novikov rings/fields. Then the equivariant pair-of-pants product induces an isomorphism ofΛ0,𝒦puniv\Lambda_{0,{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\rm univ}-modulesP^0:H​(C​(H;Λ0univ)ℤ/p⊗p^)→H​(C^ℤ/p​(H(p);Λ0,𝒦puniv))\widehat{P}_{0}:H(\widehat{C(H;\Lambda_{0}^{\rm univ})^{\otimes p}_{\mathbb{Z}/p}})\to H(\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p)};\Lambda_{0,{\mathcal{K}_{p}}}^{\rm univ}))

where the bar-lengths under consideration label the torsion parts.
∎

## Proposition C.17.

LetHHbe a 1-periodic Hamiltonian whose time one mapϕ∈Ham​(M,ω)\phi\in{\rm Ham}(M,\omega)generates a nontrivialℤ/p′{\mathbb{Z}}/p^{\prime}-action onMMfor a certain primep′p^{\prime}. Then for any odd primep≠p′p\neq p^{\prime}, the following are true.
- (1)

For anyk≥0k\geq 0, the differentialdH(pk)d_{H^{(p^{k})}}of the complexC​(H(pk))C(H^{(p^{k})})given by PropositionC.14vanishes. HenceH​F​(M;ΛΓ)≅⨁ℱ∈π0​(𝒪​(H))H​Floc​(H(pk),ℱ(pk);ΛΓ).HF(M;\Lambda^{\Gamma})\cong\bigoplus_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(H))}HF^{\rm loc}(H^{(p^{k})},{\mathcal{F}}^{(p^{k})};\Lambda^{\Gamma}).
- (2)

For anyk≥1k\geq 1, the differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}of the complexCℤ/p​(H(pk))C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p^{k})})given by PropositionC.15vanishes. HenceH​Fℤ/p​(M;Λ𝒦0,pΓ)≅⨁ℱ∈π0​(𝒪​(H))H​Fℤ/ploc​(H(pk),ℱ(pk);Λ𝒦0,pΓ).HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}(M;\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma})\cong\bigoplus_{{\mathcal{F}}\in\pi_{0}({\mathcal{O}}(H))}HF_{\mathbb{Z}/p}^{\rm loc}(H^{(p^{k})},{\mathcal{F}}^{(p^{k})};\Lambda_{{\mathcal{K}_{0,p}}}^{\Gamma}).

## Proof.

We follow the argument of[4, Section 6.3]. First, becauseϕ\phihas orderp′p^{\prime}, there holdsmax1≤j≤p′−1⁡βtot​(ϕj)≥βtot​(ϕi⋅pk).\max_{1\leq j\leq p^{\prime}-1}\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}(\phi^{j})\geq\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}(\phi^{i\cdot p^{k}}).

Now we make use of the quantitative Smith inequality (Theorem2.20), which is established for nondegenerate Hamiltonians. As the barcodes vary continuously with the Hamiltonian diffeomorphism, together with (2) of PropositionC.13, for the degenerate Hamiltonian diffeomorphismϕi\phi^{i}, one hasβtot​(ϕi⋅pk)≥pk​βtot​(ϕi).\beta_{\rm tot}(\phi^{i\cdot p^{k}})\geq p^{k}\beta_{\rm tot}(\phi^{i}).

Askkis arbitrary, we see thatβtot​(ϕi)=0\beta_{\mathrm{tot}}(\phi^{i})=0for alli∈{1,…,p′}i\in\{1,\ldots,p^{\prime}\}. In particular, the cochain complexC​(H(pk))C(H^{(p^{k})})does not admit any nontrivial differential.

For the complexCℤ/p​(H(pk))C_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p^{k})}), suppose on the contrary it has a nontrivial differentialdℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}. Then the corresponding Tate complexC^ℤ/p​(H(pk))\widehat{C}_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}(H^{(p^{k})})also admits a nontrivial differential. As shown in PropositionC.15,dℤ/pd_{{\mathbb{Z}/p}}strictly increases the filtration. Then the Tate bar-length spectrum has a nonzero shortest bar-lengthβ^1​(H(pk))\widehat{\beta}_{1}(H^{(p^{k})}). It follows from CorollaryC.16thatβ^1​(H(pk))=p​β1​(H(pk−1))≠0\widehat{\beta}_{1}(H^{(p^{k})})=p\beta_{1}(H^{(p^{k-1})})\neq 0, which contradicts the first assertion we just proved.
∎
